Random Algebraic Graphs and Their Convergence to Erdős-Rényi
Kiril Bangachev Guy Bresler

Abstract
We introduce a new probabilistic model of latent space graphs, which we call random algebraic graphs . A (right) random algebraic graph is defined by a group 𝒢 \mathcal{\mathcal{G}} with a well-defined “uniform” distribution 𝒟 \mathcal{D} over it and a measurable function σ : 𝒢 ⟶ [ 0 , 1 ] \sigma:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow[0,1] with expectation p p over 𝒟 , {\mathcal{D}}, satisfying σ ⁡ ( 𝐠 ) = σ ⁡ ( 𝐠 − 1 ) , \sigma(\mathbf{g})=\sigma(\mathbf{g}^{-1}), which we call a “connection”. The random graph 𝖱𝖠𝖦 ⁡ ( n , 𝒢 , p , σ ) \mathsf{RAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma) with vertex set [ n ] [n] is formed as follows. First, n n independent latent vectors 𝐱 1 , 𝐱 2 , … , 𝐱 n \mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n} are sampled according to 𝒟 . {\mathcal{D}}. Then, every two vertices i , j i,j are connected with probability σ ⁡ ( 𝐱 i ​ 𝐱 j − 1 ) . \sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}). The random algebraic model captures random geometric graphs with latent space the unit sphere and the hypercube, certain regimes of the stochastic block model, and random subgraphs of Cayley graphs. The main question of interest to the current paper is: for what parameters is a random algebraic graph 𝖱𝖠𝖦 ⁡ ( n , 𝒢 , p , σ ) \mathsf{RAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma) statistically and/or computationally distinguishable from an Erdős-Rényi random graph 𝖦 ⁡ ( n , p ) \mathsf{G}(n,p) ? Our results fall into two main categories. 1. Geometric: We mostly focus on the hypercube case 𝒢 = { ± 1 } d , \mathcal{G}=\{\pm 1\}^{d}, where we use Fourier-analytic tools. We expect that some of the insights will also transfer to the sphere. For hard thresholds, we match [ LMS+22a ] for p = ω ⁡ ( 1 / n ) p=\omega(1/n) and for connections that are 1 r ​ d \frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}} -Lipschitz we extend the results of [ LR21a ] when d = Ω ⁡ ( n ​ log ⁡ n ) d=\Omega(n\log n) to the non-monotone setting. We also study other connections such as indicators of interval unions and low-degree polynomials. One novel phenomenon is that when σ \sigma is even, that is σ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) = σ ⁡ ( − 𝐱 ) , \sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(-\mathbf{x}), indistinguishability from 𝖦 ⁡ ( n , p ) \mathsf{G}(n,p) occurs at d = Θ ⁡ ( n 3 / 2 ) d=\Theta(n^{3/2}) instead of d = Θ ⁡ ( n 3 ) , d=\Theta(n^{3}), the latter condition (up to problem-dependent hidden parameters) appearing in most previous results. 2. Algebraic: We provide evidence for the following exponential statistical-computational gap. Consider any finite group 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and let A ⊆ 𝒢 A\subseteq\mathcal{G} be a set of elements formed by including each set of the form { 𝐠 , 𝐠 − 1 } \{\mathbf{g},\mathbf{g}^{-1}\} independently with probability p = 1 / 2 . p=1/2. Let Γ n ​ ( 𝒢 , A ) \Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A) be the distribution of random graphs formed by taking a uniformly random induced subgraph of size n n of the Cayley graph Γ ⁡ ( 𝒢 , A ) . \Gamma(\mathcal{G},A). Then, Γ n ​ ( 𝒢 , A ) \Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A) and 𝖦 ⁡ ( n , 1 / 2 ) \mathsf{G}(n,1/2) are statistically indistinguishable with high probability over A A if and only if log ⁡ | 𝒢 | ≳ n . \log|\mathcal{G}|\gtrsim n. However, low-degree polynomial tests fail to distinguish Γ n ​ ( 𝒢 , A ) \Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A) and 𝖦 ⁡ ( n , 1 / 2 ) \mathsf{G}(n,1/2) with high probability over A A when log ⁡ | 𝒢 | = log Ω ⁡ ( 1 ) ⁡ n . \log|\mathcal{G}|=\log^{\Omega(1)}n. En-route, we also obtain two novel, to the best of our knowledge, probabilistic results that might be of independent interest. First, we give a nearly sharp bound on the moments of elementary symmetric polynomials of independent Rademacher variables in certain regimes ( Theorem 1.7 ). Second, we show that the difference of two independent n × n n\times n Wishart matrices with parameter d d converges to an appropriately scaled n × n n\times n GOE matrix in KL when d = ω ⁡ ( n 2 ) . d=\omega(n^{2}). This is polynomially smaller than the d = ω ⁡ ( n 3 ) d=\omega(n^{3}) required for the convergence of a single n × n n\times n Wishart matrix with parameter d d to a GOE matrix, occurring at d = ω ⁡ ( n 3 ) d=\omega(n^{3}) [ BDE+14 , JL13 ] .



1
Introduction

Latent space random graphs are a family of random graph models in which edge-formation depends on hidden (latent) vectors associated to nodes. One of many examples of latent space graphs—appearing in
[
SAC19
]
, for example—is that of social networks, which form based on unobserved features (such as age, occupation, geographic location, and others). One reason these models are studied in the literature is that, unlike the more common Erdős-Rényi random graphs, latent space graphs capture and explain real-world phenomena such as “the friend of my friend is also (likely) a friend of mine”
[
SAC19
]
.

A mathematical model that captures this structure is given by a probability distribution
{\mathcal{D}}
over some latent space
\Omega,
an integer
n
, and a
connection
function
\sigma:\Omega\times\Omega\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sigma(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{x})
a.s. with respect to
{\mathcal{D}}.
Denote

p={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\sim_{iid}{\mathcal{D}}}[\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]\in[0,1].

The probabilistic latent space graph
\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma,p)
is defined as follows
[
LR21a
]
.
For an adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}=(\mathbf{A}_{i,j})_{i,j\in[n]},

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}(G=\mathbf{A})={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}\sim_{iid}{\mathcal{D}}}\Bigg[\prod_{1\leq i<j\leq n}\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j})^{\mathbf{A}_{i,j}}(1-\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}))^{1-\mathbf{A}_{i,j}}\Bigg].

(1)

In words, for each node
i\in[n]=\{1,2,\ldots,n\},
an independent latent vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}
is drawn from
\Omega
according to
{\mathcal{D}}
and then for each pair of vertices
i
and
j
an edge is drawn independently with probability
\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}).

Note that if
\sigma
is equal to
p
a.s., this is just the Erdős-Rényi distribution
\mathsf{G}(n,p).
More interesting examples occur when there is richer structure in
(\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma).

The focus of this paper is the case when
\Omega
is a group and
\sigma
is “compatible” with the group structure as in
Definition

1.12
. Before delving into this novel algebraic setting, we provide some context and motivation for our work by describing the setting with a geometric structure, which is widely studied in the literature.

Suppose that
\Omega
is a metric space (most commonly
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the induced
\|\cdot\|_{2}
metric) and
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
depends only on the
distance between
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}.
Such graphs are called
random geometric graphs
and we write
\mathsf{RGG}
instead of
\mathsf{PLSG}.
In practice, random geometric graphs have found applications in
wireless networks
[
Hae12
]
, consensus dynamics
[
ES16
]
, and protein-protein interactions
[
HRP08
]
among others (see
[
Pen03
,
DC22
]
for more applications). On the more theoretical side, random geometric graphs are, by construction, random graph models with correlated edges which provide an interesting and fruitful parallel theory to the better understood Erdős-Rényi model. Khot, Tulsiani, and Worah use random geometric graphs on the sphere to provide approximation lower-bounds for constraint satisfaction problems
[
KTW14
]
, and Liu et al. recently showed that random geometric graphs are high-dimensional expanders in certain regimes
[
LMS+22
]
. Finally, the study of random geometric graphs has catalyzed the development of other areas in probability such as the convergence of Wishart matrices to GOE matrices
[
BG15
,
BDE+14
,
BBH21
]
, which in turn has applications such as average-case reductions between statistical problems
[
BB19
]
.

Starting with
[
DGL+11
,
BDE+14
]
, the high-dimensional setting (in which
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
d
grows with
n
) has gained considerable attention in recent years
[
BBH21
,
LR21a
,
LMS+22a
,
LMS+22
,
EM20
,
EMP22
,
RR19
,
LR21
,
BBN20
,
BBH22
]
. The overarching direction of study in most of these papers and also our current work is based on the following observation, first made in
[
DGL+11
]
. When
d\longrightarrow+\infty
for a fixed
n,
random geometric graphs typically lose their geometric structure and become indistinguishable from Erdős-Rényi graphs of the same expected density. This motivates the following question. How large does the dimension
d
need to be so that edges become independent?

More concretely, we view each of
\Omega,{\mathcal{D}},p,\sigma
as an implicit sequence indexed by
n
and take
n\longrightarrow+\infty.
This gives rise to the following hypothesis testing problem for latent space graphs:

H_{0}:G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)\quad\text{versus}\quad H_{1}:G\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma,p).

(2)

Associated to these hypotheses are (at least) two different questions:

1.

Statistical:

First, when is there a consistent test?
To this end, we aim to characterize the parameter regimes in which the total variation between the two distributions tends to zero or instead to one.

2.

Computational:
Second, we can ask for a computationally efficient test. In particular, when does there exist a polynomial-time test solving
Eq.

2
with high probability?

The literature has been, thus far, predominantly interested in geometric models for which there has been no evidence that the answers to the statistical and computational question are different. In particular, an efficient test (counting signed subgraphs, which we will see in
Section

6
) always matches or nearly matches the statistical threshold. In
Section

7
, we give an example of a model with evidence of an
exponential
gap between the statistical and computational detection thresholds. Evidence for the gap is provided in the increasingly popular framework of low-degree polynomial tests (see
[
SW22
]
, for example).

To evince this gap (as well as numerous other new phenomena depending on the choice of
\sigma,
see
Section

1.2
), we first focus on the case of the Boolean hypercube
1
1

1

Throughout the entire paper, when we say
hypercube
, we mean the Boolean hypercube
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
rather than
[0,1]^{d}.
This distinction is very important as
[0,1]^{d}
with its
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2}
metric is not a homogeneous metric space, which makes its behaviour substantially different from the behaviour of the unit sphere and Boolean hypercube. Specifically, recent work
[
EAG+20
]
suggests that random geometric graphs over
[0,1]^{d}
do not necessarily converge to Erdős-Rényi .
,
\Omega=\{\pm 1\}^{d}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}.
Most previous papers in the high-dimensional setting study the unit sphere or Gauss space. The advantage of the hypercube model is that it possesses a very simple algebraic structure — it is a product of
d
groups of order 2 — in addition to its geometric structure. The underlying group structure facilitates the use of Fourier-analytic tools which lead to our results for much more general connections
\sigma
than previously considered.

Our results for the hypercube motivate us to analyze more general latent space graphs for which
\Omega
is a group. We call such graphs, defined in
Section

1.3
,
random algebraic graphs
. It turns out that a natural condition in this setting is that
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
depend only on
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}
(or, alternatively, on
\mathbf{x}^{-1}\mathbf{y}
). As we will see in
Section

1.3
, random algebraic graphs turn out to be extremely expressive.

Figure 1:
Different probabilistic latent space graph models depending on the underlying structure of
\Omega.
Denoted are the sections in the introduction discussing the respective models.

The hypercube model can be viewed as a bridge between random algebraic graphs and random geometric graphs. Of course, one needs to be careful when interpreting results for the hypercube model since they can have their origin both in the underlying geometry or underlying algebra. In
Section

8
, we discuss the dependence of our results on geometry and algebra.

1.1
Prior Work

1. Hard Thresholds on The Unit Sphere and Gauss Space.

A majority of the papers on high-dimensional random geometric graphs deal with hard thresholds on the unit sphere: the latent space is
\Omega=\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
is the uniform distribution
\mathsf{Unif}
over it. The connection
\sigma
is given by
{\mathbf{T}}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathds{1}[\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle\geq\tau_{p,d}],
where
\tau_{p,d}
is chosen so that
{\mathbf{E}}[{\mathbf{T}}_{p,d}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]=p.
For example,
\tau_{1/2,d}=0.

In
[
DGL+11
]
, the authors initiated this line of work by showing that when
d=\exp(\Omega(n^{2})),
the two graph models are indistinguishable. Bubeck et. al. make an exponential improvement to
d={\omega}(n^{3})
in
[
BDE+14
]
. Implicitly, the authors of
[
BDE+14
]
also showed that the signed-triangle count statistic (which can be computed in polynomial time) distinguishes
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathsf{Unif},p,{\mathbf{T}}_{p})
when
d=\tilde{O}(n^{3}p^{3})
(explicitly, the calculation appears in
[
LMS+22a
]
, for example). It is believed that the the signed triangle statistic gives the tight bound.

Conjecture 1.1

(Implicit in
[
BDE+14
,
BBN20
,
LMS+22a
]
and others)
.

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathsf{Unif},p,{\mathbf{T}}_{p})\Big)=o_{n}(1)

when
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3}p^{3})
for
p=\Omega(\frac{1}{n}).

The current progress towards this conjecture is the following. In the sparse regime, when
p=\Theta(\frac{1}{n}),
Liu et. al. resolve the conjecture in
[
LMS+22a
]
by showing indistinguishability when
d=\Omega(\log^{36}n).
When
p\leq\frac{1}{2}
and
p=\omega(\frac{1}{n}),
the same paper shows that
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathsf{Unif},p,{\mathbf{T}}_{p})\Big)=o(1)
holds when
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3}p^{2}),
missing a factor
p
relative to
1.1
.

Several papers, including
[
BDE+14
]
, also study the related model when
(\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathsf{Unif})
is replaced by
(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})^{\otimes n}).
The two models are nearly equivalent since if
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),
then
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\times\left\lVert\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{-1}_{2}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})
and
\left\lVert\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert^{2}_{2}
is extremely well concentrated around
d.

We will say more about the significance of the Gauss space model when discussing Wishart matrices momentarily.

2. Wishart Matrices.

For
X\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})^{\otimes n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},
denote by
\mathcal{W}(n,d),
the law of the Wishart matrix
XX^{T}.
In
[
BDE+14
]
, the authors prove and use the following result. A GOE matrix
M(n)
is a symmetric
n\times n
matrix with random Gaussian entries, which are independent on the main diagonal and above. The variance of each entry on the main diagonal is
2
and above the diagonal it is 1.

Theorem 1.2

(
[
BDE+14
,
JL13
]
)
.

When
d=\omega(n^{3}),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(n,d),\sqrt{d}M(n)+I_{n}\Big)=o(1).

This result shows that when
d
is sufficiently large,
\mathcal{W}(n,d)
is, in total variation, a Gaussian matrix with i.i.d. entries. In the context of random geometric graphs, this implies that inner products of latent vectors are independent (in total variation) and, thus, the corresponding random geometric graph has independent edges. The result
Theorem

1.2
has been generalized in several ways since. In
[
BG15
]
, the authors obtain similar results when the Gaussian density used in the construction of the Wishart ensemble is replaced with a log-concave density. In
[
RR19
]
, the authors show that the transition between a Wishart ensemble and GOE is “smooth” by calculating
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(n,d),\sqrt{d}M(n)+I_{n}\Big)
when
\lim_{n\longrightarrow+\infty}d/n^{3}=c
for a fixed constant
c.
Finally, in
[
BBH21
]
, the authors consider the convergence of a Wishart matrix with hidden entries to a GOE with hidden entries. They consider both structured and random “masks” hiding the entries. A special case of one of their results for bipartite masks, which we will use later on, is the following. Let
\mathcal{M}
be an
n\times n
matrix split into four
\frac{n}{2}\times\frac{n}{2}
blocks, where the upper-left and lower-right ones contain only zeros and lower-left and upper-right contain only ones. Let
\odot
be the usual Schur (entrywise) product.

Theorem 1.3

(
[
BBH21
]
)
.

When
d={\omega}(n^{2}),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{M}\odot\mathcal{W}(n,d),\mathcal{M}\odot(\sqrt{d}M(n)+I_{n})\Big)=o_{n}(1).

We also mention that Ch’etelat and Wells establish a sequence of phase transitions of the density of
\mathcal{W}
when
n^{K+3}=\omega(d^{K+1})
for all
K\in\mathbb{N}

[
CW17
]
.

3. Soft Thresholds.

A line of work by Liu and Racz
[
LR21
,
LR21a
]
studies a soft threshold model. In it, edges are random rather than deterministic functions of
\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle,
which corresponds to

\sigma
taking other values than just
0
and
1.
In
[
LR21
]
, the connection of interest is given as a convex combination between pure noise

\sigma\equiv p,
and
{\mathbf{T}}_{p},
namely

\phi_{q}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(1-q)p+q{\mathbf{T}}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
for some
q\in[0,1].
In
[
LR21a
]
, on the other hand, the authors consider smooth monotone connections formed as follows. Take
(\Omega,{\mathcal{D}})=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})).
Suppose that
f
is a
C_{2}

\alpha
-Lipschitz density corresponding to a zero-mean distribution over
\mathbb{R}.
Let
F
be the corresponding CDF. Let
\sigma_{f}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=F\big(\frac{\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle-\mu_{p,d,r}}{r\sqrt{d}}\big),
where
r\geq 1
and
\mu_{p,d,r}
is chosen so that
{\mathbf{E}}[\sigma_{f}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]=p.
The fact that
f
is
\alpha
-Lipschitz implies that
\sigma_{f}
is
O_{\alpha}(\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}})
-Lipschitz when viewed as a function of
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
(this follows directly from
[
LR21a
, Lemma 2.6.]
).
The main result is the following.

Theorem 1.4

(
[
LR21a
]
)
.

Let
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),p,\sigma_{f})
be defined as above.

1) If

d=\omega_{p,\alpha}(\frac{n^{3}}{r^{4}}),
then

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),p,\sigma_{f})\Big)=o(1).

2) If
d=o_{p,\alpha}(\frac{n^{3}}{r^{6}})
and, additionally,
d/\log^{2}d=\omega(r^{6})
or
r/\log^{2}(r)=\omega(d^{1/6}),

then

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),p,\sigma_{f})\Big)=1-o(1).

The authors conjecture that the lower bound 2), which is derived via the signed triangle statistic, is tight
[
LR21a
]
. They derive their upper bounds in 1) using the following general claim on latent space random graphs. This claim is the starting point for the indistinguishability results in the current paper.

Claim 1.5

(
[
LR21a
]
)
.

Let
p,q\in[0,1]
be two not-necessarily equal probabilities.
Consider the graph
\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,{\mathcal{D}},\sigma,q)
for some
n,\Omega,{\mathcal{D}},\sigma,q.
Define

\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{z}\sim{\mathcal{D}}}\bigg[(\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{y})-p)\bigg].

(3)

Then,

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,{\mathcal{D}},\sigma,q)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log{\mathbf{E}}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}\bigg[\Big(1+\frac{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\Big)^{k}\bigg].

Typically, we take
p=q.
We state the result in this more general form for the purely technical reason that in
Section

5
we will need it for
q=p(1+\epsilon),
where
\epsilon
is exponentially smaller than
p.

In light of Pinsker’s inequlity (see
Theorem

2.2
), showing that the KL-divergence is of order
o(1)
implies that the total variation is also of order
o(1).

Expanding the expression above, we obtain

{\mathbf{E}}_{(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\sim\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}\Big[\Big(1+\frac{\gamma_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\Big)^{k}\Big]=\sum_{t=0}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma_{p}^{t}]}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}.

(4)

Thus,
1.5
reduces the task of proving indistinguishability bounds to bounding moments of
\gamma_{p}.
In
[
LR21a
]
, the authors achieve this via a wide range of tools in Gaussian analysis. Our approach is quite different and it relies on
interpreting
\gamma_{p}
as an autocorrelation function when
\Omega
has a group-theoretic structure.

Unfortunately, as noted by
[
LR21a
]
, the claim is likely not tight. In
Section

8.1
, we demonstrate new instances in which (and reasons why) it is not tight, which hopefully will shed light on how to improve
1.5
. Improving the claim has the potential to resolve several open problems such as
1.1
and the gap in
Theorem

1.4
.

4. Other Random Geometric Graphs.

Several other random geometric graph models have been studied recently as well. For example,
[
BBH22
,
EM20
]
consider the hard thresholds model for anisotropic Gaussian vectors. Together, the two papers prove a tight condition on the vector of eigenvalues of the covariance matrix under which the total variation is of order
o(1).

1.2
Our Results on The Hypercube

There is a large gap in the literature, with all prior works focusing on connections
\sigma
with the following two properties:

1.

Monotonicity:
Monotonicity of
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle\longrightarrow\sigma(\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle)
is a natural assumption which can be interpreted as closer/more aligned vectors are more likely to form a connection.
Nevertheless, many interesting choices of
\sigma
are not monotone. For example, connections could be formed when vectors are instead weakly correlated, corresponding to the non-monotone
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathds{1}[|\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle|\leq\delta]
for some
\delta.
As we will see in
Section

4.1
, the underlying symmetry around
0,
i.e.,
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sigma(\mathbf{x},-\mathbf{y})
leads to a different indistinguishability rate.

2.

Symmetry:
Connections
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
depending only on
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
are symmetric with respect to permutations of coordinates. However, in some interesting examples different coordinates can influence the connection in qualitatively different ways. For example, suppose that the latent vectors correspond to characteristics of people and edges in the graph to friendships among the set of people. Similarity in some characteristics—such as geographical location—can make people more likely to form a friendship, but others—such as competition for scarce resources—can make them less likely to become friends due to competitiveness.
In
Section

4.2
we use a simple mathematical model of such connections, which again leads to different indistinguishability rates.

Theorem

3.1
in the current paper makes a step towards filling this gap.
It addresses a large family of probabilistic latent space graphs over the hypercube
\Omega=\{\pm 1\}^{d}
with the uniform measure
{\mathcal{D}}=\mathsf{Unif}
for which neither monotonicity nor symmetry holds. We remark that because our proofs are based on Fourier-analytic tools, which have generalizations to products of abelian groups and tori, our results likely hold in greater generality.
In the interest of obtaining simple and interpretable results, we do not pursue this here.

The main insight is that over the hypercube, Fourier-analytic tools facilitate the analysis of connections depending on the coordinate-wise (group-theoretic) product
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\longrightarrow\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}.
This setup is strictly more general than the setting of inner products since
\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y})_{i}=\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle.
When
\sigma
only depends on
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y},
we use the notation
\mathsf{RAG}
(standing for random algebraic graph, see
Definition

1.12
) instead of
\mathsf{PLSG}.

Theorem

3.1
yields indistinguishability rates depending on the largest magnitude of Fourier coefficients on each level (see
Section

2.2
for preliminaries on Boolean Fourier analysis). Informally, we have:

Theorem 1.6

(Informal
Theorem

3.1
)
.

Suppose that
d=\Omega(n),

\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
has expectation
p,
and

m\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant. Let
\sigma=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{\sigma}(S)\omega_{S}
.
For
1\leq i\leq d,
let
B_{i}=\max\big\{|\widehat{\sigma}(S)|\binom{d}{i}^{1/2}:|S|=i\big\}
. Let

C_{m}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{d/2en}B_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=d-\frac{d}{2en}}^{d-m-1}B_{i}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad D=\sum_{\frac{d}{2en}\leq j\leq d-\frac{d}{2en}}B_{i}^{2}.

If
C_{m},B_{u}^{2},B_{d-u}^{2}\leq p(1-p)
for
0\leq u\leq m,
then

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=O\Bigg(\frac{n^{3}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\times\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{d^{i}}+\sum_{i=d-m}^{d}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{d^{i}}+\frac{C^{2}_{m}}{{d}^{m+1}}+{D^{2}}\times\exp\left(-\frac{d}{2en}\right)\right)\Bigg).

Qualitatively, the
{\mathsf{KL}}
divergence is small whenever all Fourier coefficients of
\sigma
are small. In
Remark

6.6
, we show the converse – a single large Fourier coefficient makes the
{\mathsf{KL}}
divergence large.

In the beginning of
Section

3
, we summarize the four main steps in our proof of
Theorem

1.6
before we fill in all the technical details. Here, we highlight the following inequality we derive which is crucial to the proof. Its utility comes into play as we
derive an upper bound of the desired
{\mathsf{KL}}
divergence via the moments of a symmetric function. Symmetric functions over the hypercube are weighted sums of elementary symmetric polynomials.

Theorem 1.7

(
Theorem

3.5
)
.

Let
d\in\mathbb{N}.
Consider the elementary symmetric polynomial
e_{s}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by
e_{s}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]\;:\;|S|=s}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.
If
t\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
\frac{d}{2et}<s<d-\frac{d}{2et},
then

\binom{d}{s}\exp\Big(-\ln 2\times\frac{d}{t}\Big)\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}\big[|e_{s}(\mathbf{x})|^{t}\big]^{1/t}\leq\binom{d}{s}\exp\Big(-\frac{d}{2et}\Big).

We have not tried to optimize the constants in the exponent. The upper bound also holds for other distributions such as uniform on the sphere and Gaussian when
s<{d}/{2}.

We now describe some applications of
Theorem

1.6

to various choices of
\sigma
.
While the claim of the theorem is rather long, it turns out to be relatively simple to apply. We organize our results based on the degree of symmetry in the respective connections. Note that in the symmetric case over
\{\pm 1\}^{d},

\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y})
only depends on

\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}y_{i}=\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
and this is the usual inner-product model.

1.2.1
Symmetric Connections

Most previous work on indistinguishability focuses on monotone connections and proves a necessary condition of the form
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3})
(with hidden constants, depending on
p,
Lipschitzness, etc). We show that even some of the simplest instances of non-monotone, but still symmetric, connections violate this trend.

Theorem 1.8

(Informal,
Corollaries

4.10
,
4.5
and
4.14
)
.

If
\sigma
is symmetric and even (i.e.,
\sigma(\mathbf{g})=\sigma(-\mathbf{g})
), then
{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\big)=o(1),
whenever
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3/2})
.

The reason why this holds is that odd Fourier levels of
\sigma
vanish for even connections (so, in particular
B_{1}=0
in
Theorem

1.6
). We generalize this result with the following conjecture, which, unfortunately, does not follow from our
Theorem

3.1
for
m>3
as the latter only applies to the regime
d=\Omega(n).

Conjecture 1.9

(
4.16
)
.

Suppose that
\sigma
is a symmetric connection such that
\widehat{\sigma}(S)=0
whenever
1\leq|S|\leq m-1
or
d-m-1\leq|S|\leq d
for a fixed constant
m.
If
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3/m})
holds,

{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\big)=o(1).

In
Proposition

6.13
we show that, if correct, this conjecture is tight up to logarithmic factors. Specifically, there exist connections
\sigma
for which the conditions of
4.16
are satisfied and the signed-triangle statistic distinguishes the two models whenever
d=\tilde{o}(n^{3/m}).

Some further nearly immediate applications of
Theorem

1.6
in the symmetric setting are the following.

1.

Hard Thresholds Connections.
When
\sigma={\mathbf{T}}_{p}
(defined over the hypercube analogously to the definition of the unit sphere), we prove in
Corollary

4.8
that if
d=\max\big(\tilde{\omega}(n^{3}p^{2}),\Omega(n\log n)\big)
holds, then
{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,{\mathbf{T}}_{p}),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\big)=o(1).
This matches the state of the art result on the sphere in the regime
p=\omega(n^{-1})
in
[
LMS+22a
]
. For the even analogue of

{\mathbf{T}}_{p}
given by
\mathbf{D}_{p}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathds{1}\left[|\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle|\geq\tau_{p/2,d}|\right]
with expectation
p,

\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\mathbf{D}_{p})
are indistinguishable when
d=\max\big((\tilde{\omega}(n^{3/2}p),\Omega(n\log n)\big)
and distinguishable when
d=o(n^{3/2}p^{3/2})
(see
Corollaries

4.10
and
6.2
).

2.

Lipschitz Connections.
In
Corollary

4.4
we show that if
d=\max\big(\Omega(n^{3}/r^{4}),\Omega(n\log n)\big)
for an
\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}}
-Lipshcitz connection
\sigma,

{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\big)=o(1)
, extending the work of
[
LR21a
]
to the non-monotone case. Again, we improve the dependence to
d=\max\big(\Omega(n^{3/2}/r^{2}),\Omega(n\log n)\big)
in the even case in
Corollary

4.5
.

3.

Interval Unions and Fluctuations.
In
Section

4.1.3
we extend the classical threshold model in which
\sigma
is an indicator of a single interval, i.e.
[\tau_{p,d},+\infty)
to the case when
\sigma
is an indicator of a union of
s
disjoint intervals. Namely, we have indistinguishability when
d=\Omega(n^{3}+n^{3/2}s^{2})
in the general case and
d=\Omega(n^{3/2}s^{2})
in the even case.
We give lower bounds in
Section

6.1.2
when
d=o(ns^{2}),

showing that the dependence on
s
is tight.

4.

Low-Degree Connections.
In
Section

4.1.5
we consider the case when
\sigma
is a symmetric polynomial of constant degree. In that case, we prove that the two graph models are indistinguishable when
d=\Omega(n)
and
d=\tilde{\omega}(\min(n^{3}p^{2},np^{-2/3})).
In
Section

6.1.4
, we give a detection lower bound for
d=o((np)^{3/4}),
which leaves a polynomial gap in this setting.

1.2.2
Modifications of Symmetric Connections

As
Theorem

3.1
only depends on the size of Fourier coefficients on each level, it also applies to transformations of symmetric connections which do not increase the absolute values of Fourier coefficients. We analyse two such transofrmations, which we call “coefficients contractions” and “repulsion-attraction” twists. Both have natural interpretations, see
Section

4.2.1
.

A special case of a coefficient contraction is the following. For a symmetric connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
and fixed
\mathbf{g}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d},
the coefficient contraction
\sigma_{\mathbf{g}}
is given by
\sigma_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x}):=\sigma(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{g})
(which simply negates the coordinates
x_{i}
for which
g_{i}=-1
). All of our indistinguishability results from
Section

1.2.1
continue to hold with the exact same quantitative bounds on
d
if we replace the connection
\sigma
with an arbitrary coefficient contraction
\sigma_{\mathbf{g}}
of it. While it is a virtue of our proof techniques for indistinguishability results that they capture these more general non-symmetric connections, it is also a drawback. It turns out that certain coefficient contractions become statistically indistinguishable from
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
for polynomially smaller dimensions, but this phenomenon is not captured by
Theorem

1.6
.

A particularly interesting case which illustrates this is the following. In
Section

6.2
, we consider coefficient contractions with
\mathbf{h}=\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{d_{1}}\underbrace{-1,-1,\ldots,-1}_{d_{1}}
for
d=2d_{1}.
This choice of
\mathbf{h}
negates exactly half the variables. In that case,
\sigma_{\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x}^{-1}\mathbf{y})=\sigma_{\mathbf{h}}(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y})
depends on
\sum_{i\leq d_{1}}x_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{j>d_{1}}x_{j}y_{j}.
Thus, edges depend on a difference of inner products rather than inner products (equivalently, inner products with signature

(\underbrace{+,+,\ldots,+}_{d_{1}}\underbrace{-,-,\ldots,-}_{d_{1}})

). The corresponding analogue of Wishart matrices is of the form
XX^{T}-YY^{T},
where
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{1}}.
In the Gaussian case, we prove the following statement.

Theorem 1.10

(
Theorem

6.17
)
.

Define the law of the difference of two Wishart matrices as follows.
\mathcal{W}(n,d_{1},-d_{1})
is the law of
XX^{T}-YY^{T},
where
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{1}}
are iid matrices with independent standard Gaussian entries. If

d_{1}={\omega}(n^{2}),
then

\displaystyle{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathcal{W}(n,d_{1},-d_{1}),\sqrt{d_{1}}M(n)\big)=o_{n}(1).

Compared to
Theorem

1.2
, this statement differs only in dimension. A Wishart matrix converges to GOE when
d=\omega(n^{3}),
but a difference of independent Wishart matrices converges to GOE when
d=\omega(n^{2}).

This proves that in the Gaussian case, for any connection
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
over
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}

that only depends on
\sum_{i\leq d_{1}}x_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{j>d_{1}}x_{j}y_{j},
it is the case that

{\mathsf{TV}}\big(\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{2d_{1}},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{2d_{1}}),p,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\big)=o(1)
whenever
d={\omega}(n^{2}).
We expect that the same thing holds for the hypercube model, but our
Theorem

3.1
yields dependence
d={\omega}(n^{3}).

In
Section

8.1
, we use
Theorem

1.10
to rigorously show that there is an inefficiency in deriving
1.5
arising from the use of KL-convexity, as predicted in
[
LR21a
]
.

1.2.3
Typical Indicator Connections Induce a Statistical-Computational Gap

Finally, we study the behaviour of
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
where
A\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{d}
is a “typical” subset of the hypercube,
\sigma_{A}
is its indicator, and
p_{A}=|A|/2^{d}
is the expectation. We obtain strong evidence for a nearly-exponential statistical-computational gap.

Theorem 1.11

(Informal,
Theorems

4.19
,
7.5
,
7.2
and
7.3
)
.

In the setup above,

(a)

If
d=\Omega(n\log n),
then
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p_{A},\sigma_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)\Big)=o(1)
for a
1-2^{-\Omega(d)}
fraction of the subsets
A
of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.

(b)

If
d=\frac{n}{2}-\omega(1),
then

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p_{A},\sigma_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)\Big)=1-o(1)
for all subsets
A
of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.

(c)

If
d=\log^{\Omega(1)}n,
no low-degree polynomial test can distinguish
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
and
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
for a
1-2^{-\Omega(d)}
fraction of the subsets
A
of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.

Our result is more general and holds for other values of
p
beyond
1/2.
We describe this in more detail in the next section, when we extend
Theorem

1.11
to arbitrary groups of appropriate size. The advantage of the Fourier-analytic proof in the hypercube setting over our proof in the setting of general groups is that it gives an explicit construction of connections for which the statistical limit occurs at
d=\tilde{\Theta}(n).
In particular, this is the case for all
A
such that
\sigma_{A}
is
{{d^{C}}}/{2^{d/2}}
-regular for a fixed constant
C
(see
4.20
and
4.21
).

1.3
Random Algebraic Graphs

The main insight about the Boolean hypercube is that we can analyze connections
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
depending only the group product, which is much more general than the inner product induced by
\mathbb{R}^{d}.
It turns out that this construction can be naturally extended to a wide class of groups.

Definition 1.12

(Random Algebraic Graphs)
.

Suppose that
\Omega
is a unimodular locally compact Hausdorff topological group
\mathcal{G}
with the unique left- and right-invariant probability Haar measure
\mu
corresponding to distribution
\mathcal{D}.
2
2

2

If
\mathcal{G}
is finite, this measure is just the uniform measure over the group elements. If
\mathcal{G}
is, say, the orthogonal group, this is the Haar measure over it. Importantly, since the group is unimodular, the left and right Haar measures coincide.

Suppose further that the connection
\zeta:\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow[0,1]
given by
\zeta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
only depends on
\mathbf{x}^{-1}\mathbf{y},
that is
\zeta(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\sigma(\mathbf{x}^{-1}\mathbf{y})
for some
\sigma:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow[0,1].
Then, we call
\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\mathcal{G},{\mathcal{D}},p,\zeta)
a left random algebraic graph. We use the notation

\mathsf{LRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma).
Similarly, define a right random algebraic graph and denote by
\mathsf{RRAG}
for connections depending only on
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}.

Clearly, for abelian groups such as
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
, right- and left- random algebraic graphs coincide, for which reason we will use the simpler notation
\mathsf{RAG}.
We now demonstrate the expressivity of random algebraic graphs by relating them to (approximate versions) of other well-studied graph models.

1. Random Geometric Graphs over the Sphere and Hypercube.

Random geometric graphs over the hypercube are random algebraic graphs. More surprisingly, any random geometric graph defined by
\Omega=\mathbb{S}^{d-1},{\mathcal{D}}=\mathsf{Unif},
and a connection
\zeta
depending only on inner products
can be represented as a random algebraic graph even though
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
does not possess a group structure when
d>3.

Namely, consider a different latent space - the orthogonal group
\mathcal{O}(d)=\Omega
with its Haar measure. The latent vectors are iid
“uniformly distributed” orthogonal matrices

U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{n}.
To generate uniform iid latent vectors on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1},
take

U_{1}v,U_{2}v,\ldots,U_{n}v
for an arbitrary
v\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},
say
v=e_{1}.
Define
\sigma(U):=\zeta(\langle vv^{T},U\rangle)
and observe that

\zeta(U_{i}v,U_{j}v)=\zeta(\langle U_{i}v,U_{j}v\rangle)=\zeta(\langle vv^{T},U_{i}^{T}U_{j}\rangle)=\sigma(U_{i}^{-1}U_{j}).

Clearly,
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\mathsf{Unif},p,\zeta)
and
\mathsf{LRAG}(n,\mathcal{O}(d),p,\sigma)
have the same distribution.

2. Cayley Graphs: Blow-ups and Random Subgraphs.

Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is a finite group and
\sigma
is an indicator of some
S\subseteq\mathcal{G}
which satisfies
a\in S
if and only if
a^{-1}\in S.
The Cayley graph
\Gamma(\mathcal{G},S)
has vertex set
\mathcal{G}
and edges between any two distinct vertices
g,h\in V(\Gamma(\mathcal{G},S))=\mathcal{G}
for which
gh^{-1}\in S.
We consider two regimes.

First, when
n=o(\sqrt{|\mathcal{G}|}).
With high probability, the latent vectors in
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma)
are pairwise distinct by the birthday paradox. This immediately leads to the following observation.

Observation 1.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is a group of size at least
n
and
S\subseteq\mathcal{G}
is closed under taking inverses.
Denote by
\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},S)
the uniform distribution over
n
-vertex induced subgraphs of
\Gamma(\mathcal{G},S).

If
|\mathcal{G}|=\omega(n^{2}),

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma),\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},S)\Big)=o(1).

Second, when
|\mathcal{G}|=o(\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\log n}).
With high probability, each
g\in\mathcal{G}
appears
\frac{n}{|\mathcal{G}|}(1+o(1))
times as a latent vector in
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma).
It follows that
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p,\sigma)
is approximately an
\frac{n}{|\mathcal{G}|}
-blow-up of
\Gamma(\mathcal{G},S).

It is interesting to consider whether this relationship to Cayley graphs can be used to understand random geometric graphs. For example, there are several results on the expansion properties of Cayley graphs
[
AR94
,
Con17
]
, which could be related to the expansion properties of random geometric graphs
[
LMS+22
]
.

3. Stochastic Block Model.

The stochastic block-model is given by an
n
vertex graph, in which
k
communities and edges between them are formed as follows (
[
RB17
]
). First, each vertex is independently assigned a uniform label in
\{1,2,\ldots,k\}.
Then, between every two vertices, an edge is drawn with probability
p
if they have the same label and with probability
q<p
otherwise. Denote this graph distribution by
\mathsf{SBM}(n,k,p,q).

\mathsf{SBM}(n,k,p,q)
can be modeled as a random algebraic graph as follows. Take an arbitrary abelian group
\mathcal{G}
which has a subgroup
\mathcal{H}
of order
|\mathcal{G}|/k.
Define
\sigma:\mathcal{G}\longrightarrow[0,1]
by

\sigma(\mathbf{g}):=q+(p-q)\times\mathds{1}[\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{H}].
Then,

\mathsf{RAG}(n,\mathcal{G},\frac{p+(k-1)q}{k},\sigma)
has the same distribution as
\mathsf{SBM}(n,k,p,q).
This follows directly from the observation that communities in the stochastic block model correspond to subsets of vertices with latent vectors in the same coset with respect to
\mathcal{H}
in the random algebraic graph.

1.4
Our Results on Typical Cayley Graphs

We utilize the connection between random algebraic graphs and Cayley graphs to study the high-probability behaviour of Cayley graphs when the generating set is chosen randomly. The distribution over random generating sets that we consider is the following.

Definition 1.14

(The ante-inverse-closed uniform measure)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a group and
0\leq p\leq 1
a real number, possibly depending on
\mathcal{G}.
Define the density
\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)
as follows. Consider the action of
C_{2}=\{1,\rho\}
on
\mathcal{G}
defined by
\rho(\mathbf{g})=\mathbf{g}^{-1}
and let
\mathcal{O}_{1},\mathcal{O}_{2},\ldots,\mathcal{O}_{t}
be the associated orbits. Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{t}
be
t
iid
\mathsf{Bernoulli}(p)
random variables. Then,
\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)
is the law of
S=\bigcup_{j\;:\;X_{j}=1}\mathcal{O}_{j}.

Our main result, extending
Theorem

1.11
, is the following.

Theorem 1.15

(Informal,
Theorems

5.1
,
7.5
and
7.2
)
.

Consider a finite group
\mathcal{G}.

(a)

If
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp\big(\Omega(n\log\frac{1}{p})\big),
then
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1)
with probability at least
1-|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/7}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p).

(b)

If
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(O(nH(p))),
where
H
is the binary entropy function, then
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=1-o(1)
for any generating set
A.

(c)

If
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(\Omega(k\log n)),
no polynomial test of degree at most
k
with input the graph edges can distinguish
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A)
with high probability over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p).

We note that these results can be equivalently phrased if we replace
\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A)
by
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A}),
where
\sigma_{A}
is the indicator of
A
and
p_{A}
is its expectation.

In particular, this suggests that indistinguishability between
\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},A)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
depends only on the size of
\mathcal{G},
but not on its group structure. This continues the long-standing tradition of certifying that important properties of random Cayley graphs only depend on group size. Prior literature on the topic includes results on random walks
[
AD86
,
DH96
,
HO21
]
(see
[
HO21
]
for further references), related graph-expansion properties
[
AR94
]
, and chromatic numbers
[
Alo13
]
of typical Cayley graphs.

One major difference between previous results and our current result is the measure over generating sets used. Previous works used is what we call the
post-inverse-closed uniform measure.

Definition 1.16

(The post-inverse-closed uniform measure)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a finite group and
k\leq|\mathcal{G}|
be an integer. Define the density
\mathsf{PostU}(\mathcal{G},k)
as follows. Draw
k
uniform elements
Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{k}
from
\mathcal{G}
and set
Z=\{Z_{1},Z_{2},\ldots,Z_{k}\}.
Then,

\mathsf{PostU}(\mathcal{G},k)
is the law of
S=Z\cup Z^{-1}.

There are several differences between the two measures on generating sets, but the crucial one turns out to be the following. In the
ante-inverse-closed uniform measure
, each element appears in
S
with probability
p.
In the
post-inverse-closed uniform measure
, an element
\mathbf{g}
of order two appears with probability
q:=1-(1-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|})^{k}
since
\mathbf{g}\in Z\cup Z^{-1}
if and only if
\mathbf{g}\in Z.
On the other hand, an element
\mathbf{h}
of order greater than 2 with probability
1-(1-\frac{2}{|\mathcal{G}|})^{k}\approx 2q.
This suggests that we cannot use the
\mathsf{PostU}
measure for groups which simultaneously have a large number of elements of order two and elements of order more than two to prove indistinguishability results such as the ones given in
Theorem

1.15
. Consider the following example.

Example 1.17
.

Consider the group
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}_{3}\times\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{2}}_{d}.
Denote the three different cosets with respect to the
\mathcal{C}_{3}
subgroup by
H_{i}=\{i\}\times\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{2}}_{d}
for
i\in\{0,1,2\}.
Elements in
H_{0}
have order at most
2,
while elements in
H_{1},H_{2}
have order
3.
Now, consider
\mathsf{PostU}(\mathcal{G},k),
where
k
is chosen so that
{\mathbf{E}}[|S|\times|\mathcal{G}|^{-1}]\approx\frac{1}{2}.
Each element from
H_{0}
appears in
S
with probability
q
and each element in
H_{1},H_{2}
with probability
\sim 2q,
such that
q\times|H_{0}|+2q\times(|H_{1}|+|H_{2}|)=\frac{|\mathcal{G}|}{2}.
It follows that
q\approx\frac{3}{10}.

Let
S
be drawn from
\mathsf{PostU}(\mathcal{G},k)
and
G\sim\Gamma_{n}(\mathcal{G},S).
With high probability, the vertices of
G
can be split into three groups -
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2},
where
G_{i}
contains the vertices coming from coset
H_{i}.
The average density within each of
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
in
G
will be approximately
\frac{3}{10},
while the average density of edges of
G
which have at most one endpoint in each of
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
will be approximately
\frac{6}{10}.
Clearly, such a partitioning of the vertices
G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
typically does not exist for Erdős-Rényi graphs. Thus, no statistical indistinguishability results can be obtained using the
\mathsf{PostU}
measure over
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}_{3}\times\underbrace{\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{C}_{2}}_{d}.

Of course, there are other differences between
\mathsf{AnteU}
and
\mathsf{PostU},
such as the fact that in the
\mathsf{PostU}
measure elements from different orbits do not appear independently (for example, as the maximal number of elements of
S
is
2k
). These turn out to be less consequential. In
Appendix

E
, we characterize further distributions beyond
\mathsf{AnteU}
for which our statistical and computational indistinguishability results hold. Informally, the desired property of a distribution
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)
is the following. Let
A\sim\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p).
For nearly all elements
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{G},
if we condition on any event
E
that
\mathsf{polylog}(|G|)
elements besides
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{-1}
are or are not in
A,
we still have
{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{x}\in A|E]=p+O(|\mathcal{G}|^{-c}),
where
c\in(0,1)
is constant.

In particular, this means that our results over
\mathcal{G}=\{\pm 1\}^{d}
hold when we use
\mathsf{PostU}(\mathcal{G},k)
for an appropriately chosen
k
so that
(1-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|})^{k}=p+O\big(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}\big)
since all elements of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
have order
2.
Similarly, we can use the
\mathsf{PostU}
density with an appropriate
k
for all groups without elements of order two, which include the groups of odd cardinality.

2
Notation and Preliminaries

2.1
Information Theory

The main subject of the current paper is to understand when two graph distributions -
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma,p)
- are statistically indistinguishable or not. For that reason, we need to define similarity measures between distributions.

Definition 2.1

(For example,
[
PWng
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}
be two probability measures over the measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F}).
Suppose that
\mathbf{P}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbf{Q}
and has a Radon-Nikodym derivative
\frac{d{\mathbf{P}}}{d\mathbf{Q}}.
For a convex function
f:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
f(1)=0,
the
f
-divergence between
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}
is given by

\mathsf{D}_{f}({\mathbf{P}}\|\mathbf{Q})=\int_{\Omega}f\bigg(\frac{d{\mathbf{P}}}{d\mathbf{Q}}\bigg)d\mathbf{Q}.

The two
f
-divergences of primary interest to the current paper are the following:

1.

Total Variation:

{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}):=\mathsf{D}_{f}({\mathbf{P}}\|\mathbf{Q})
for
f(x)=\frac{1}{2}|x-1|.
It easy to show that
{\mathsf{TV}}
can be equivalently defined by

{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q})=\sup_{A\in\mathcal{F}}|\mathbf{P}(A)-\mathbf{Q}(A)|.

2.

KL-Divergence:

{\mathsf{KL}}(\mathbf{P}\|\mathbf{Q}):=\mathsf{D}_{f}({\mathbf{P}}\|\mathbf{Q})
for
f(x)=x\log x
(where
f(0)=0
).

It is well-known that total variation characterizes the error probability of an optimal statistical test.
However, it is often easier to prove bounds on KL and translate to total variation via Pinsker’s inequality.

Theorem 2.2

(For example,
[
PWng
]
)
.

Let
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q}
be two probability measures over the measurable space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
such that
\mathbf{P}
is absolutely continuous with respect to
\mathbf{Q}.
Then,

{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q})\leq\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}{\mathsf{KL}}(\mathbf{P}\|\mathbf{Q})}.

Another classic inequality which we will need is the data-processing inequality. We only state it in the very weak form in which we will need it.

Theorem 2.3

(For example,
[
PWng
]
)
.

For any two distributions
\mathbf{P},\mathbf{Q},
any measurable deterministic function
h,
and any
f
-divergence
\mathsf{D}_{f},
the inequality
\mathsf{D}_{f}({\mathbf{P}}\|\mathbf{Q})\geq\mathsf{D}_{f}(h_{*}({\mathbf{P}})\|h_{*}(\mathbf{Q}))
holds. Here,
h_{*}({\mathbf{P}}),h_{*}(\mathbf{Q})
are the respective push-forward measures.

2.2
Boolean Fourier Analysis

One of the main contributions of the current paper is that it introduces tools from Boolean Fourier analysis to the study of random geometric graphs. Here, we make a very brief introduction to the topic. An excellent book on the subject is
[
ODo14
]
, on which our current exposition is largely based.

We note that
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
is an abelian group (isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}_{2}^{d}
) and we denote the product of elements
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}
in it simply by
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}.
Note that
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}^{-1}
over
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.

Boolean Fourier analysis is used to study the behaviour of functions
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}.
An important fact is the following theorem stating that all functions on the hypercube are polynomials.

Theorem 2.4
.

Every function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
can be uniquely represented as

f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x}),

where
\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}
and

\widehat{f}(S)
is a real number, which we call the Fourier coefficient on
S.

We call the monomials
\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})

characters
or
Walsh polynomials
. One can easily check that
\omega_{\emptyset}\equiv 1
and
\omega_{s}\omega_{T}=\omega_{S\Delta T}.

The Walsh polynomials form an orthogonal basis on the space of real-valued functions over
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.
Namely, for two such functions
f,g,
define

\langle f,g\rangle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[f(\mathbf{x})g(\mathbf{x})].

This is a well-defined inner-product, to which we can associate an
L_{2}
-norm
\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{2}:=\langle f,f\rangle
.

Theorem 2.5

(For example,
[
ODo14
]
)
.

The following identities hold.

1.

\langle\omega_{s},\omega_{T}\rangle=\mathds{1}[T=S].

2.

\langle f,g\rangle=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\widehat{g}(S).
In particular,

(a)

\widehat{f}(S)={\mathbf{E}}[f(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})].

(b)

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}^{2}=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)^{2}.

(c)

{\mathbf{E}}[f]=\widehat{f}(\emptyset),{\mathbf{Var}}[f]=\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)^{2}.

3.

The Fourier convolution
(f*g):\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
defined by

(f*g)[\mathbf{x}]={\mathbf{E}}[f(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{z}^{-1})g(\mathbf{z})]
can be expressed as

(f*g)[\mathbf{x}]=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\widehat{g}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x}).

Oftentimes, it is useful to study other
L_{p}
-norms, defined for
p\geq 1
by

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{p}:={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[|f|^{p}]^{1/p}.

By Jensen’s inequality,
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{q}
holds whenever
p<q.
It turns out, however, that a certain reverse inequality also holds. To state it, we need the noise operator.

Definition 2.6
.

For
\mathbf{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d},
let
\mathcal{N}_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})
be the
\rho
-correlated distribution of
\mathbf{x}
for
\rho\in[0,1].
That is,
\mathbf{y}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})
is defined as follows.
For each
i\in[d],
independently
y_{i}=x_{i}
with probability
\frac{1+\rho}{2}
and
y_{i}=-x_{i}
with probability
\frac{1-\rho}{2}.
Define:

1.

Noise Operator: The linear operator
T_{\rho}
on functions over the hypercube is defined by

T_{\rho}f[\mathbf{x}]:={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{y}\sim\mathcal{N}_{\rho}(\mathbf{x})}[f(\mathbf{y})].
The Fourier expansion of
T_{\rho}f
is given by

T_{\rho}f=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\rho^{|S|}\widehat{f}(S)\omega_{S}.

In light of this equality,
T_{\rho}
can also be defined for
\rho>1.

2.

Stability: The Stability of a function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given by

{\mathbf{Stab}}_{\rho}[f]:=\langle T_{\rho}f,f\rangle=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\rho^{|S|}\widehat{f}(S)^{2}=\left\lVert T_{\sqrt{\rho}}f\right\rVert_{2}^{2}

We are ready to state the main hypercontractivity result which we will use in
Section

3
.

Theorem 2.7

(
[
Bon70
]
)
.

For any
1\leq p\leq q
and any
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},
we have the inequality

\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{q}\leq\Big\|{T_{\sqrt{\frac{q-1}{p-1}}}f}\Big\|_{p}.

In particular, when
q\geq p=2,
one has

\displaystyle\left\lVert f\right\rVert^{2}_{q}\leq\sum_{S\subseteq\emptyset}(q-1)^{|S|}\widehat{f}(S)^{2}.

The way in which the noise operator changes the Fourier coefficient of
\omega_{S}
depends solely on the size of
S.
For that, and other, reasons, it turns out that it is useful to introduce the Fourier weights. Namely, for a function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},
we define its weight on level
i\in\{0,1,2,\ldots,d\}
by

\mathcal{W}^{i}({f})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]\;:\;|S|=i}\widehat{f}(S)^{2}.

We similarly denote
\mathcal{W}^{\leq i}({f})=\sum_{j\leq i}\mathcal{W}^{j}({f})
and
\mathcal{W}^{\geq i}({f})=\sum_{j\geq i}\mathcal{W}^{j}({f}).

Trivially, for each
i,
one has
\mathcal{W}^{i}({f})\leq\sum_{j=0}^{d}\mathcal{W}^{j}({f})=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{2}^{2}.
In particular, this means that if
|f|\leq 1
holds a.s. (which is the case for connections
\sigma
in random geometric graphs by definition),
\mathcal{W}^{i}({f})\leq 1
also holds for each
i.
It turns out that in certain cases, one can derive sharper inequalities.

Theorem 2.8

(
[
KKL88
]
)
.

For any function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[-1,1]
such that
\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{1}=\alpha,
the inequalities

\mathcal{W}^{\leq k}({f})\leq\Big(\frac{2e}{k}\ln(1/\alpha)\Big)^{k}\alpha^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathcal{W}^{\geq d-k}({f})\leq\Big(\frac{2e}{k}\ln(1/\alpha)\Big)^{k}\alpha^{2}

hold whenever
k\leq 2\ln(1/\alpha).

We end with a discussion on low-degree polynomials. We say that
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
has degree
k
if it is a degree
k
polynomial, that is
\widehat{f}(S)=0
whenever
|S|>k.
It turns out that such polynomials have surprisingly small Fourier coefficients. Namely, Eskenazis and Ivanisvili use a variant of the Bohnenblust-Hille to prove the following result.

Theorem 2.9

(
[
EI22
]
)
.

For any fixed number
k\in\mathbb{N},
there exists some constant
C_{k}
with the following property. If

f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[-1,1]
is a degree
k
function, then for any
\ell>0

\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=\ell}|\widehat{f}(S)|\leq C_{k}d^{\frac{\ell-1}{2}}.

In particular, if
f
is symmetric (
f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{n})=f(x_{\pi(1)},x_{\pi(2)},\ldots,x_{\pi(n)})
holds for each
\mathbf{x}
and permutation
\pi
), the following bound holds.

Corollary 2.10
.

If

f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[-1,1]
is a symmetric constant degree function, then

\mathcal{W}^{\ell}({f})=O(1/d)
for any
\ell>0.

Proof.

We simply note that
Theorem

2.9
implies that

\mathcal{W}^{\ell}({f})=\binom{d}{\ell}\bigg(\frac{\sum_{S\;:\>|S|=\ell}|\widehat{f}(S)|}{\binom{d}{\ell}}\bigg)^{2}=O\Big(d^{\ell}\times\frac{d^{\ell-1}}{d^{2\ell}}\Big).\qed

2.3
Detection and Indistinguishability via Fourier Analysis

Before we jump into the main arguments of the current paper, we make two simple illustrations of Fourier-analytic tools in the study of random algebraic graphs, which will be useful later on. These observations cast new light on previously studied objects in the literature.

2.3.1
Indistinguishability

First, we will interpret the function
\gamma
from
1.5
as an autocorrelation function.

Proposition 2.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}=\{\pm 1\}^{d}
and suppose that

\sigma
only depends on
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}.
Then,

\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=(\sigma-p)*(\sigma-p)[\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}].

Proof.

Let
\sigma(\mathbf{g})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{\sigma}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g})
for
\mathbf{g}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}.
In particular,
\widehat{\sigma}(\emptyset)=p.
Now, we have

\displaystyle\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{z})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z})-p)]={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{z}}\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq S,T\subseteq[d]}\left[\widehat{\sigma}(S)\widehat{\sigma}(T)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{z}^{-1})\omega_{T}(\mathbf{z}\mathbf{y}^{-1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq S,T\subseteq[d]}\widehat{\sigma}(S)\widehat{\sigma}(T)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})\omega_{T}(\mathbf{y}^{-1}){\mathbf{E}}[\omega_{T\delta S}(\mathbf{z})]=\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{2}\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1})

\displaystyle=(\sigma-p)*(\sigma-p)[\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}].\qed

The same argument applies verbatim when we replace
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
with an arbitrary finite abelian group (and some other groups such as a product of a finite abelian group with a torus, provided
\sigma
is
L_{2}
-integrable with respect to
{\mathcal{D}}
).
Hence our use of
\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}
.
In light of
Eq.

4
, we simply need to prove bounds on the moments of the autocorrelation of
\sigma-p
(i.e., the centered moments of the autocorrelation of
\sigma
).

2.3.2
Detection Using Signed Cycles in Temrs of Fourier Coefficients

Let the indicator of edge
i,j
be
G_{i,j}.
Essentially all detection algorithms appearing in the literature on random geometric graphs are derived by counting “signed”
k
-cycles in the respective graph for
k\in\{3,4\}

[
LR21a
,
BDE+14
,
BBH21
]
.
Formally, these works provide the following detection algorithm, which is efficiently computable in time
O(n^{k})
when
k
is a constant. One sums over the set
\mathcal{C}
of all simple cycles
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k}
the quantity
\prod_{t=1}^{k}(G_{i_{t},i_{t+1}}-p),

where
i_{k+1}:=i_{1}.
The resulting statistic is

\tau_{k,p}(G):=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k})\in\mathcal{C}}\prod_{t=1}^{k}(G_{i_{t},i_{t+1}}-p)\,.

(5)

Since each edge
G_{i,j}
appears independently with probability
p
in an Erdős-Rényi graph, clearly
\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[\tau_{k}(G)]=0.

Thus, to distinguish with high probability between
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,p),
it is enough to show that

|{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)}\tau_{k}(G)|=\omega\b{(}{\mathbf{Var}}_{G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[\tau_{k}(G)]+{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{k}(H)]\b{)}

by Chebyshev’s inequality. We now demonstrate how to compute the relevant quantities in the language of Boolean Fourier analysis.

Observation 2.12
.

For any set of
k
indices
i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{k},
it is the case that

{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)}\left[\prod_{t=1}^{k}(G_{i_{t},i_{t+1}}-p)\right]=\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{k}.

Proof.

We simply compute

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[\prod(G_{i_{t},i_{t+1}}-p)\right]={\mathbf{E}}\left[{\mathbf{E}}\left[\prod(G_{i_{t},i_{t+1}}-p)|\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{x}_{i_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{t}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\left[{\mathbf{E}}\left[\prod(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i_{t}}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i_{t+1}})-p)|\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{x}_{i_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{t}}\right]\right].

Now, observe that
\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{x}_{i_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{k}}
are independently uniform over the hypercube. Thus, when we substitute
\mathbf{g}_{\ell}=\mathbf{x}_{i_{\ell-1}}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i_{\ell}},
the vectors
\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{k-1}
are independent and

\mathbf{g}_{k}=\mathbf{g}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{2}^{-1}\cdots\mathbf{g}_{k-1}^{-1}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[{\mathbf{E}}\left[\prod(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i_{t}}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{i_{t+1}})-p)|\mathbf{x}_{i_{1}},\mathbf{x}_{i_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i_{t}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{(\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{g}_{k-1})\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{1})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{2})-p)\cdots(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{t-1})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{g}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{2}^{-1}\cdots\mathbf{g}_{k-1}^{-1})-p).

Expanding the above product in the Fourier basis yields

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[\sum_{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{t}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\widehat{\sigma}(S_{i})\times\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\omega_{S_{i}}(\mathbf{g}_{i})\times\omega_{S_{t}}(\mathbf{g}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{g}_{2}^{-1}\cdots\mathbf{g}_{t-1}^{-1})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{t}}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\widehat{\sigma}(S_{i})\times\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}{\mathbf{E}}\left[\omega_{S_{i}}(\mathbf{g}_{i})\omega_{S_{t}}(\mathbf{g}_{i}^{-1})\right].

Since
\langle\omega_{S_{i}},\omega_{S_{t}}\rangle=\mathds{1}[S_{i}=S_{t}],
the conclusion follows.
∎

It follows that when
k=O(1),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{k}(G)]=\Theta\Big(n^{k}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{k}\Big).

In
Appendix

A
, using very similar reasoning, we show that the variance can also be bounded in terms of the Fourier coefficients of
\sigma.

Proposition 2.13
.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Var}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{3}(G)]=O\Big(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}+n^{4}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\Big).

Combining
2.12
and
2.13
, we make the following conclusion.

Corollary 2.14
.

If

n^{6}\Big(\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\Big)^{2}=\omega\Big(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}+n^{4}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\Big),

then
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=1-o(1).

Furthermore, the polynomial-time algorithm of counting signed triangles distinguishes
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and

\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
with high probability.

Thus, we have shown that the entirety of the detection via signed triangles argument has a simple interpretation in the language of Boolean Fourier analysis. One can argue similarly for counting signed
k
-cycles when
k>3,
but the variance computation becomes harder. We illustrate a very simple instance of this computation for
k=4
in
Appendix

F
.

3
The Main Theorem On Indistinguishability Over The Hypercube

In this section, we prove our main technical result on indistinguishability between
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma).
The statement itself does not look appealing due to the many conditions one needs to verify. In subsequent sections, we remove these conditions by exploring specific connections
\sigma.

Theorem 3.1
.

Consider a dimension
d\in\mathbb{N},
connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with expectation
p,
and constant

m\in\mathbb{N}.
3
3

3

We will usually take
m\leq 3
when applying the theorem, so one can really think of it as a small constant.
There exists a constant
K_{m}
depending only on
m,
but not on
\sigma,d,n,p,
with the following property.
Suppose that
n\in\mathbb{N}
is such that
nK_{m}<d.

For
1\leq i\leq d,
let
B_{i}=\max\Big\{|\widehat{\sigma}(S)|\binom{d}{i}^{1/2}:|S|=i\Big\}.
Denote also

C_{m}=\sum_{i=m+1}^{\frac{d}{2en}}B_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=d-\frac{d}{2en}}^{d-m-1}B_{i}^{2}\quad\text{and}\quad D=\sum_{\frac{d}{2en}\leq j\leq d-\frac{d}{2en}}B_{i}^{2}.

If the following conditions additionally hold

•

d\geq K_{m}\times n\times\Big(\frac{C_{m}}{p(1-p)}\Big)^{\frac{2}{m+1}},

•

d\geq K_{m}\times n\times\Big(\frac{B^{2}_{u}}{p(1-p)}\Big)^{\frac{2}{u}}
for all
2\leq u\leq m,

•

d\geq K_{m}\times n\times\Big(\frac{B^{2}_{d-u}}{p(1-p)}\Big)^{\frac{2}{u}}
for all
2\leq u\leq m,

then

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p))

\displaystyle\leq K_{m}\times\frac{n^{3}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\times\left(\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{d^{i}}+\sum_{i=d-m}^{d}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{d^{i}}+\frac{C^{2}_{m}}{{d}^{m+1}}+{D^{2}}\times\exp\left(-\frac{d}{2en}\right)\right).

Before we proceed with the proof, we make several remarks. First, when one applies the above theorem, typically the four conditions are reduced to just the much simpler
d\geq K_{m}\times n.
This is the case, for example, when
\sigma
is symmetric. Indeed, note that
\sum_{i=1}^{d}B_{i}^{2}={\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]\leq p(1-p)
in that case, so the three inequalities are actually implied by
d\geq K_{m}\times n.
This also holds for “typical”
\{0,1\}
-valued connections as we
will see in
Section

4.3
.

Next, we explain the expression bounding the
{\mathsf{KL}}
divergence. In it, we have separated the Fourier levels
into essentially
2m+3
intervals:

•

I_{u}=\{u\}
for
u\in[m],

•

I_{m+u}=\{d-u\}
for
u\in[m],

•

I_{2m+1}=[m+1,\frac{d}{2en}]\cup[d-\frac{d}{2en},d-m-1],

•

I_{2m+2}=(\frac{d}{2en},d-\frac{d}{2en}),

•

I_{2m+3}=\{d\}.

There are two reasons for this. First, as we will see in
Sections

4.1
and
4.1.4
, levels very close to 0 and
d,
i.e indexed by
O(1)
or
d-O(1)
play a fundamentally different role than the rest of the levels. This explains why
I_{1},I_{2},\ldots I_{2m},I_{2m+3}
are handled separately and why
I_{2m+1}
and
I_{2m+2}
are further separated (note that if
m
is a constant, levels
m,m+1,\ldots,2m
in
I_{2m+1}
also have indices of order
O(1)
). There is a further reason why
I_{2m+2}
is held separately from the rest. On the respective levels, significantly stronger hypercontractivity bounds hold (see
Theorem

3.5
).

While the above theorem is widely applicable, as we will see in
Section

4
, it has an unfortunate strong limitation - it requires that
d=\Omega_{m}(n)
even when the other three inequalities are satisfied for much smaller values of
d
(for an example, see
Section

4.1.4
). In
Section

8.1
, we show that this is not a consequence of the complex techniques for bounding moments of
\gamma
that we use. In fact, any argument based on
1.5
applied to
\{0,1\}
-valued connections over the hypercube requires
d=\Omega(n).

In particular, this means that one needs other tools to approach
1.1
in its analogue version on the hypercube when
p=o(n^{-2/3})
and our conjecture
4.16
. In
Theorem

7.5
, we illustrate another, independent of proof techniques, reason why
d=\Omega(n)
is necessary for
\{0,1\}
-valued connections with expectation of order
\Theta(1).

Finally, we end with a brief overview of the proof, which is split into several sections.

1.

First, in
Section

3.1
, we “symmetrize”
\sigma
by increasing the absolute values of its Fourier coefficients, so that all coefficients on the same level are equal. This might lead to a function which does not take values in
[0,1].
As we will see, this does not affect our argument.

2.

Then, in
Section

3.2
, using a convexity argument, we bound the moments of the symmetrized function by the moments on several (scaled) elementary symmetric polynomials which depend on the intervals
I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{2m+3}
described above.

3.

Next, in
Section

3.3
, we bound separately the moments of elementary symmetric polynomials using hypercontractivity tools. We prove new, to the best of our knowledge, bounds on the moments of elementary symmetric polynomials in certain regimes.

4.

Finally, we put all of this together to prove

Theorem

3.1
in
Section

3.4
.

3.1
Symmetrizing The Autocorrelation Function

In light of
Eq.

4
and
1.5
, we need to bound the moments of
\gamma.
Our first observation is that we can, as far as bounding
k
-th moments is concerned, replace
\gamma
by a symmetrized version.

Proposition 3.2
.

Suppose that
\gamma,B_{1},B_{2},\ldots,B_{d}
are as in
Theorem

3.1
and consider the function

f(\mathbf{g}):=\sum_{\emptyset\subseteq S\subsetneq[d]}\frac{B_{|S|}^{2}}{\binom{d}{|S|}}\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g}).

Then, for any
k\in\mathbb{N},
it holds that

\displaystyle 0\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}}[\gamma(\mathbf{g})^{k}]\leq{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}}[f(\mathbf{g})^{k}].

Proof.

Using
Proposition

2.11
, we know that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}}[\gamma(\mathbf{g})^{k}]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\left(\sum_{\emptyset\subsetneq S\subset[d]}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g})\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k}}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{1})\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{2})\cdots\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{k}){\mathbf{E}}[\omega_{S_{1}}(\mathbf{g})\omega_{S_{2}}(\mathbf{g})\cdots\omega_{S_{k}}(\mathbf{g})].

Now, observe that
{\mathbf{E}}[\omega_{S_{1}}(\mathbf{g})\omega_{S_{2}}(\mathbf{g})\cdots\omega_{S_{k}}(\mathbf{g})]
takes only values
0
and 1, which immediately implies non-negativity of
{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{k}].
Let
\mathcal{ECV}
be the set of
k
-tuples
(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k})
on which it is 1 (this is the set of even covers). Using that
|\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S)|\leq B_{|S|}^{2}\binom{d}{|S|}^{-1}
holds for all
S\neq\emptyset
by the definition of
B_{i},
we conclude that

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}}[\gamma(\mathbf{g})^{k}]&=\sum_{(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k})\in\mathcal{ECV}}\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{1})\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{2})\cdots\widehat{\sigma}^{2}(S_{k})\\
&\leq\sum_{(S_{1},S_{2},\ldots,S_{k})\in\mathcal{ECV}}\frac{B_{|S_{1}|}^{2}}{\binom{d}{|S_{1}|}}\frac{B_{|S_{2}|}^{2}}{\binom{d}{|S_{2}|}}\cdots\frac{B_{|S_{k}|}^{2}}{\binom{d}{|S_{k}|}}={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}}[f(\mathbf{g})^{k}].\end{split}

(6)

The last equality holds for the same reason as the first one in
Eq.

6
.
∎

Since
f
is symmetric, we can express it in a more convenient way as a weighted sum of the elementary symmetric polynomials
e_{i}.
That is,

f(\mathbf{g})=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{B_{i}^{2}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}(\mathbf{g}).

(7)

Note that if
\sigma
is symmetric, we have not incurred any loss at this step since
\gamma=f.
Moving forward, we will actually bound the
L_{k}
-norms of
f,
i.e. use the fact that
{\mathbf{E}}[f^{k}]\leq{\mathbf{E}}[|f|^{k}]=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{k}^{k}.
There is no loss in doing so when
k
is even. One can also show that there is no loss (beyond a constant factor in the KL bound in
Theorem

3.1
) for
k
odd as well due to the positivity of
{\mathbf{E}}[f^{k}].
Considering the expression in
Eq.

4
, we have

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}}_{(x,y)\sim\mathcal{D}\times\mathcal{D}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]=\sum_{t=0}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[\gamma(\mathbf{g})^{k}]}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}&=1+\sum_{t\geq 2}\binom{k}{t}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{k}]}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\\
&\leq 1+\sum_{t\geq 2}\binom{k}{t}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}[f^{k}]}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\\
&\leq 1+\sum_{t\geq 2}\binom{k}{t}\frac{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{k}^{k}}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}},\end{split}

(8)

where we used that
{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma]=\widehat{\gamma}(\emptyset)=0
by
Proposition

2.11
.

3.2
Splitting into Elementary Symmetric Polynomials

Our next step is to use a convexity argument to show that when bounding
Eq.

8
, we simply need to consider the case when all the mass is distributed on constantly many levels.
The key idea is to view
Eq.

8
as a function of the coefficients on different levels. To do so, define

h(W^{1},W^{2},\ldots,W^{d}):=1+\sum_{t\geq 2}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{W^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}(\mathbf{g})\right\rVert^{k}_{k}.

Clearly,
(B^{2}_{1},B^{2}_{2},\ldots,B^{2}_{d})=(W^{1},W^{2},\ldots,W^{d})
corresponds to
f
and
h(B_{1}^{2},B_{2}^{2},\ldots,B_{d}^{2})
is the expression in the last line of
Eq.

8
. The high-level idea is that
h
is convex and, thus, its maximum on a convex polytope is attained at a vertex of a polytope. We will construct polytopes in such a way that vertices correspond to specific vectors of constant sparsity.

Proposition 3.3
.

The function
h
is convex.

Proof.

We simply show that
h
is a composition of convex functions. First, for any
t,

\displaystyle({W}^{i})_{i\in[d]}\longrightarrow\bigg\|\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{{W}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}(g)\bigg\|_{t}

is convex. Indeed, for any
\alpha\in[0,1]
and vectors
({W}_{1}^{i})_{i\in[d]}
and

({W}_{2}^{i})_{i\in[d]},

we have

\displaystyle\bigg\|{\sum_{i\in[d]}\frac{\alpha{W}_{1}^{i}+(1-\alpha){W}_{2}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}}\bigg\|_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|{\alpha\sum_{i\in[d]}\frac{{W}_{1}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}}\bigg\|_{t}+\bigg\|{(1-\alpha)\sum_{i\in I[d]}\frac{{W}_{2}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}}\bigg\|_{t}

\displaystyle=\alpha\bigg\|{\sum_{i\in[d]}\frac{{W}_{1}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}}\bigg\|_{t}+(1-\alpha)\bigg\|{\sum_{i\in[d]}\frac{{W}_{2}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}}\bigg\|_{t},

by convexity of norms. Since
x\longrightarrow x^{t}
for
t\geq 2
is increasing and convex on
[0,+\infty),
it follows that
\displaystyle({W}^{i})_{i\in I}\longrightarrow\bigg\|{\sum_{i\in[d]}\frac{{W}^{i}}{\binom{d}{i}}e_{i}(g)}\bigg\|^{t}_{t}
is also convex. Thus,
h
is convex as a linear combination with positive coefficients of convex functions.
∎

Now, we will define suitable polytopes on which to apply the convexity argument as follows. For any partition size
\Xi\in[d],
partition
\mathbf{I}=(I_{\xi})_{\xi=1}^{\Xi}
of
[d]
into
\Xi
disjoint sets, and non-negative vector

\mathbf{w}=(w_{\xi})_{\xi=1}^{\Xi},

define the following product of simplices
\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}.
It is given by

0\leq{W}^{i}
for all
i\in[d]
and
\sum_{i\in I_{\xi}}{W}^{i}\leq w_{\xi}
for all
\xi\in[\Xi].
Using convexity of
h
on
\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}},
we reach the following conclusion.

Corollary 3.4
.

For any
\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}
defined as just above,

\sup_{({W}^{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}}h\left(({W}^{i})_{i=1}^{d}\right)\leq\sup_{i_{\xi}\in I_{\xi}\;\forall\xi\in[\Xi]}1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{\Xi^{t}}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\bigg(\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{\xi}}{\binom{d}{i_{\xi}}}e_{i_{\xi}}}\bigg\|_{t}^{t}\bigg).

Proof.

Since
h
is convex by the previous theorem,
its supremum over the convex polytope
\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}
is attained at a vertex. Vertices correspond to vectors

({V}^{i})_{i=1}^{d}
of the following type. For each
\xi\in[\Xi],
there exists a unique index
i_{\xi}\in I_{\xi}
such that
V^{i_{\xi}}\neq 0
and, furthermore,
V^{i_{\xi}}=w_{\xi}.
Thus,

\sup_{({W}^{i})_{i=1}^{d}\in\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}}h\left(({W}^{i})_{i=1}^{d}\right)\leq\sup_{i_{\xi}\in I_{\xi}\;\forall\xi\in[\Xi]}1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}\bigg\|{\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}\frac{w_{\xi}}{\binom{d}{i_{\xi}}}e_{i_{\xi}}}\bigg\|_{t}^{t}.

Using triangle inequality and the simple inequality

(\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}u_{\xi})^{t}\leq\Xi^{t}\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}u^{t}_{\xi}
for non-negative reals
u_{\xi},

we complete the proof

\bigg\|{\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}\frac{w_{\xi}}{\binom{d}{i_{\xi}}}e_{i_{\xi}}}\bigg\|^{t}_{t}\leq\bigg(\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{\xi}}{\binom{d}{i_{\xi}}}e_{i_{\xi}}}\bigg\|_{t}\bigg)^{t}\leq\Xi^{t}\sum_{\xi\in[\Xi]}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{\xi}}{\binom{d}{i_{\xi}}}e_{i_{\xi}}}\bigg\|^{t}_{t}.\qed

Given the statement of
Theorem

3.1
, it should be no surprise that we will apply the intervals
I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{2m+3}
defined right after
Theorem

3.1
. In doing so, however, we will need to bound the norms of elementary symmetric polynomials which appear in
Corollary

3.4
. This is our next step.

3.3
Moments of Elementary Symmetric Polynomials

When dealing with norms of low-degree polynomials, one usually applies
Theorem

2.7
for
q=2
due to the simplicity of the formula for second moments. If we directly apply this inequality in our setup, however, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\lVert e_{i}(g)\right\rVert_{t}\leq\left\lVert(t-1)^{i/2}e_{i}(g)\right\rVert_{2}=(t-1)^{i/2}\binom{d}{i}^{1/2}.

This inequality becomes completely useless for our purposes when we consider large values of
i.
Specifically, suppose that

i=d/2.
Then, this bound gives
(t-1)^{d/4}2^{\Theta(d)},
which is too large if
t=\omega(1),
since

\left\lVert e_{i}(\mathbf{g})\right\rVert_{t}\leq\left\lVert e_{i}(\mathbf{g})\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq\binom{d}{i}\leq 2^{d}.

Even though the
L_{\infty}
bound is already much better, it is still insufficient for our purposes. Instead, we obtain the following better bounds in
Theorem

3.5
. To the best of our knowledge, the second of them is novel.

Theorem 3.5
.

Suppose that
d
and
t
are integers such that
d>2te
and
t>1.
Take any
s\in[0,d]
and define
\mathsf{sm}(s)=\min(s,d-s).
Then, we have the following bounds:

•

For any
s,

\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}\leq\binom{d}{s}^{1/2}(t-1)^{\frac{\mathsf{sm}(s)}{2}}.

•

If, furthermore,
\mathsf{sm}(s)\geq\frac{d}{2te},
then

\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}\leq\binom{d}{s}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{4te}\right).

Finally, this second bound is tight up to constants in the exponent.

Proof.

First, we show that we can without loss of generality assume
s\leq d/2
and, hence,
\mathsf{sm}(s)=s.
Indeed, note that for any function
q
on the hypercube and any character
\omega_{T},
clearly

\left\lVert q\omega_{T}\right\rVert_{k}={\mathbf{E}}[|q\omega_{T}|^{k}]^{1/k}={\mathbf{E}}[|q|^{k}]^{1/k}=\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{k}.
Thus,

\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}=\Big\|{\omega_{[d]}\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=s}\omega_{S}}\Big\|_{t}=\Big\|{\omega_{[d]}\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=s}\omega_{S}}\Big\|_{t}=\Big\|{\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=s}\omega_{\bar{S}}}\Big\|_{t}=\left\lVert e_{d-s}\right\rVert_{t}.

With this, we are ready to prove the first bound, which simply uses the hypercontractivity inequality
Theorem

2.7
. Indeed, when
s<d/2,
