\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}\leq\sqrt{t-1}^{s}\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{2}=(t-1)^{\frac{\mathsf{sm}(s)}{2}}\binom{d}{s}^{1/2}.

We now proceed to the much more interesting second bound. Suppose that
s\geq\frac{d}{2et}
and chose
\psi
such that
s-\psi=\frac{d}{2et}
(for notational simplicity, we assume
\frac{d}{2et}
is an integer). The problem of directly applying
Theorem

2.7
is that the degree
s
of the polynomial
e_{s}
is too high. We reduce the degree as follows.

\displaystyle\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}

\displaystyle=\Big\|{\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=t}\omega_{S}}\Big\|_{t}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|{\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\omega_{A\cup T}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-1}}\bigg\|_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}\bigg\|{\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\omega_{A}\omega_{T}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-1}}\bigg\|_{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}\bigg\|{\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\omega_{A}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-1}}\bigg\|_{t}.

We used the following facts.
Each
S
can be represented in
\binom{s}{\psi}
ways as
A\cup T,
where
A
and
T
are disjoint and
|T|=\psi.
Second, as
A
and
T
are disjoint,

\omega_{A\cup T}=\omega_{A}\omega_{T}.
Finally, we used that for any polynomial
q,

\left\lVert\omega_{T}q\right\rVert_{t}=\left\lVert q\right\rVert_{t}.
Now, as the polynomial in the last equation is of degree
s-\psi=\frac{d}{2et},
which is potentially much smaller than
s,
we can apply
Theorem

2.7
followed by Paresval’s identity to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}\bigg\|{\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\omega_{A}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-1}}\bigg\|_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}(t-1)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}\bigg\|{\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\omega_{A}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-1}}\bigg\|_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}(t-1)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}\sqrt{\sum_{A\;:\;|A|=s-\psi,A\cap T=\emptyset}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-2}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{T\;:\;|T|=\psi}(t-1)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}\sqrt{\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-2}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\binom{d}{\psi}(t-1)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}\sqrt{\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-2}}_{2}

\displaystyle=\binom{d}{s}\sqrt{(t-1)^{s-\psi}\binom{d}{\psi}^{2}\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-2}\binom{d}{s}^{-2}}.

We now use the simple combinatorial identity that

\displaystyle\binom{d}{\psi}\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}=\binom{s}{\psi}\binom{d}{s}.

One can either prove it by expanding the factorials or note that both sides of the equation count pairs of disjoint subsets
(T,A)
of
[d]
such that
|T|=\psi,|A|=s-\psi.
In other words, we have

\displaystyle\binom{d}{s}\sqrt{(t-1)^{s-\psi}\binom{d}{\psi}^{2}\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\binom{s}{\psi}^{-2}\binom{d}{s}^{-2}}

\displaystyle=\binom{d}{s}\sqrt{(t-1)^{s-\psi}\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}^{-1}}\leq\binom{d}{s}\sqrt{(t-1)^{s-\psi}\left(\frac{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\right)^{-(s-\psi)}}

\displaystyle=\binom{d}{s}\left(\frac{(t-1)(s-\psi)}{d-\psi}\right)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}\leq\binom{d}{s}\left(\frac{2t(s-\psi)}{d}\right)^{\frac{s-\psi}{2}}=\binom{d}{s}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{4et}\right),

as desired. Above, we used the bounds
\displaystyle\binom{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\geq\left(\frac{d-\psi}{s-\psi}\right)^{(s-\psi)}
and

d-\psi\geq d-s\geq d/2,
and the choice of
\psi
given by
\displaystyle s-\psi=\frac{d}{2et}.
With this, the proof of the bounds is complete.

All that remains to show is the tightness of the second bound. Note, however, that

{\mathbf{P}}[e_{s}(\mathbf{g})=\binom{d}{s}]=\frac{1}{2^{d}}

as this event happens when
\mathbf{g}_{i}=1
holds for all
i.

Therefore,

\left\lVert e_{s}\right\rVert_{t}\geq\left(\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{s}^{t}\right)^{1/t}=\binom{d}{s}2^{d/t}=\binom{d}{s}\exp\left(-O\left(\frac{d}{t}\right)\right).\qed

We make the following remarks about generalizing the inequality above. They are of purely theoretical interest as we do not need the respective inequalities in the paper.

Remark 3.6
.

The exact same bounds and proof also work if we consider weighted versions with coefficients in
[-1,1].
That is, polynomials of the form

q(\mathbf{g})=\sum_{S\;:\;|S|=s}\varepsilon_{S}\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g}),

where
\varepsilon_{S}\in[-1,1]
holds for each
S.
This suggests that as long as
q
has a small spectral infinity norm (i.e., uniform small bound on the absolute values of its Fourier coefficients), one can improve the classical theorem
Theorem

2.7
. We are curious whether this phenomenon extends beyond polynomials which have all of their weight on a single level.

Remark 3.7
.

When
\frac{d}{2et}<s<\frac{d}{2},
the same inequality with the exact same proof also holds when we evaluate
e_{s}
at other random vectors such as standard Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and the uniform distribution on the sphere since analogous inequalities to
Theorem

2.7
hold (see
[
SA17
, Proposition 5.48, Remark 5.50]
).

3.4
Putting It All Together

Finally, we combine all of what has been discussed so far to prove our main technical result.

Proof of
Theorem

3.1
.

First, we note that without loss of generality, we can make the following assumptions:

1.

B^{4}_{1}=O_{m}(\frac{dp^{2}(1-p)^{2}}{n^{3}}),

2.

B^{4}_{d-1}=O_{m}(\frac{dp^{2}(1-p)^{2}}{n^{3}}),

3.

B^{4}_{d}=O_{m}(\frac{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}{n^{3}}),

4.

D^{2}=O_{m}\left(\exp(\frac{d}{2en})\times\frac{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}{n^{3}}\right).

Indeed, if one of those assumptions is violated, the bound on the KL-divergence that we are trying to prove is greater than 2 (for appropriate hidden constants depending only on
m
). The resulting bound on total variation via Pinsker’s inequality is more than 1 and is, thus, trivial.

We now proceed to apply
Corollary

3.4
with the intervals
I_{1},I_{2},\ldots,I_{2m+3}
and the following weights.

•

I_{u}=\{u\}
and
w_{u}=B_{u}^{2}
for
u\in[m],

•

I_{m+u}=\{d-u\}
and

w_{m+u}=B_{d-u}^{2}
for
u\in[m],

•

I_{2m+1}=[m+1,\frac{d}{2en}]\cup[d-\frac{d}{2en},d-m-1]
and
w_{2m+1}=C_{m},

•

I_{2m+2}=[\frac{d}{2en},d-\frac{d}{2en}]
and
w_{2m+2}=D,

•

I_{2m+3}=\{d\}
and
w_{2m+3}=B_{d}^{2}.

The defined weights vector
\mathbf{w}
exactly bounds the weight of
f
on each respective level. Thus, the supremum of
h
over
\Delta_{\mathbf{I},\mathbf{w}}
is at least as large as the expression in
Eq.

8
which gives an upper bound on the total variation between the two random graph models of interest.
Explicitly, using
Eq.

4

and
Corollary

3.4
, we find

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{t}]

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\sup_{i_{u}\in I_{u}\;\forall u\in[2m+3]}\sum_{u\in[2m+3]}\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{u}}{\binom{d}{u}}e_{i_{u}}}\bigg\|_{t}^{t}.

We now consider different cases based on
u
in increasing order of difficulty.

Case 1)

When
u=2m+3.
Then,
i_{u}=d,
so
\left\lVert e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{t}=\left\lVert e_{d}\right\rVert_{t}=1.
We have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\left\lVert w_{u}e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}B_{d}^{2t}

\displaystyle=(1+\psi)^{k}-1-k\psi,

where
\displaystyle\psi=\frac{2m+3}{p(1-p)}B^{2}_{d}=o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)
by the assumption on
B_{d}
at the beginning of the proof. It follows that

(1+\psi)^{k}-1-k\psi\leq e^{1+\psi k}-1-\psi k=O(\psi^{2}k^{2}).

Thus, for
u=2m+3,

\begin{split}&\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\left\lVert w_{u}e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}=O_{m}\left(k^{2}\frac{B_{d}^{4}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(9)

Case 2)

When
u=2m+2.

We bound

\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\left\lVert\frac{w_{2m+2}}{\binom{d}{i_{2m+2}}}e_{i_{2m+2}}\right\rVert^{t}_{t}.

Note that for
i_{u}\in I_{2m+2},
we have
\mathsf{sm}(i)\geq\frac{d}{2ne}.
Thus,

\left\lVert e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{t}\leq\left\lVert e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{n}\leq\binom{d}{i_{u}}\exp(-\frac{d}{4en})
by
Theorem

3.5
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\frac{D^{t}}{\binom{d}{i_{u}}^{t}}\left\lVert e_{i_{u}}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\frac{D^{t}}{\binom{d}{i_{u}}^{t}}\binom{d}{i_{u}}^{t}\exp\left(-\frac{dt}{4en}\right)

\displaystyle=\left(1+\frac{D(2m+3)}{p(1-p)}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{4en}\right)\right)^{k}-1-k\frac{D(2m+3)}{p(1-p)}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{4en}\right).

Note, however, that

\psi:=\frac{D(2m+3)}{p(1-p)}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{4en}\right)=o\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)

by the assumption on
D
at the beginning of the proof. It follows that

(1+\psi)^{k}-1-\psi k=O(\psi^{2}k^{2}).

Thus, for
u=2m+2,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{2m+2}}{\binom{d}{i_{u}}}e_{i_{u}}}\bigg\|^{t}_{t}=O_{m}\left(\frac{D^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\exp\Big(-\frac{d}{2en}\Big)\right)

for a large enough hidden constant depending only on
m.

Case 3)

When
u=2m+1.
Again,
we assume without loss of generality that
i_{u}\leq d/2
and we want to bound

\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\bigg\|{\frac{w_{2m+1}}{\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}}e_{i_{u}}}\bigg\|_{t}^{t}.

Using
Theorem

3.5
, we know that
\displaystyle\left\lVert e_{i_{2m+1}}\right\rVert_{t}\leq\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{1/2}(t-1)^{\frac{i_{2m+1}}{2}},
so

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\frac{C_{m}^{t}}{\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{t}}\left\lVert e_{i_{2m+1}}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}(2m+3)^{t}\frac{C_{m}^{t}}{\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{t}}\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{t/2}(t-1)^{i_{2m+1}t/2}.

This time we show exponential decay in the summands. More concretely, we prove the following inequality

\binom{k}{t}\left(\frac{(2m+3)C_{m}}{p(1-p)\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{1/2}}\right)^{t}(t-1)^{i_{2m+1}t/2}\geq\frac{e}{2}\binom{k}{t+1}\left(\frac{(2m+3)C_{m}}{p(1-p)\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{1/2}}\right)^{t+1}t^{i_{2m+1}(t+1)/2}\,.

This reduces to showing that

\displaystyle\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{1/2}\geq\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}\frac{k-t}{t+1}\frac{C_{m}}{p(1-p)}t^{i_{2m+1}/2}\left(\frac{t}{t-1}\right)^{ti_{2m+1}/2}.

Using that
\left(\frac{t}{t-1}\right)^{t}\leq 4,\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}\geq\left(\frac{d}{i_{2m+1}}\right)^{i_{2m+1}},
and
n\geq k-t\geq k,

we simply need to prove that

\begin{split}\left(\frac{d}{i_{2m+1}}\right)^{i_{2m+1}/2}\geq\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}n\frac{C_{m}}{p(1-p)}t^{i_{2m+1}/2-1}4^{i_{2m+1}/2}.\end{split}

(10)

Since
t\leq k\leq n,
the right-hand side is maximized when
t=n,
so we simply need to show that

\displaystyle\left(\frac{d}{i_{2m+1}}\right)^{i_{2m+1}/2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}n^{i_{2m+1}/2}4^{i_{2m+1}/2}\frac{C_{m}}{p(1-p)}\Longleftrightarrow

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle\geq i_{2m+1}\times n\times 4\times\left(\frac{(2m+3)eC_{m}}{2p(1-p)}\right)^{\frac{2}{i_{2m+1}}}.

Now, the right-hand side has form
KxM^{\frac{1}{x}}:=Z(x)
for
x=i_{2m+1}.
The derivative of
\log Z(x)
is

\frac{1}{x}(1-\frac{1}{x}\log M),
which has at most one positive root, at
x=\log M.
Thus, the maximum of
Z(x)
on
[m+1,\frac{d}{2en}]
is at either
m+1
or
\frac{d}{2en}.
Substitution at these values, we obtain the following expressions for the right-hand side. First, when
i_{2m+1}=m+1,

O_{m}\left(n\times\left(\frac{C_{m}}{p(1-p)}\right)^{\frac{2}{m+1}}\right)\leq d,

where the inequality holds from the assumptions in the theorem for large enough constant
K_{m}.

Second, when
i_{2m+1}=\frac{d}{2en},

\displaystyle\frac{2}{e}d\times\left(\frac{(2m+3)eC_{m}}{2p(1-p)}\right)^{\frac{4en}{d}}

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{e}d\times\left(\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}\right)^{\frac{4en}{d}}\times\left(\left(\frac{C}{p(1-p)}\right)^{\frac{2}{m+1}}\right)^{\frac{en(m+1)}{d}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{e}d\times\left(\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}\right)^{\frac{4en}{d}}\times\left(\frac{1}{K_{m}}\times\frac{d}{n}\right)^{\frac{en(m+1)}{d}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{e}d\times\left(\frac{(2m+3)e}{2}\right)^{\frac{4en}{d}}\times K_{m}^{-\frac{en(m+1)}{d}}e^{e(m+1)}\,,

where we used
\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)^{\frac{n}{d}}\leq\exp(\left(\left(\frac{d}{n}\right)-1\right)\frac{n}{d})\leq e.
Now, for a large enough value of
K_{m},
when
d>K_{m}n,
the above expression is at most
d.

Since the exponential decay holds,

\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}\frac{C_{m}^{t}}{\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{t}}\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}^{t/2}(t-1)^{i_{2m+1}t/2}

\displaystyle=O\left(k^{2}\frac{C_{m}^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p^{2})\binom{d}{i_{2m+1}}}\right)

\displaystyle=O_{m}\left(k^{2}\frac{C_{m}^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p^{2})d^{m+1}}\right).

Case 4)

When
u\leq 2m.
The cases
u\in[2m]\backslash\{1,m+1\}
are handled in the exact same way as the previous Case 3), except that we use
w_{u}=B_{u}^{2}
when
u\in[m]
and

w_{u}=B^{2}_{d-u+m}
when
u\in[m+1,2m]
instead of
w_{u}=C_{m}.
The only subtlety occurs when
i_{u}\in\{1,d-1\}.
Then, the analogue of

Eq.

10
becomes

d^{1/2}\geq O_{m}\left(n\frac{w_{u}}{p(1-p)}t^{-1/2}\right).

This time, however, the right-hand side is maximized for
t=2.
Thus, we need

d=\Omega_{m}\left(n^{2}\frac{w_{u}^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\right),

which is satisfied by the assumption made in the beginning of the proof.

Combining all of these cases, we obtain that for any fixed
k,

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}p^{-t}(1-p)^{-t}{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{t}]

\displaystyle=1+O_{m}\Bigg(k^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{i}}+k^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\frac{B_{d-i}^{4}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{i}}+k^{2}\frac{C_{m}^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m+1}}+\frac{D^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{d}{en}\bigg)\Bigg).

Thus, the logarithm of this expression is at most

K_{m}\Bigg(k^{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{B_{i}^{4}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{i}}+k^{2}\sum_{i=0}^{m}\frac{B_{d-i}^{4}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{i}}+k^{2}\frac{C_{m}^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m+1}}+k^{2}\frac{D^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{d}{en}\bigg)\Bigg)

for an appropriately chosen
K_{m}.
Summing over
k\in[n-1],
we arrive at the desired conclusion.
∎

4
Applications of the Main Theorem

As already discussed, part of the motivation behind the current work is to extend the ongoing effort towards understanding random geometric graphs to settings in which the connection is not necessarily monotone or symmetric. We remove the two assumptions separately.
Initially, we retain symmetry and explore the implications of
Theorem

3.1
in existing monotone and novel non-monotone setups. Then, we focus on two specific classes of non-monotone connections which are obtained via simple modifications of symmetric connections. These two classes will be of interest later in
Section

6
as they lead to the failure of the commonly used signed-triangle statistic, and in
Section

8.1
as they rigorously demonstrate an inefficiency in
1.5
.
Finally, we go truly beyond symmetry by studying “typical” dense indicator connections.

4.1
Symmetric Connections

Consider
Theorem

3.1
for symmetric functions. Then,
B_{i}^{2}=\mathcal{W}^{i}({\sigma})\leq{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]\leq p(1-p).
Similarly, the inequalities
C_{m}\leq p(1-p),D\leq p(1-p)
hold. In particular, this means that as long as
d=\Omega_{m}(n),
all other conditions are automatically satisfied. Now, if
d=\Omega(n\log n)
for a large enough hidden constant, the exponential term disappears. If we set
m=2,
the term
\frac{n^{3}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}\frac{C_{m}^{2}}{d^{m+1}}\leq\frac{n^{3}}{d^{3}}=o_{n}(1)
also disappears. We have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.1
.

There exists an absolute constant
K
with the following property. Suppose that
d>Kn\log n.
Let
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
be a symmetric connection. Then,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p))

\displaystyle\quad=O\left(\frac{n^{3}\mathcal{W}^{d}({\sigma})^{2}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{1}({\sigma})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-1}({\sigma})^{2})}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{2}({\sigma})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-2}({\sigma})^{2})}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{2}}\right)+o(1).

For symmetric connections in the regime
d=\Omega(n\log n),
only Fourier weights on levels
1,2,d-1,d-2,d-1,d
matter. In
Section

4.1.4
we conjecture how to generalize this phenomenon.

Now, we proceed to concrete applications of
Corollary

4.1
. In them, we simply need to bound the weights of
\sigma
on levels
1,2,d-1,d-2,d.
All levels besides
d
are easy since they have a normalizing term polynomial in
d
in the denominator. However, more care is needed to bound
\mathcal{W}^{d}({\sigma}).
As we will see in
Section

4.1.3
, this is not just a consequence of our proof techniques, but an important phenomenon related to the “fluctuations” of
\sigma.
In the forthcoming sections, we use the following simple bound, the proof of which is deferred to
Appendix

B
.

Lemma 4.2
.

Suppose that
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is symmetric.
Denote by
f_{i}
the value of
f
when exactly
i
of its arguments are equal to
1
and the rest are equal to
-1.
Then,

|\widehat{f}([d])|\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{j\sim Bin(d-1,\frac{1}{2})}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|=O\left(\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{d}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

We remark that the quantity
\sum_{j=1}^{d}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|
is a direct analogue of the
total variation
appearing in real analysis literature
[
SS05
, p.117]
, which measures the “fluctuations” of a function. As the name “total variation” is already taken by a more central object in our paper, we will call this new quantity
fluctuation
instead. We denote it by
{\mathsf{Fl}}(f):=\sum_{j=1}^{d}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|.

We believe that the interpretation of
|\widehat{\sigma}([d])|
as a measure of fluctuations may be useful in translating our results to the Gaussian and spherical setups when non-monotone connections are considered. We discuss this in more depth in
Section

4.1.3
.

4.1.1
Lipschitz Connections

As the main technical tool for our indistinguishability results is
1.5
, we first consider Lipschitz connections to which Racz and Liu originally applied the claim
[
LR21a
]
. We show that their result
Theorem

1.4

can be retrieved using our framework even if one drops the monotonicity assumption. The first step is to bound

|\widehat{f}([d])|.

Proposition 4.3
.

If
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is a symmetric
L
-Lipschitz function with respect to the Hamming distance, meaning that

|f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i-1},1,x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n})-f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i-1},-1,x_{i+1},\ldots,x_{n})|\leq L

holds for all
i
and
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{i_{1}},x_{i},\ldots,x_{n},

then

|\widehat{f}([d])|\leq\frac{L}{2}.

Proof.

The proof is immediate given
Lemma

4.2
. Note that

|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|=|f(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{j+1},\underbrace{-1,\ldots,-1}_{d-j+1})-f(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{j},\underbrace{-1,\ldots,-1}_{d-j})|\leq L

holds for all
j.

∎

Using this statement, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 4.4
.

Suppose that
\sigma
is a symmetric
\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}}
-Lipschitz connection. If

d=\omega_{p}(\frac{n^{3}}{r^{4}})
and
d=\Omega(n\log n),

{\mathsf{KL}}(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p))=o(1).

Proof.

We will apply
Corollary

4.1
.
By the well-known bounded difference inequality,
[
Han
, Claim 2.4]
we know that since
\sigma
is
\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}}
-Lipschitz,
{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]\leq\frac{1}{r^{2}},
so each
\mathcal{W}^{i}({\sigma})
is bounded by
\frac{1}{r^{2}}.
On the other hand,
\mathcal{W}^{d}({\sigma})
is bounded by
\frac{1}{r^{2}d}
by
Proposition

4.3
. It follows that the expression in
Corollary

4.1
is bounded by

O\left(\frac{n^{3}}{dr^{4}}+\frac{n^{3}}{d^{2}r^{4}}+\frac{n^{3}}{d^{2}r^{4}}\right)=o(1).\qed

The flexibility of our approach allows for a stronger result when the map
\sigma
is even.

Corollary 4.5
.

Suppose that
\sigma
is a symmetric even
\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}}
-Lipschitz connection in the sense that

\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(-\mathbf{x})
also holds. If
d
is even,
d=\Omega(n\log n),
and

d=\omega_{p}(\frac{n^{3/2}}{r^{2}}),
then

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one in the previous proposition. However, we note that when
\sigma
is even, all Fourier coefficients on odd levels vanish. In particular, this holds for levels
1
and
d-1.
Thus, the expression
Corollary

4.1
is bounded by

O\left(\frac{n^{3}}{d^{2}r^{4}}+\frac{n^{3}}{d^{2}r^{4}}\right)=o(1).\qed

Remark 4.6
.

The stronger results for even connections observed in this section is a general phenomenon, which will reappear in the forthcoming results. It is part of our more general conjecture in
Section

4.1.4
. We note that the requirement that
d
is even is simply needed to simplify exposition. Otherwise, one needs to additionally bound
\mathcal{W}^{d-1}({\sigma})
which is a standard technical task (see
Appendix

D
).

4.1.2
Hard Threshold Connections

We now move to considering the classical setup of hard thresholds. Our tools demonstrate a striking new phenomenon if we make just a subtle twist – by introducing a “double threshold” – to the original model.

We start with the basic threshold model.
Let
\tau_{p}
be such that
{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[\sum_{i}x_{i}\geq\tau_{p}]=p.
Let
{\mathbf{T}}_{p}
be the map defined by
\mathds{1}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\geq\tau_{p}\right].
Again, we want to understand the Fourier coefficient on the last level. Using that there exists a unique
j
such that
f_{j+1}\neq f_{j}
for
f={\mathbf{T}}_{p}
in
Lemma

4.2
, in
Appendix

B
we obtain the following bound.

Proposition 4.7
.

\widehat{{\mathbf{T}}_{p}}([d])=O\Big(\frac{p\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\Big).

Corollary 4.8
.

Suppose that
d=\Omega(n\log n),d=\omega(n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}),
and
p=d^{O(1)}.
Then,

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,{\mathbf{T}}_{p})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Proof.

From
Theorem

2.8
, we know that
\mathcal{W}^{1}({\sigma})=O(p^{2}\log\frac{1}{p}),\mathcal{W}^{d-1}({\sigma})=O(p^{2}\log\frac{1}{p}),

\mathcal{W}^{2}({\sigma})=O(p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}),
and
\mathcal{W}^{d-2}({\sigma})=O(p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}).
From
Proposition

4.7
,

\mathcal{W}^{d}({\sigma})=O(d^{-1}p^{2}\log\frac{1}{p}).
Thus, the expression in
Corollary

4.1
becomes

O\left(\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}}{d}+\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{4}\frac{1}{p}}{d^{2}}+\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}}{d^{2}}\right)=o(1),

where we used the fact that
p
is polynomially bounded in
d.

∎

Remarkably, this matches the state of the art result for

p=\omega(\frac{1}{n})

of
[
LMS+22a
]
. As before, if we consider an even version of the problem, we obtain a stronger result.
Let
\delta_{p}
be such an integer that

{\mathbf{P}}[|\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}|\geq\delta_{p}]=p.
Consider the“double threshold” connection given by the indicator
\mathbf{D}_{p}(\mathbf{x})=\mathds{1}\left[|\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}|\geq\delta_{p}\right].
In
Appendix

B
, we prove the following inequality.

Proposition 4.9
.

\widehat{\mathbf{D}}_{p}([d])=O\Big(\frac{p\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\Big).

Corollary 4.10
.

Suppose that
d
is even,
d=\Omega(n\log n),d=\omega(n^{3/2}p\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}),
and
p=d^{O(1)}.
Then,

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\mathbf{D}_{p})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Proof.

As in the proof of
Corollary

4.5
, we simply note that when
d
is even, the weights on levels 1 and
d-1
vanish as
\mathbf{D}_{p}
is an even map. Thus, we are left with

O\left(\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{4}\frac{1}{p}}{d^{2}}+\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}}{d^{2}}\right)=o(1).\qed

Note that the complement of
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\mathbf{D}_{p})
is the random algebraic graph defined with respect to the connection
\sigma(\mathbf{x}):=\mathds{1}[\sum^{n}_{i=1}x_{i}\in(-\delta_{p},\delta_{p})].
This random graph is very similar to the one used in
[
KTW14
]
, where the authors consider the interval indicator
\mathds{1}\left[\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle\in[\tau_{1},\tau_{2}]\right],
albeit with latent space the unit sphere. The utility of interval indicators in
[
KTW14
]
motivates us to ask:
What if
\sigma
is the indicator of a union of more than one interval?

4.1.3
Non-Monotone Connections, Interval Unions, and Fluctuations

To illustrate what we mean by fluctuations, consider first the simple connection for
\kappa\in[0,1/2],
given by

\pi_{\kappa}(\mathbf{x}):=\frac{1}{2}+\kappa\omega_{[d]}(\mathbf{x}).

(11)

When viewed as a function of the number of ones in
\mathbf{x},
this function “fluctuates” a lot for large values of
\kappa.
Note that
\pi_{\kappa}
only takes values
{1}/{2}\pm\kappa
and alternates between those when a single coordinate is changed. In the extreme case
\kappa={1}/{2},

\pi_{\kappa}
is the indicator of all
\mathbf{x}
with an even number of coordinates equal to
-1.
Viewed as a function of
\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i},
this is
a union of
d/2+O(1)
intervals. A simple application of
1.5
gives the following proposition.

Corollary 4.11
.

Whenever
{n\kappa^{4/3}}=o(1),

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{\kappa}),\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)\Big)=o(1).

Proof.

Let
\gamma_{\kappa}
be the autocorrelation of
\pi_{\kappa}-\frac{1}{2}.
Clearly,

\gamma_{\kappa}=\kappa^{2}\omega_{[d]}(\mathbf{x}),
so
{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma_{\kappa}^{t}]=0
when
t
is odd and

{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma_{\kappa}^{t}]=\kappa^{2t}
when
t
is even. Since
n\kappa^{2}=o(1)
holds as
{n\kappa^{4/3}}=o(1),
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log{\mathbf{E}}\left[\left(1+\frac{\gamma_{\kappa}(\mathbf{x})}{p(1-p)}\right)^{k}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(1+\sum_{t=2}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{\kappa^{2t}}{4^{t}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(\left(1+\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\right)^{k}-k\frac{\kappa^{2}}{4}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\left(1+O\left(k^{2}\frac{\kappa^{4}}{16}\right)\right)=O\left(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}k^{2}\times\kappa^{4}\right)=O(n^{3}\kappa^{4}),

from which the claim follows.
∎

We will explore tightness of these bounds later on. The main takeaway from this example for now is the following - when the connection “fluctuates” more, it becomes more easily distinguishable. Next, we show that this phenomenon is more general. Recall the definition of fluctuations
{\mathsf{Fl}}
from
Lemma

4.2
. We begin with two simple examples of computing the quantity
{\mathsf{Fl}}.

Example 4.12
.

If
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
is monotone, then
{\mathsf{Fl}}(\sigma)\leq 1.
Indeed, this holds as all differences
f_{i+1}-f_{i}
have the same sign and, so,
\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}|f_{i+1}-f_{i}|=|\sum_{i=0}^{d-1}f_{i+1}-f_{i}|=|f_{d}-f_{0}|\leq 1.

Example 4.13
.

Suppose that
I=[a_{1},b_{1}]\cup[a_{2},b_{2}]\cup\cdots\cup[a_{s},b_{s}],
where these are
s
disjoint intervals with integer endpoints (
b_{i}+1<a_{i+1}
). Then, if

\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\mathds{1}[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\in I],
it follows that

{\mathsf{Fl}}(f)\leq 2s
as
|f_{b_{i}+1}-f_{b_{i}}|=1,|f_{a_{i}}-f_{a_{i}-1}|=1
and all other marginal differences equal
0.

We already saw in
Lemma

4.2
that
|\widehat{f}([d])|=O\left(\frac{{\mathsf{Fl}}(\sigma)}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

Using the same proof techniques developed thus far, we make the following conclusion.

Corollary 4.14
.

Suppose that
\sigma
is a symmetric connection. Whenever
d=\Omega(n\log n)
and
\displaystyle d=\omega\left(\max\left(n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p},n^{3/2}{\mathsf{Fl}}(\sigma)^{2}p^{-1}\right)\right)
are satisfied,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

If
\sigma
and
d
are even, the weaker condition

\displaystyle d=\omega\left(\max\Big(n^{3/2}p\log^{2}\frac{1}{p},n^{3/2}{\mathsf{Fl}}(\sigma)^{2}p^{-1}\Big)\right),

is sufficient to imply

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Example 4.15
.

A particularly interesting case is when
\sigma
is the indicator of
s
disjoint intervals as in
Example

4.13
. We obtain indistinguishability rates by replacing
{\mathsf{Fl}}(\sigma)
with
2s
above. Focusing on the case when
p=\Omega(1)
(so that the dependence on the number of intervals is clearer), we
need

d=\omega(n^{3}+n^{3/2}s^{2})

for indistinguishability for an arbitrary union of intervals and

d=\omega(n^{3/2}s^{2})

for indistinguishability for a union of intervals symmetric with respect to
0.

We note that both of these inequalities make sense only when
s=o(\sqrt{d}).
The collapse of this behaviour at
s\approx\sqrt{d}

is not a coincidence as discussed in
Remark

6.7
.

4.1.4
Conjecture for Connections Without Low Degree and High Degree Terms

We now present the following conjecture, which generalizes the observation that random algebraic graphs corresponding to connections
\sigma
satisfying
\sigma(\mathbf{x})=\sigma(-\mathbf{x})
are indistinguishable from
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
for smaller dimensions
d.

Let
k\geq 0
be a constant.
Suppose that the symmetric connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
satisfies
\mathcal{W}^{i}({\sigma})=0
whenever
i<k
and
\mathcal{W}^{i}({\sigma})=0
whenever
i>d-k.
Consider the bound on the
{\mathsf{KL}}
divergence between
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
in
Theorem

3.1
for
m=k+1.

Under the additional assumption
d=\Omega(n\log n),
it becomes of order

\frac{n^{3}B^{2}_{m}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m}}+\frac{n^{3}C_{m}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m+1}}\leq\frac{n^{3}{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m}}+\frac{n^{3}{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{m+1}}=O\left(\frac{n^{3}}{d^{m}}\right).

In particular, this suggests that whenever
d=\omega(n^{3/m}),

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).
Unfortunately, in our methods, there is the strong limitation
d=\Omega(n\log n).

Conjecture 4.16
.

Suppose that
m\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant and
\sigma
is a symmetric connection such that
the weights on levels
\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m+1,\ldots,d\}
are all equal to 0. If
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n^{3/m}),
then

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Unfortunately, our methods only imply this conjecture for
m=\{1,2,3\}
as can be seen from
Theorem

3.1
as we also need
d=\Omega(n\log n).
In
Section

6.1.3
, we give further evidence for this conjecture by proving a matching lower bound.
Of course, one should also ask whether there exist non-trivial symmetric functions satisfying the property that their Fourier weights on levels
\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m+1,\ldots,d\}
are all equal to 0. In
Appendix

C
,
we give a positive answer to this question.

4.1.5
Low-Degree Polynomials

We end with a brief discussion of low-degree polynomials, which are a class of particular interest in the field of Boolean Fourier analysis (see, for example,
[
EI22
,
ODo14
]
). The main goal of our discussion is to complement
4.16
. One could hastily conclude from the conjecture that it is only the “low degree” terms of
\sigma
that make a certain random algebraic graph
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
distinct from an Erdős-Rényi graph for large values of
d.
This conclusion, however, turns out to be incorrect - symmetric connections which are low-degree polynomials, i.e. everything is determined by the low degrees, also become indistinguishable from
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
for smaller dimensions
d.
Thus, a more precise intuition is that the interaction between low-degree and high-degree is what makes certain random algebraic graphs
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
distinct from Erdős-Rényi graphs for large values of
d.

Corollary 4.17
.

Suppose that
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a fixed constant and
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
is a symmetric connection of degree
k.
If
d=\Omega(n)
and
d=\omega_{k}\left(\min(n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p},np^{-2/3})\right),
then

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Proof.

We apply
Corollary

4.1
for
m=k
and obtain

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=O\left(\frac{n^{3}\mathcal{W}^{1}({\sigma})}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d}+\frac{n^{3}\mathcal{W}^{2}({\sigma})}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{2}}\right).

Now, we use
Corollaries

2.10
and
2.8
to bound the right-hand side in two ways by, respectively:

O\left(\frac{n^{3}}{p^{2}(1-p)^{2}d^{3}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad O\left(\frac{n^{3}p^{2}\log^{2}\frac{1}{p}}{d}\right).

The conclusion follows by taking the stronger bound of the two.
∎

4.2
Transformations of Symmetric Connections

One reason why
Theorem

3.1
is flexible is that it does not explicitly require
\sigma
to be symmetric, but only requires a uniform bound on the
absolute values
of Fourier coefficients on each level. This allows us, in particular, to apply it to modifications of symmetric connections. In what follows, we present two ways to modify a symmetric function such that the claims in
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.5
,
4.8
,
4.10
and
4.14
still hold. The two modifications are both interpretable and will demonstrate further important phenomena in
Section

6.2
.

It is far from clear whether previous techniques used to prove indistinguishability for random geometric graphs can handle non-monotone, non-symmetric connections. In particular, approaches based on analysing Wishart matrices (
[
BDE+14
,
BBN20
]
) seem to be particularly inadequate in the non-symmetric case as the inner-product structure inherently requires symmetry. The methods of Racz and Liu in
[
LR21a
]
on the other hand, exploit very strongly the representation of connections via CDFs which inherently requires monotonicity.
While our approach captures these more general settings, we will see in
Section

6.2
an example demonstrating that it is unfortunately not always optimal.

4.2.1
Coefficient Contractions of Real Polynomials

Suppose that
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
is given. We know that it can be represented as a polynomial

f(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.

We can extend
f
to the polynomial
\tilde{f}:\mathbb{R}^{n}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R},
also given by

\tilde{f}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.

Now, for any vector
\alpha=(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\ldots,\alpha_{d})\in[-1,1]^{d},
we can “contract” the coefficients of
\tilde{f}
by considering the polynomial

\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}):=\tilde{f}(x_{1}\alpha_{1},x_{2}\alpha_{2},\ldots,x_{d}\alpha_{d})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\prod_{i\in S}\alpha_{i}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.

Subsequently, this induces the polynomial

f_{\alpha}:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
given by

{f}_{\alpha}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d})=\sum_{S\subseteq[d]}\widehat{f}(S)\prod_{i\in S}\alpha_{i}\prod_{i\in S}x_{i}.

This is a “coefficient contraction” since
|\widehat{f_{\alpha}}(S)|\leq|\widehat{f}(S)|
clearly holds.
Note, furthermore, that when
\mathbf{g}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d},
clearly
f_{\mathbf{g}}(\mathbf{x})=f(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{x}).
In particular, this means that if
f
takes values in
[0,1],
so does
f_{\mathbf{g}}.
This turns out to be the case more generally.

Observation 4.18
.

For any function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
and vector
\alpha\in[-1,1]^{d},

f_{\alpha}
takes values in
[0,1].

Proof.

Note that the corresponding real polynomials
\tilde{f},\tilde{f}_{\alpha}
are linear in each variable. Furthermore, the sets
A=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\left[-|\alpha_{i}|,|\alpha_{i}|\right],B=[-1,1]^{d}
are both convex and
A\subseteq B.
Thus,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}}f_{\alpha}(\mathbf{x})=\sup_{\mathbf{y}\in B}\tilde{f}_{\alpha}(\mathbf{y})=\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in A}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{z})\leq\sup_{\mathbf{z}\in B}\tilde{f}(\mathbf{z})=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}}f(\mathbf{x})\leq 1.

In the same way, we conclude that
f_{\alpha}\geq 0
holds.
∎

In particular, this means that for any
\alpha\in[-1,1]^{d}
and connection
\sigma
on
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
,
\sigma_{\alpha}
is a well-defined connection. Therefore, all of
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.5
,
4.8
,
4.10
and
4.14
hold if we replace the respective connections in them with arbitrary coefficient contractions of those connections. As we will see in
Section

6.2
, particularly interesting is the case when
\alpha\in\{\pm 1\}^{d}
since this corresponds to simply negating certain variables in
\sigma.
In particular, suppose that
\alpha=(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{\ell},\underbrace{-1,-1,\ldots,-1}_{d-\ell})
for some
1\leq\ell\leq d.
Then,
the connection
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
becomes a function of

\sum_{i\leq\ell}x_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{i>\ell}x_{i}y_{i}.

The corresponding two-form is not PSD, so it is unlikely that approaches based on Gram-Schmidt and analysing Wishart matrices could yield meaningful results in this setting.

4.2.2
Repulsion-Attraction Twists

Recall that one motivation for studying non-monotone, non-symmetric connections was that in certain real world settings - such as friendship formation - similarity in different characteristics might have different effect on the probability of forming an edge.

To model this scenario, consider the simplest case in which
d=2d_{1}
and each latent vector has two components of length
d_{1},
that is
\mathbf{x}=[\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}],
where
\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d_{1}}
and
[\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}]
is their concatenation. Similarity in the
\mathbf{y}
part will “attract”, making edge formation more likely, and similarity in
\mathbf{z}
will “repulse”, making edge
formation less likely. Namely, consider two symmetric connections
a:\{\pm 1\}^{d_{1}}\longrightarrow[0,1]
and
r:\{\pm 1\}^{d_{1}}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with, say, means
1/2.
We call the following function their
repulsion-attraction
twist

\rho_{a,r}([\mathbf{y},\mathbf{z}])=\frac{1+a(\mathbf{y})-r(\mathbf{z})}{2}.

One can check that
\rho_{a,r}
is, again, a connection with mean
1/2
and satisfies
{\mathbf{Var}}[\rho_{a,r}]=\frac{1}{4}({\mathbf{Var}}[a]+{\mathbf{Var}}[r]).
Furthermore,

(\rho_{a,r}-{1}/{2})*(\rho_{a,r}-{1}/{2})=(a-1/2)*(a-1/2)+(r-1/2)*(r-1/2).

Thus, if we denote
\gamma_{\rho}=\left(\rho_{a,r}-\frac{1}{2}\right)*\left(\rho_{a,r}-\frac{1}{2}\right),\gamma_{a}=(a-1/2)*(a-1/2),\gamma_{r}=(r-1/2)*(r-1/2),
we have
\gamma_{\rho}=\gamma_{a}+\gamma_{r}.
Using
\left\lVert\gamma_{a}+\gamma_{r}\right\rVert_{p}\leq\left\lVert\gamma_{a}\right\rVert_{p}+\left\lVert\gamma_{r}\right\rVert_{p}
for each
p,
we can repeat the argument in
Theorem

3.1
losing a simple factor of 2 for
\gamma_{\rho}.
In particular, this means that when
d=\Omega(n\log n),
we have that for any two symmetric connections
a,r,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\rho_{a,r})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)

\displaystyle=O\left({n^{3}\mathcal{W}^{d}({a})^{2}}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{1}({a})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-1}({a})^{2})}{d}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{2}({a})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-2}({a})^{2})}{d^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle+O\left({n^{3}\mathcal{W}^{d}({r})^{2}}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{1}({r})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-1}({r})^{2})}{d}+\frac{n^{3}(\mathcal{W}^{2}({r})^{2}+\mathcal{W}^{d-2}({r})^{2})}{d^{2}}\right)+o(1).

as in
Corollary

4.1
. One can now combine with
Corollaries

4.4
,
4.5
,
4.8
,
4.10
and
4.14
. As we will see in
Section

6.2
, particularly interesting from a theoretical point of view is the case when
a=r.

One could also consider linear combinations of more than two connections. We do not pursue this here.

4.3
Typical Indicator Connections

Finally, we turn to a setting which goes truly beyond symmetry. Namely, we tackle the question of determining when a “typical” random algebraic graph with an indicator connection is indistinguishable from
\mathsf{G}(n,p).
More formally, we consider

\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},\sigma_{A},p_{A})
for which
\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g})=\mathds{1}[\mathbf{g}\in A],
where
A
is sampled from
\mathsf{AnteU}(\{\pm 1\}^{d},p).
Recall that
\mathsf{AnteU}(\{\pm 1\}^{d},p)
is the distribution over subsets of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
in which every element is included independently with probability
p
as all elements over the hypercube have order at most 2 (see
Definition

1.14
).

Theorem 4.19
.

Suppose that
d=\Omega(n\log n).
With probability at least
1-2^{-d}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\{\pm 1\}^{d},p),

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},\sigma_{A},p_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o_{n}(1).

As we will see in
Section

7
, the dependence of
d
on
n
is tight up to the logarithmic factor when
p=\Omega(1).

We use the following well-known bound on the Fourier coefficients of random functions.

Claim 4.20

(
[
ODo14
]
)
.

Suppose that
B
is sampled from
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
by including each element independently with probability
p
(i.e.,
B\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)
). Let
\sigma_{B}
be the indicator of
B.
Then,
with probability at least
1-{2^{-d}},
the inequalities

|\widehat{\sigma_{B}}(\emptyset)-p|\leq 2^{1-d/2}\sqrt{d}
and

|\widehat{\sigma_{B}}(S)|\leq 2^{1-d/2}\sqrt{d}
for all
S\neq\emptyset
hold simultaneously.

Now, we simply combine

4.20
and
Theorem

3.1
.

Proof of
Theorem

4.19
.

We will prove the claim for all sets
A\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{d}
satisfying that

|\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(\emptyset)-p|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
and

|\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(S)|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
holds for all
S\neq\emptyset.
In light of
4.20
, this is enough.
Let
A
be such a set and let
p_{A}={\mathbf{E}}[\sigma_{A}]=\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(\emptyset).

Consider
Theorem

3.1
. It follows that
B_{i}\leq\Delta\binom{d}{i}^{1/2}
for each
i,
where
\Delta=\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
for brevity of notation. Take
m=1.
Then,

\displaystyle C_{m}

\displaystyle=\sum_{2\leq i\leq\frac{d}{2en}}\Delta^{2}\binom{d}{i}+\sum_{d-\frac{d}{2en}\leq i\leq d-2}\Delta^{2}\binom{d}{i}

\displaystyle=O\left(\Delta^{2}\binom{d}{\frac{d}{2en}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{4d}{2^{d}}(2e^{2}n)^{\frac{d}{2en}}\right)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{2^{O(d\log n/n)}}{2^{d}}\right)=O\left(\frac{1}{2^{d/2}}\right).

Similarly,

D=\sum_{\frac{d}{2en}<i<d-\frac{d}{2en}}\Delta^{2}\binom{d}{i}\leq\Delta^{2}2^{d}=4d.

Since
d=\Omega(n\log n),
the conditions of
Theorem

3.1
are satisfied. Thus,

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\sigma_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p_{A})\Big)

(12)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{n^{3}}{p_{A}^{2}}\times\left(\frac{B_{1}^{4}}{d}+\frac{B_{d-1}^{4}}{d}+B_{d}^{4}+\frac{O(\frac{1}{2^{d}})}{d^{2}}+{4d}\exp\left(-\frac{d}{2en}\right)\right)\right)

(13)

\displaystyle=O\left(\frac{n^{3}d^{3}}{p_{A}^{2}2^{2d}}+\frac{n^{3}}{p_{A}^{2}d^{2}2^{d}}+n^{3}d\times\exp\left(-\frac{d}{2en}\right)\right).

(14)

The last expression is of order
o_{n}(1)

when
d=\Omega(n\log n)
for a large enough hidden constant. Finally, note that

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{G}(n,p_{A})\Big)=o(1)

whenever
p=\Omega({1}/{n^{2}}),
and
|p_{A}-p|\leq 2^{1-d/2}\sqrt{d}.
Indeed, this follows from the following observation. Suppose that
p_{A}<p
without loss of generality. Then,
\mathsf{G}(n,p_{A})
can be sampled by first sampling
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and then retaining each edge with probability
\frac{p_{A}}{p}\geq 1-2^{1-d/2}\sqrt{d}p^{-1}=1-o(n^{-3}).
Thus, with high probability
(1-o(n^{-3}))^{\binom{n}{2}}=1-o(n^{-1}),
all edges are retained and the two distributions are a distance
o(1)
apart in
{\mathsf{TV}}.
We use triangle inequality and combine with
Eq.

12
.
∎

Remark 4.21
.

The above proof provides a constructive way of finding connections
\sigma
for which indistinguishability occurs when
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n).
Namely, a sufficient condition is that all of the Fourier coefficients of
\sigma_{A}
except for
\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(\emptyset)
are bounded by
\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
and
|\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(\emptyset)-p|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
In fact, one could check that the same argument holds even under the looser condition that all coefficients are bounded by
\frac{d^{C}}{2^{d}}
for any fixed constant
C.
Such functions with Fourier coefficients bounded by
\epsilon
on levels above
0
are known as
\epsilon
-regular

[
ODo14
]
. Perhaps the simplest examples for
\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
-regular indicators with mean 1/2 are given by the inner product mod 2 function and the complete quadratic function

[
ODo14
, Example 6.4]
. These provide explicit examples of functions for which the phase transition to Erdős-Rényi occurs at
d=\tilde{\Theta}(n).

5
Indistinguishability of Typical Random Algebraic Graphs

We now strengthen
Theorem

4.19
by (1) extending it to all groups of appropriate size and (2)
replacing the
\log n
factor by a potentially smaller
\log\frac{1}{p}
factor. The proof is probabilistic and, thus, does not provide means for explicitly constructing functions
for which the phase transition occurs at
d=\tilde{\Omega}(n).

Theorem 5.1
.

An integer
n
is given and a real number
p\in[0,1].
Let
\mathcal{G}
be any finite group of size at least
\exp(Cn\log\frac{1}{p})
for some universal constant
C.
Then, with probability at least
1-|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/7}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p),
we have

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},\sigma_{A},p_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1),

where
p_{A}={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}[\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g})].
The exact same holds for
\mathsf{LRAG}
instead of
\mathsf{RRAG}.

Proof.

In light of
Eq.

4
, we simply need to show that for typical sets
A,
the function

\gamma_{A}(\mathbf{g})={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{z}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}[(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{z}^{-1})-p)(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z})-p)]

has sufficiently small moments. We bound the moments via the second moment method over
A.
Namely, for
t\geq 1,
define the following functions:

\phi_{t}(A)={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}[\gamma_{A}(\mathbf{g})^{t}]\;\text{ and }\;\Phi(t)={\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}[\phi_{t}^{2}(A)].

We show that for typical
A,

\phi_{t}(A)
is small by bounding the second moment
\Phi(t)
and using Chebyshev’s inequality. We begin by expressing
\phi_{t}(A)
and
\Phi(t).
First,

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(A)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}[\gamma_{A}(\mathbf{g})^{t}]={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\Big[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{z}}[(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{z}^{-1})-p)(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z})-p)]^{t}\Big]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g},\{\mathbf{z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{z}_{i}^{-1})-p)(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}_{i})-p)\Bigg].

Therefore,

\displaystyle\Phi(t)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}[\phi_{t}^{2}(A)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Bigg[\Bigg({\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g},\{\mathbf{z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}\mathbf{z}_{i}^{-1})-p)(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}_{i})-p)\bigg]\Bigg)^{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\bigg]\Bigg].

The main idea now is that we can change the order of expectation between
A
and
\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\}.
Since
{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{h})=p
for each
\mathbf{h}\in\mathcal{G}
by the definition of the density
\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p),
each of the terms of the type
(\sigma_{A}(\cdot)-p)

has zero expectation over
A.
The lower bound we have on the size of
\mathcal{G}
will ensure that for “typical” choices of
\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\},
at least one of the terms of the type
(\sigma_{A}(\cdot)-p)
is independent of the others, which will lead to a (nearly) zero expectation. Namely, rewrite the above expression as

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}&\Bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\bigg]\Bigg].\end{split}

(15)

Now, observe that if there exists some
\xi\in\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\}
such that
\xi\neq\zeta,\xi\neq\zeta^{-1}
for any other
\zeta\in\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\},
the above expression is indeed equal to 0 since this means that
\sigma_{A}(\xi)
is independent of all other terms
\sigma_{A}(\zeta).
Call
B=B\bigg(\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\}\bigg)
the event that all
\xi\in\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\}
satisfy this property. As discussed, over
B
we have

{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\Bigg]=0,

and outside of
B,
we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\Bigg]

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\right\rVert_{\infty}\leq 1.

It follows that
\Phi(t)\leq 1-{\mathbf{P}}[B].

Next, we will show that
{\mathbf{P}}[B]=1-O\left({|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3}}\right).

Denote by
M\subseteq\mathcal{G}
the set of group elements
\mathbf{h}
for which the equation
\mathbf{x}^{2}=\mathbf{h}
has at least
|\mathcal{G}|^{1/3}
solutions
\mathbf{x}
over
\mathcal{G}.
By Markov’s inequality,
|M|\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}.
We now draw the elements
\{\mathbf{g}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)},\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t},\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{t}\}
sequentially in that order and track the probability with which one of the following conditions fails (we terminate if a condition fails):

1.

\mathbf{g}^{(1)}\not\in M\cup\{1\}
and
\mathbf{g}^{(2)}\not\in M\cup\{1\}.

2.

\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}\cup\{(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}.

3.

\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1}\;:\;j\leq i\}\cup\{(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}.

4.

\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}\cup\{\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\},
and

\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j\leq n\}.

5.

\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}\cup\{\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\},
and

\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j\leq n\}.

Note that conditions 2-5 exactly capture that event
B
occurs, so conditions 1-5 hold together with probability at most
{\mathbf{P}}[B].
Condition 1 is added to help with the proof. We analyze each step separately.

1.

{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{g}^{(1)}\in M\cup\{1\}]=\frac{|M|\cup\{1\}}{|\mathcal{G}|}=O(|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3})
and the same for
\mathbf{g}^{(2)}.
Thus, the drawing procedure fails at this stage with probability at most
O(|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3}).

2.

Since the elements
\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}\not\in\{\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j},(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}\cup\{(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1},(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{j})^{-1})^{-1}\;:\;j<i\}
are independent of
\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}
and there are at most
4i\leq 4t\leq 4n
of them, we fail with probability
O({n}/{|\mathcal{G}|})
at this step.

3.

The calculation for
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}
is almost the same, except that we have to separately take care of the cases
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}=(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}
and
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}=\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i}.
The first event can only happen if
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}=1,
in which case the drawing procedure has already terminated at Step 1. The second event is equivalent to
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}=(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{2}.
If the drawing procedure has not terminated at Step 1, this occurs with probability at most
|\mathcal{G}|^{-2/3}
since
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}\not\in M.
Thus, the total probability of failure is

O({n}/{|\mathcal{G}|}+{|\mathcal{G}|^{-2/3}}).

4.

As in Step 2, the calculation for
\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i}
gives
O({n}/{|\mathcal{G}|}).

5.

As in Step 5, the calculation for
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1}
gives
O({n}/{|\mathcal{G}|}+{|\mathcal{G}|^{-2/3}}).

Summing over all
j\in[t],\ell\in\{1,2\},
via union bound, we obtain a total probability of failure at most

O\bigg(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|^{1/3}}\bigg)+O\bigg(\frac{t}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}}\bigg)+O\bigg(\frac{tn}{|\mathcal{G}|}\bigg)=O\bigg(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|^{1/3}}\bigg)

as
t\leq n\leq\log|\mathcal{G}|
by the definition of
t
and assumption on
|\mathcal{G}|.

Going back, we conclude that

1-{\mathbf{P}}[B]\leq{C}{|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3}}
for some absolute constant
C,
so

\Phi(t)\leq{C}{|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3}}
for some absolute constant
C.

Now, we can apply the moment method over
A
and conclude that

{\mathbf{P}}_{A}[|\phi_{t}(A)|>|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/12}]\leq\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{A}[\phi_{t}(A)^{2}]}{|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/6}}=\frac{\Phi(t)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/6}}\leq C|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/6}.

Therefore, with high probability
1-C|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/6}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p),

\phi_{t}(A)\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/12}.
Taking a union bound over
t\in\{1,2,\ldots,n-1\},
we reach the conclusion that with high probability
1-Cn|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/6}>1-|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/7}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p),

\phi_{t}(A)\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/12}
holds for all
t\in[n-1].

Now, suppose that
A
is one such set which satisfies that

\phi_{t}(A)\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/12}
holds for all
t\in[n-1].
Using
Eq.

4
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\sum_{t=0}^{k}\binom{k}{t}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\left[\frac{\gamma(\mathbf{g})^{t}}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\right]\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\Bigg(1+\sum_{t=1}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/12}}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\Bigg).

Now, suppose that
|\mathcal{G}|>n^{24}2^{12n}p^{-12n}(1-p)^{-12n},
which is of order
\exp(Cn\log\frac{1}{p})
for some absolute constant
C
when
p\leq\frac{1}{2}.
Then, the above expression is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{n^{2}2^{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\frac{p^{n}(1-p)^{n}}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{n^{2}2^{n}}\sum_{t=1}^{k}\binom{k}{t}\Bigg)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\Bigg(1+\frac{1}{n^{2}2^{n}}2^{k}\Bigg)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\log\bigg(1+\frac{1}{n^{2}}\bigg)\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\frac{1}{n^{2}}=\frac{1}{n}=o(1).\qed

6
Detection via Signed Triangle Count over the Hypercube

Here, we explore the power of counting signed triangles. We will first provide counterparts to the arguments on symmetric connection in
Section

4.1
. As we will see in
Section

6.2
, there are fundamental difficulties in applying the signed triangle statistic to non-symmetric connections.
Section

7
shows an even deeper limitation that goes beyond counting any small subgraphs.

6.1
Detection for Symmetric Connections

We first simplify
Corollary

2.14
.

Corollary 6.1
.

Suppose that
d=\omega(n),p=\Omega({1}/{n}),
and
\sigma
is a symmetric connection. If

n^{6}\Big(\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\Big)^{2}=\omega\Big(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{4}\sum_{|S|\in\{1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\Big),

then
the signed triangle statistic distinguishes between

\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and

\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)

with high probability.

Proof.

We will essentially show that the above inequality, together with
d=\omega(n),p=\Omega({1}/{n}),
imply the conditions of

Corollary

2.14
.
Denote

A=\displaystyle\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}.

As
\sum_{|S|\in\{1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}>0,
the conditions above imply that

n^{6}A^{2}=\omega(n^{3}p^{3}),
so
A=\omega(n^{-3/2}p^{3/2}).
Note however, that

B:=\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\not\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\leq\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\not\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}|\widehat{\sigma}(S)|^{3}=\sum_{3\leq j\leq d-3}\binom{d}{j}\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{j}({\sigma})}{\binom{d}{j}}\bigg)^{3/2},

where for the last equality we used the symmetry of
\sigma.
However, as
\mathcal{W}^{j}({\sigma})\leq{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]\leq p-p^{2}<p,
the last sum is bounded by

\sum_{3\leq j\leq d-3}\binom{d}{j}^{-1/2}p^{3/2}=O\left(d^{-3/2}p^{3/2}\right)=o(n^{-3/2}p^{3/2})=o(A).

In particular, this means that
A+B=\Omega(A),
and
n^{3}|A+B|=\omega(n^{3/2}p^{3/2})=\omega(1).
Thus,

\displaystyle n^{6}\Big(\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\Big)^{2}=\Omega\Big(n^{6}\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\Big)^{2}

\displaystyle=\omega\Big(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{4}\sum_{|S|\in\{1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\Big)+\omega\Big(n^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\Big)

\displaystyle=\omega\Big(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}+n^{4}\sum_{|S|\in\{1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\Big),

where we used the assumptions of the corollary and the fact that
n^{6}(A+B)^{2}=\omega(|n^{3}(A+B)|)
which follows from

|n^{3}(A+B)|=\omega(1).
To finish the proof, we simply need to add the missing terms in the sum of fourth powers. Using the same approach with which we bounded
B,
we have

n^{4}\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\not\in\{1,2,d-1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}=n^{4}\sum_{2\leq j\leq d-2}\binom{d}{j}p^{2}\binom{d}{j}^{-2}=O(n^{4}p^{2}d^{-2})=o(n^{2}p^{2})=o(n^{3}p^{3}),

where in the last line we used the fact that
pn=\Omega(1).
With this, the conclusion follows.
∎

Note that this proposition gives an exact qualitative counterpart to
Corollary

4.1
since it also depends only on levels
1,2,d-2,d-1,d.

We now transition to proving detection results for specific functions, which serve as lower bounds for our indistinguishability results. We don’t give any lower-bound results for
Corollary

4.4
and
Corollary

4.8
as these already appear in literature (even though for the slightly different model over Gauss space). Namely, in

[
LR21a
]
, Liu and Racz prove that when
there
\sigma
is monotone
\frac{1}{r\sqrt{d}}
-Lipschitz,
the signed triangle statistic distinguishes the underlying geometry whenever
d=o({n^{3}}/{r^{6}}).
In
[
LMS+22a
]
, the authors show that the signed triangle statistic distinguishes the connection
{\mathbf{T}}_{p}
when
d=\tilde{o}(n^{3}p^{3}).

6.1.1
Hard Threshold Connections

Here, we prove the following bound corresponding to
Corollary

4.10
.

Corollary 6.2
.

When
d
is even and
d=\omega(n),p=\Omega(\frac{1}{n})
and
d=o(n^{3/2}p^{3/2}),

{\mathsf{TV}}(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\mathbf{D}_{p}))=1-o(1).

It should be no surprise that we need to calculate the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{D}_{p}
to prove this statement. From
Proposition

4.9
we know that

\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([d])=\tilde{O}(\frac{p}{d^{1/2}}).

In
Section

D.1
, we prove the following further bounds.

Proposition 6.3
.

When
p=d^{O(1)},
the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{D}_{p}
satisfy

1.

\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([2])=\Omega(\frac{p}{d}).

2.

\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([d-2])=\tilde{O}(\frac{p}{d^{3/2}}).

These are enough to prove the corollary.

Proof of

Corollary

6.2

.

We simply apply
Corollary

6.1
. As all Fourier coefficients on levels, 1,
d-1,
equal zero, we focus on levels
2,d-2,d.

\displaystyle\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\in\{1,2,d-2,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{p^{3}}{d^{3}}\binom{d}{2}\right)+\tilde{O}\left(\binom{d}{2}\frac{p^{3}}{d^{9/2}}+\frac{p^{3}}{d^{3/2}}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{p^{3}}{d}\right),

\displaystyle\sum_{S\;:\;|S|\in\{1,d-1,d\}}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{p^{4}}{d^{2}}\right).

Therefore, by
Corollary

6.1
, we can detect geometry when

n^{6}\frac{p^{6}}{d^{2}}=\omega(n^{3}p^{3})+\tilde{\omega}\left(\frac{p^{4}}{d^{2}}\right).

One can easily check that this is equivalent to
d=o(n^{3/2}p^{3/2}).

∎

6.1.2
Non-Monotone Connections, Interval Unions, and Fluctuations

We now turn to the more general case of non-monotone connections and interval unions. We only consider the case when
p=\Theta(1)
so that we illustrate better the dependence on the correlation with parity, manifested in the number of intervals and analytic total variation.
We first handle the simple case of
\pi_{\kappa}.

Corollary 6.4
.

If
n\kappa^{2}=\omega(1),
the signed triangle statistic distinguishes

\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{\kappa})
with high probability.

Proof.

By
Corollary

2.14
, the signed triangle statistic distinguishes the two graph models with high probability whenever

n^{6}\kappa^{6}=\omega(n^{3}+n^{3}\kappa^{3}+n^{4}\kappa^{4}).

Trivially, this is equivalent to
n\kappa^{2}=\omega(1).

∎

Remark 6.5
.

In particular, note that when
\kappa=\Theta(1),
one can distinguish with high probability
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{\kappa})
for
any

d.
This phenomenon is clearly specific to the hypercube. In the Gaussian setting, whenever
d=\tilde{\omega}(n^{3}),

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),p,\sigma)\Big)=o(1)
for any connection
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
depending only on
\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle
simply because of the fact that Wishart and GOE matrices are indistinguishable in this regime due to
Theorem

1.2
.
One way to understand this difference between Gaussian space and the hypercube is that the latter also posses an arithmetic structure, so connections such as
\pi_{\kappa}
which heavily depend on the arithmetic might lead to a very different behaviour. In the particular case of
\pi_{1/2},
the resulting graph
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{1/2})
is bipartite: vertices
i,j
in this graph are adjacent if and only if the number of coordinates equaling
-1
has the same parity in
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{x}_{j}.

Remark 6.6
.

Note that
\pi_{1/2}
has a single large Fourier coefficient on non-zero levels, but this already makes
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{1/2})
detectable for
any
value of
n.
This is the qualitative converse of
Theorem

3.1
.

Remark 6.7
.

Finally, take any
s=\omega(\sqrt{d})
and let
h(\mathbf{x}):=\mathds{1}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\in[-s,s]\right].
Then, the connection
\sigma(\mathbf{x}):=h(\mathbf{x})\times\pi_{1/2}(\mathbf{x})
is an indicator of a union of
O(s)
intervals. Nevertheless,
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
are distinguishable with probability
1-o(1).
Indeed,
{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[\sigma(\mathbf{g})=\pi_{1/2}(\mathbf{g})]=1-o(1),
which implies that
{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(m,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\sigma),\mathsf{RAG}(m,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\pi_{1/2})\Big)=o(1)
for all small enough
m.
Thus, one can distinguish
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
by focusing on the induced subgraphs on vertices
\{1,2,\ldots,m\}
for a small enough
m=\omega(1).

We now proceed to the more interesting interval unions.
Note that in this case, the indistinguishability rates we prove are

d=\omega(n^{3}+n^{3/2}s^{2})
for a general union of intervals and
d=\omega(n^{3/2}s^{2})
for a union of intervals symmetric around 0.
The counterparts to these rates that we present are based on the following interval unions. For simplicity, assume that
d
is even and
d=2d_{1}.
We begin with the general case.

Corollary 6.8
.

Suppose that
s=\tilde{\Omega}(d^{1/3})
and
s=o(\sqrt{d}).
Then, there exists a connection
\zeta
which is the indicator of a union of
s
intervals and

{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta]=\Omega(1).
Furthermore,

\zeta
is detected by the signed triangle statistic whenever
d=o(ns^{2}).

We construct this function
\zeta
as follows.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
s=o(\sqrt{d})

and consider the union of
s
intervals given by

I_{s}=\left\{d_{1},d_{1}+2,d_{1}+4,\ldots,d_{1}+2(s-2)\right\}\cup[d_{1}+\sqrt{d},d].

Let the connection
\zeta_{s}
be defined as

\displaystyle\zeta_{s}(x)=\mathds{1}[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\in I_{s}].
Then, the following facts hold:

{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta_{s}]=\Omega(1),

\displaystyle|\widehat{\zeta_{s}}([\ell])|\leq{\binom{d}{l}}^{-1/2}
for
\ell\in\{1,2,d-1,d-2\},
and
\widehat{\zeta_{s}}([d])=\Theta(\frac{s}{\sqrt{d}}).

We defer the proof to
Section

D.2
. We proceed to providing lower bounds for connections which are indicators of interval unions.

Proof of
Corollary

6.8
.

We use
Corollary

6.1
for the interval union indicator

\zeta_{s}.

The left-hand side becomes

n^{6}\Bigg(\tilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{s^{3}}{d^{3/2}}\bigg)+dO\bigg(\binom{d}{1}^{-3/2}\bigg)+\binom{d}{2}O\bigg(\binom{d}{2}^{-3/2}\bigg)\Bigg)=n^{6}\tilde{\Theta}\Bigg(\frac{s^{3}}{d^{3/2}}\Bigg)

as
s=\tilde{\Omega}(d^{1/3}).
Similarly, in the right-hand side is

\Theta(n^{3})+n^{4}\tilde{\Theta}\bigg(\frac{s^{4}}{d^{2}}\bigg)+n^{4}dO\bigg(\binom{d}{1}^{-2}\bigg)+n^{4}s^{2}O\bigg(\binom{d}{2}^{-2}\bigg)=O\Bigg(n^{3}+n^{4}\frac{s^{4}}{d^{2}}\Bigg).

Thus,
we need the following inequality to hold for detection:

n^{6}\frac{s^{6}}{d^{3}}=\tilde{\omega}\Bigg(n^{3}+n^{4}\frac{s^{4}}{d^{2}}\Bigg).

This is equivalent to
d=\tilde{o}(ns^{2}).

∎

Our construction for the symmetric case is similar.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
s=o(\sqrt{d})

and consider the union of
2s-1
intervals given by

I^{sym}_{s}=[-d,-d_{1}-\sqrt{d}]\cup\left\{d_{1}-2(s-2),\ldots,d_{1},\ldots,d_{1}+2(s-2)\right\}\cup[d_{1}+\sqrt{d},d].

Define the connection

\zeta^{sym}_{s}(x)=\mathds{1}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\in I^{sym}_{s}\Big].
Then, the following facts hold about
\zeta^{sym}_{s}
:

{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta^{sym}_{s}]=\Omega(1),

|\widehat{\zeta^{sym}_{s}}([\ell])|\leq{1}/{\sqrt{\binom{d}{l}}}
for
\ell\in\{1,2,d-1,d-2\},
and
\widehat{\zeta^{sym}_{s}}([d])=\Theta({s}/{\sqrt{d}}).

The same proof yields the following.

Corollary 6.11
.

Suppose that
s=\Omega(d^{1/6})
and

s=o(\sqrt{d}).

Then, there exists a connection
\zeta
which is the indicator of a union of
2s-1
intervals, symmetric around 0, and
{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta]=\Omega(1).
Furthermore
\zeta

is detected by the signed triangle statistic whenever
d=o(ns^{2}).

6.1.3
Connections Without Low Degree and High Degree Terms

Finally, we turn to proving a detection rate that matches the conjecture we stated in

4.16
.

Example 6.12
.

Suppose that
m
is fixed.
There exists a symmetric connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that

\mathcal{W}^{k}({\sigma_{1}})=0
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,\ldots,d\}
and, furthermore, if
d=\tilde{\omega}_{p}(n^{3/m}),
the graphs
G(n,p)
and

\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
are distinguishable with high probability via the signed-triangle statistic.

To prove this result, we will use the following construction. Again, we focus on the case
p=\frac{1}{2}
to illustrate better the dependence on

m.

Proposition 6.13
.

For any constant
m\in\mathbb{N},
there exists a symmetric connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that

1.

{\mathbf{E}}[\sigma_{1}]=\frac{1}{2},

2.

\mathcal{W}^{m}({\sigma_{1}})=\Omega(\frac{1}{\log^{m}d}),

3.

\mathcal{W}^{k}({\sigma_{1}})=0
for
k\in\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,d-m+1,\ldots,d\}.

We construct this connection in
Appendix

C
and now we continue to the proof of the detection statement.

Proof of
Example

6.12
.

We simply apply
Corollary

2.14
. To do so, we need to compute the sum of third and fourth powers of Fourier coefficients. Let the sign on the
m
-th level of
\sigma
be
\epsilon.

\displaystyle\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}=\sum_{S:|S|=m}\widehat{\sigma_{1}}(S)^{3}+\sum_{S:m<|S|<d-m}\widehat{\sigma_{1}}(S)^{3}

\displaystyle=\epsilon\binom{d}{m}\left(\frac{\mathcal{W}^{m}({\sigma_{1}})}{\binom{d}{m}}\right)^{3/2}+O\left(\sum_{m<j<d-m}\binom{d}{j}\left(\frac{p^{2}}{\binom{d}{j}}\right)^{3/2}\right)

\displaystyle=\epsilon\times\Omega(d^{-m/2}\log^{-3m/2}d)+O(d^{-(m+1)/2})=\Omega(d^{-m/2}\log^{-3m/2}d).

In the exact same way, we conclude that

\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}=O(d^{-m/2})\text{ and }\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}=O(d^{-m}).

Thus, to detect the geometry, we simply need the following inequality to be satisfied.

n^{6}d^{-m}\log^{-3m}d=\omega(n^{3}+n^{3}d^{-m/2}+n^{4}d^{-m}).

One can easily check that this is satisfied when

d=o(n^{3/m}\log^{3}d)
or , equivalently,

d=\tilde{o}(n^{3/m}).

∎

6.1.4
Low Degree Polynomials

Our lower bounds for low-degree connections leave a large gap to
Corollary

4.17
.

Theorem 6.14
.

There exists a degree 1 symmetric connection
\sigma
with expectation
p
such that the signed triangle statistic distinguishes
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
with probability
1-o(1)
when
d=o((np)^{3/4}).

Proof.

Consider the connection
\sigma(\mathbf{g})=p+\sum_{i=1}^{d}\frac{p}{d}g_{i}
and apply
Corollary

6.1
.
∎

6.2
The Doom Of Signed Triangles: Fourier-Paired Connections

Thus far, we have used the signed triangle statistic as a useful tool in detecting geometry. However, this technique is not foolproof - it might be the case that the signed triangle statistic does not yield any detection rates. We must note that
[
BBH21
]
explores a similar idea as the authors introduce a bipartite mask and, thus, their graphs do not include any (signed) triangles. Here, we illustrate a different, perhaps more intrinsic way, of making the signed triangle statistic fail. Indeed suppose that

\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}=0.

Then, in expectation, the signed triangle statistic applied to
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
gives the same count: zero.

Of course, one should ask: are there any interesting functions
\sigma
which satisfy
\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}=0
? We show that the answer is yes. We analyze two concrete families of connections, both of which satisfy the following broader definition.

Definition 6.15
.

We call a connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
Fourier-paired if the following condition holds. There exists a bijection
\beta
on the nonempty subsets of
[d]
such that
\widehat{\sigma}(S)=-\widehat{\sigma}(\beta(S))
for each
S\neq\emptyset.

Trivially, any Fourier-paired connection
\sigma
satisfies
\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{k}=0
for any odd integer
k.
The converse is also true – if

\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{k}=0
holds for any odd
k,
the connection is Fourier-paired. So, how can we construct Fourier-paired connections? It turns out that in
Section

4.2
.
we already have!

First, note that for any connection
\sigma
(not even necessarily, symmetric), its repulsion-attraction twist
\rho_{\sigma,\sigma}
is Fourier-paired! Another example can be derived using the approach of negating certain variables. Consider first the majority mapping over
d=2d_{1}
variables defined as follows.
{\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{g})=1
if
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{d}g_{i}>0,

{\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{g})=1/2
if
\displaystyle\sum g_{i}=0,
and
{\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{g})=0
if
\displaystyle\sum g_{i}<0.
Note that
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,{\mathbf{Maj}})
is just the analogue of the usual dense random geometric graph with hard thresholds considered in
[
BDE+14
]
.
4
4

4

Of course, there is the small difference at latent vectors
\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}
for which
\langle\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{j}\rangle=0
since the edge between them is not formed deterministically. But we will ignore this.
One can easily show using
Corollary

6.1
that
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,{\mathbf{Maj}})
are distinguishable when
d=o(n^{3})
and this is tight in light of
Corollary

4.8
.

Now, consider the small modification of
{\mathbf{Maj}},
which we call the half-negated majority and denote by
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}.
Let
\mathbf{h}=(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{d_{1}}\underbrace{-1,-1,\ldots,-1}_{d_{1}})
and
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}(\mathbf{g}):={\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{h}\mathbf{g}),
which is just the coefficient contraction with respect to
\mathbf{h}.
It turns out that it is relatively easy to show that
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
is Fourier-paired.

Proposition 6.16
.

The mapping
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
is Fourier-paired.

Proof.

First, we claim that all coefficients on even levels greater than 1 of
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
are equal to 0. Indeed, consider
f(\mathbf{g}):=2{\mathbf{HNMaj}}(\mathbf{g})-1.
Clearly ,
f
is odd in the sense that
f(\mathbf{g})=-f(-\mathbf{g})
holds for all
\mathbf{g}.
Thus, all coefficients of
f
on even levels equal
0,
so this also holds for
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
except for level
0.

Now, for each
S\subseteq[d]
of odd size, define
\mathsf{s}(S)=S\cap[1,d_{1}]
and
\mathsf{b}(S)=S\cap[d_{1}+1,2d].
We claim that the bijection
\beta
sending
S
to a set
T
such that
\mathsf{s}(S)=\mathsf{b}(T)
and
\mathsf{s}(T)=\mathsf{b}(S)
proves that
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
is Fourier paired. Indeed, this follows from the following fact. For any two
\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2}\in\{\pm 1\}^{d_{1}},
we have
5
5

5

By
\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2},
below we mean their concatenation.

{\mathbf{HNMaj}}(\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2})={\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{g}_{1},-\mathbf{g}_{2})={\mathbf{Maj}}(-\mathbf{g}_{2},\mathbf{g}_{1})={\mathbf{HNMaj}}(-\mathbf{g}_{2},-\mathbf{g}_{1}).
Combining these two statements, we obtain:

\widehat{{\mathbf{HNMaj}}}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g}_{1},\mathbf{g}_{2})=\widehat{{\mathbf{HNMaj}}}(T)\omega_{T}(-\mathbf{g}_{2},-\mathbf{g}_{1})=-\widehat{{\mathbf{HNMaj}}}(T)\omega_{T}(\mathbf{g}_{2},\mathbf{g}_{1}),

from which the claim follows as
|T|=|S|
and
|S|
is odd.
∎

Now, for both
\rho_{{\mathbf{Maj}},{\mathbf{Maj}}}
and
{\mathbf{HNMaj}},
our
Theorem

3.1
implies that the corresponding random algebraic graphs are indistinguishable from
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
when
d=\omega(n^{3}).
However, we cannot apply our usual statistic for counting triangles to detect those graphs as the number of signed triangles matches the corresponding quantity in the Erdős-Rényi model. Clearly, counting signed 4-cycles, one can hope to obtain some lower bound as their number
\sum_{S\neq}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}
(see
2.12
) is positive unless
\sigma
is constant. Indeed, one can easily verify the following facts:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)}[\tau_{4}(G)]

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Var}}_{G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)}[\tau_{4}(G)]

\displaystyle=\Theta(n^{4}),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,{\mathbf{HNMaj}})}[\tau_{4}(H)]

\displaystyle=\Theta\Big(\frac{n^{4}}{d}\Big),

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{K\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\rho_{{\mathbf{Maj}},{\mathbf{Maj}}})}[\tau_{4}(K)]

\displaystyle=\Theta\Big(\frac{n^{4}}{d}\Big).

These suggest that when
d=o(n^{2}),
the two random algebraic graph models have a total variation of
1-o(1)
with
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2).
Of course, to make this conclusion rigorous, we also need to compute the quantities
{\mathbf{Var}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},1/2,\cdot)}[\tau_{4}(G)].
We choose not to take this approach in the current paper as it is computationally cumbersome. Instead, we demonstrate that this in the case for the analogue of
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
with Gaussian latent vectors using
Theorem

1.3
from
[
BBH21
]
.

Suppose that
d=2d_{1},\Omega=\mathbb{R}^{d},

{\mathcal{D}}=\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
and
\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathds{1}\left[\sum_{i\leq d_{1}}x_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{j>d_{1}}x_{j}y_{j}\geq 0\right].
We want to understand when the following inequality holds.

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),1/2,\sigma),\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)\Big)=o(1).

(16)

The edges of
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),1/2,\sigma)
are formed as follows. Each of the latent vectors
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
can be represented as a concatenation of two iid standard Gaussian vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},
that is

\mathbf{x}_{i}=(\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{2}_{i}).
With respect to those, one forms the matrix
W\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n},
where
W_{i,j}=\langle\mathbf{x}^{1}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{1}_{j}\rangle-\langle\mathbf{x}^{2}_{i},\mathbf{x}^{2}_{j}\rangle.
Finally, one forms the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
of
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),1/2,\sigma)
by taking
\mathbf{A}_{i,j}=sign(W_{i,j}).
As in
[
BDE+14
]
, it is enough to show that

W
is close in total variation to a symmetric matrix with iid entries to conclude that
Eq.

16
holds. This is our next result. Recall the definition of
M(n)
from
Section

1.1
.

Theorem 6.17
.

Define the law of the difference of two Wishart matrices as follows.
\mathcal{W}(n,d,-d)
is the law of
XX^{T}-YY^{T},
where
X,Y\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
are iid matrices with independent standard Gaussian entries. If

d={\omega}(n^{2}),
then

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathcal{W}(n,d,-d),\sqrt{d}M(n)\Big)=o_{n}(1).

Proof.

Since
X,Y
are independent Gaussian matrices, so are
X-Y,X+Y.
Thus

V=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{pmatrix}X-Y\\
X+Y\end{pmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{1}})^{\otimes 2n}.

Now, consider the bipartite mask on
VV^{T}
from
Theorem

1.3
. Specifically, focus on the lower-left triangle. From
VV^{T},
we have

\frac{1}{2}(X+Y)(X-Y)^{T}
and from

\sqrt{d_{1}}M(2n)+I_{2n},
we have
\sqrt{d_{1}}Z,
where
Z\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)^{n\times n}
since it is the lower-left corner of a GOE matrix. From
Theorem

1.3
, when
d={\omega}(n^{2}),
we conclude

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}(X+Y)(X-Y),\sqrt{d_{1}}Z\Big)=o(1).

By data-processing inequality (
Theorem

2.3
) for the function
f(V)=(V+V^{T}),
we conclude that

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(f\left(\frac{1}{2}(X+Y)(X-Y)\right),f\left(\sqrt{d_{1}}Z\right)\Big)=o(1).

Now,
f(\frac{1}{2}(X+Y)(X-Y))=XX^{T}-YY^{T}\sim\mathcal{W}(n,d,-d)
and

f(\sqrt{d_{1}}Z)=\sqrt{d_{1}}(Z+Z^{T})\sim\sqrt{d}M(n).
The conclusion follows.
∎

Conversely,
[
BBH21
, Theorem 3.2]
directly shows that counting signed 4-cycles in the adjacency matrix
\mathbf{A}
distinguishes
W
from a GOE matrix and, thus,
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),1/2,\sigma)
from
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2),
so the phase transition indeed occurs at
d=\Theta(n^{2}).

We use this result in
Section

8.1
to formally demonstrate the inefficiency of
{\mathsf{KL}}
