-convexity used to derive
1.5
in
[
LR21a
]
.

6.2.1
Beyond Triangles

Thus far in this section, we showed that the triangle statistic can sometimes be useless. Certain connections – for example, the Fourier-paired ones – result in latent space graphs which cannot be distinguished from
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
using signed triangle counts. One can prove that something more is true. For any odd
k
and
\mathcal{H},
an induced subgraph of the
k
-cycle, the expected number of copies of
\mathcal{H}
in
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
is the same whenever
\sigma
is Fourier-paired.

At a first glance,
the key word here turns out to be
odd
. Note that unless
\sigma
is constant, the expected number of signed
k
-cycles in
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
for
k
even,
\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{k},
is positive and, thus, strictly greater than the expected number for
\mathsf{G}(n,p).
Therefore, for any fixed
\sigma
and
d,
if we take
n
to be large enough, we can distinguish
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
and
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
by counting signed 4-cycles (or signed
k
-cycles for some other
k
even). In particular, this demonstrates an extremal property of Erdős-Rényi graphs
which is a priori not all obvious. For any fixed
p,
the latent space graph
\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma)
with the minimal expected number of signed
k
-cycles for any even
k
is
\mathsf{G}(n,p).

This difference between odd and even cycles in random graphs which are close to Erdős-Rényi is not accidental. It plays a crucial role in the literature on quasi-random graphs
[
KS06
]
.

Nevertheless, we next show that counting (signed) even cycles is also sometimes useless.

7
Detection for Typical Indicator Connections

We move on to establishing lower bounds for
Theorem

4.19
and
Theorem

5.1
.

7.1
Signed Subgraph Counts

Most naturally, we would like to apply signed cycle counts to detect a random algebraic graph. However, a simple calculation over the hypercube shows that this approach will typically fail unless
\log|\mathcal{G}|=O(\log n).

Proposition 7.1
.

A fixed constant
k\in\mathbb{N},k\geq 3
is given.
With probability at least
1-2^{-d}
over
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p),

{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},\sigma_{A},p_{A})}[\tau_{k,p}(H)]=O\Big(\frac{(2\sqrt{d})^{k}}{2^{(k-2)d/2}}\Big)=O\left(2^{-d/3}\right).

Proof.

In light of
4.20
, it is enough to prove the claim for all sets
A\subseteq\{\pm 1\}^{d}
such that
|\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(S)|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}
holds for all
S\neq\emptyset
and

|\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(\emptyset)-p|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}.

Let
A
be such a set. Applying
2.12
, we have

{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},\sigma_{A},p_{A})}[\tau_{k}(H)]=(p_{A}-p)^{k}+\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma_{A}}(S)^{k}=O\bigg(2^{d}\times\Big(\frac{2\sqrt{d}}{2^{d/2}}\Big)^{k}\bigg),

as desired.
∎

This makes the signed
k
-cycle statistic useless for
k=O(1)
when
d=\omega(\log n),p=\omega(\frac{1}{n})
since one can trivially check that
{\mathbf{Var}}_{G\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[\tau_{k}(G)]=\Theta(np^{k}(1-p)^{k}).

7.2
Low Degree Polynomials

So, can we do better by considering some other poly-time computable statistic? We show that the answer to this question is no, insofar as one considers statistics which are low-degree polynomial tests. Our argument applies in the same way over any finite group
\mathcal{G}
of appropriate size. We consider right algebraic graphs, due to their relevance to Cayley graphs, but the same statement with the exact same proof holds for left algebraic graphs.

Theorem 7.2
.

An integer
k=o(n)
and real number
p\in[\frac{1}{n^{3}},1-\frac{1}{n^{3}}]
are given.
Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is an arbitrary group of size at least
\exp(Ck\log n)
for some universal constant
C.
Let
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p).
Consider the connection
\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}\in A]
with
{\mathbf{E}}[\sigma_{A}]=p_{A}.
Then, one typically cannot distinguish
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
using low-degree polynomials in the following sense. With high probability
1-\exp(-\Omega(k\log n))
over
A,

|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}P(G)-{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}P(H)|=O\left(\frac{n^{2k}}{|\mathcal{G}|^{1/3}}\right)=\exp(-\Omega(k\log n))

holds simultaneously for all polynomials
P
of degree at most
k
in variables the edges of the input graph which satisfy
{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[P(H)]\leq 1.

As we will see, the proof of this theorem highly resembles the proof of
Theorem

5.1
.

Proof.

Suppose that
P
is a polynomial of degree at most
k.
For a graph
G,
denote by
(G)_{(i,j)\in[n]\otimes[n]}
the
\{0,1\}
edge indicators of edges of
G,
where
[n]\otimes[n]
is the set of
\binom{n}{2}
pairs of distinct numbers
i,j
in
[n].

We can express
P
as

P=\sum_{\mathcal{H}\subseteq[n]\otimes[n]:|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}a_{\mathcal{H}}\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{H}}\frac{G_{(i,j)}-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}.

Indeed,
\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G):=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{H}}\frac{G_{(i,j)}-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}

are just the
p
-based Walsh polynomials with respect to the
\{\pm 1\}
-valued indicators
(2G-1)_{(i,j)\in[n]\otimes[n]}.
Note that the
Walsh polynomial
\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
corresponds to (signed) subgraph
indicator of
\mathcal{H}

in
G.

Now, by Parseval’s identity,

1={\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[P(H)]=\sum_{0<|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}a^{2}_{\mathcal{H}}.

In particular, this means that
|a_{\mathcal{H}}|\leq 1
holds for each non-empty subgraph
\mathcal{H}
with at most
k
edges. Using triangle inequality,

\begin{split}&|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}P(G)-{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}P(H)|\\
&\leq\sum_{\mathcal{H}\subseteq[n]\otimes[n]:0<|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}|a_{\mathcal{H}}|\times|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)-{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(H)|\\
&=\sum_{\mathcal{H}\subseteq[n]\otimes[n]:0<|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}|a_{\mathcal{H}}|\times|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|\\
&\leq\sum_{\mathcal{H}\subseteq[n]\otimes[n]:0<|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|\\
&\leq n^{2k}\times\sup_{\mathcal{H}\subseteq[n]\otimes[n]:0<|\mathcal{H}|\leq k}|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|,\end{split}

(17)

where in the last line we used the fact that there are at most
n^{2k}
choices for
\mathcal{H}
as
|[n]\otimes[n]|=\binom{n}{2}.
Thus, we simply need to establish a sufficiently strong bound on
|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|
for all small enough
\mathcal{H}.

We do so by taking a square of the desired quantity and using second moment method as follows.

\begin{split}\Psi(A)&:=|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|^{2}\\
&\;={\mathbf{E}}_{G_{1},G_{2}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1})\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G_{2})\right]\\
&\;={\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{K}}(G)\right],\end{split}

(18)

where
\mathcal{K}
is an arbitrary union of two vertex-disjoint copies of
\mathcal{H}.
Indeed,
suppose that
\mathcal{H}
has a vertex set
V(\mathcal{H})\subseteq[n],
where
|V(\mathcal{H})|\leq 2k
(as
|E(\mathcal{H})|\leq k
). Take some set of vertices
V_{1}\subseteq[n]\backslash V(\mathcal{H})
such that

|V_{1}|=|V(\mathcal{H})|
(this is possible since
n>4k=o(n)
) and let
\mathcal{H}_{1}
be an isomorphic copy of
\mathcal{H}
on
V_{1}.
Since the vertex sets of
\mathcal{H}
and
\mathcal{H}_{1}
are disjoint, the variables
\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
and
\psi_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(g)
are independent. Indeed,

\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)
only depends on latent vectors
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in V(\mathcal{H})}
and

\psi_{\mathcal{H}_{1}}(G)
on latent vectors
(\mathbf{x}_{i})_{i\in V(\mathcal{H}_{1})}.
Furthermore, the two variables are trivially iid. Thus,

{\mathbf{E}}_{G_{1},G_{2}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G_{1})\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G_{2})\right]={\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}_{1}}(G)\right],

and
\mathcal{K}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{H}\cup\mathcal{H}_{1}.

Note that
\mathcal{K}
has at most
2k
edges. Without loss of generality, let
V(\mathcal{K})=\{1,2,\ldots,s\},
where
s\leq 4k.

We want to show that, with high probability over
A,

\displaystyle\Psi(A)={\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{K}}(G)\right]

is small. Note that

0\leq\Psi(A)
by construction (see
Eq.

18
), which will allow us to use Markov’s inequality. Now, taking the expectation over
A,
we have

\begin{split}{\mathbf{E}}_{A}[\Psi(A)]&={\mathbf{E}}_{A}{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim_{iid}\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\left[\psi_{\mathcal{K}}(G)\right]\\
&={\mathbf{E}}_{A}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}\\
&={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}.\end{split}

(19)

We proceed in a similar fashion to
Theorem

5.1
. Let
B\subseteq\mathcal{G}^{s}
be the subset of
s
-tuples of vectors for which the following holds. For each
(i,j)\neq(i^{\prime},j^{\prime}),
where
(i,j)\in[s]\otimes[s]
and

(i^{\prime},j^{\prime})\in[s]\otimes[s],

\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}_{j^{\prime}}^{-1}
and

\mathbf{x}_{j}\mathbf{x}_{i}^{-1}\neq\mathbf{x}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{x}_{j^{\prime}}^{-1}.
Clearly, each of these equalities, which
B
has to avoid, happens with probability at most
\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|}
when

\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G}).

As there are at most
2\binom{s}{2}^{2}\leq O(k^{4})
such equalities to be avoided,

{\mathbf{P}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}\left[(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s})\in B\right]\geq 1-O\left(\frac{k^{4}}{|\mathcal{G}|}\right).

Note that if
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s})\in B,
then

{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}=\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Big[\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p\Big]}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}=0,

where we used that
p\leq

This follows directly from the definition of
\mathsf{AnteU}
since the different terms
\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}
are all in different orbits as defined in
Definition

1.14
. On the other hand, whenever
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s})\not\in B,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|{\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}}\bigg\|_{\infty}=\bigg(\max\Big(\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}},\sqrt{\frac{p}{1-p}}\Big)\bigg)^{|E(\mathcal{K})|}\leq\sqrt{n^{3}}^{2k}\leq n^{3k},

where we used the fact that
p\in[\frac{1}{n^{3}},1-\frac{1}{n^{3}}].

Combining these observations,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{A}[\Psi(A)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{s}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}{\mathbf{E}}_{A}\prod_{(i,j)\in E(\mathcal{K})}\frac{\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}_{i}\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\in A]-p}{\sqrt{p(1-p)}}

\displaystyle\leq(1-{\mathbf{P}}[B])n^{3k}=O\left(\frac{k^{4}n^{3k}}{|\mathcal{G}|}\right)<\frac{n^{4k}}{|\mathcal{G}|}

for sufficiently large
n.

Therefore, by positivity of
\Psi,
we conclude from Markov’s inequality that

{\mathbf{P}}[\Psi(A)\geq|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}\times|\mathcal{G}|^{-1}]=\frac{n^{4k}}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}}\leq\frac{\exp(4k\log n)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}}.

Thus, with high probability
1-\frac{\exp(4k\log n)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}}
over
A,
we conclude from

Eq.

18
that

|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3},

where we used
p=\Omega(n^{-2}).

Taking union bound over all
O(n^{2k})
Walsh polynomials of degree at most
k,
we conclude that

|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}\psi_{\mathcal{H}}(G)|\leq|\mathcal{G}|^{-1/3}

holds for all
\mathcal{H}
with at most
k
edges with probability at least
1-O(n^{2k}\frac{\exp(4k\log n)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}})=1-\frac{\exp(6k\log n)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}}.
Combining with
Eq.

17
, we conclude that with probability at least
1-\frac{\exp(6k\log n)}{|\mathcal{G}|^{2/3}},
the inequality

|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}P(G)-{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}P(H)|\leq\frac{n^{2k}}{|\mathcal{G}|^{1/3}}

holds for all polynomials
P
satisfying the assumptions of the theorem.
∎

In particular, this means that if
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(\omega(\log n)),
one cannot distinguish
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
for typical sets
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p)
with constant-degree polynomials and if
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(\omega(\log^{2}n)),
one cannot distinguish the two graph models with polynomials of degree
O(\log n).
Polynomials of degree
O(\log n)
capture a very wide range of algorithms such as spectral methods and others
[
SW22
]
.

It turns out that the dependence on
|\mathcal{G}|
is essentially tight and this is illustrated by a variety of simple algorithms which identify the latent space structure for groups of size at most
\exp(C\log n)=n^{C}
for appropriate constants
C.
In fact, some of these algorithms do not even use the algebraic structure, but apply over arbitrary latent-space graphs with indicator connections! One such algorithm (which is not a direct converse to the low-degree polynomials) is the following.

Theorem 7.3
.

Suppose that
\Omega
is an arbitrary finite latent space of size
o(n^{2}).
Let
\sigma
be any indicator connection with
{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\Omega)}[\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]=p=\omega(\frac{\log n}{n}).

One can distinguish the graphs
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and

\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)
with high probability in polynomial time using
O(n^{2})
polynomials of degree 2. Namely, in

\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma),
with high probability there exist two vertices
u\neq v
which satisfy the following property. For any
w\not\in\{v,u\},

(v,w)
is an edge if and only if
(u,w)
is an edge.

Proof.

Call two vertices
u\neq v

neighbourhood-identical
if for any
w\not\in\{v,u\},

(v,w)
is an edge if and only if
(u,w)
is an edge. A simple union bound calculation shows that in
\mathsf{G}(n,p),
the probability that two neighbourhood-identical vertices exist is of order
O\left(\binom{n}{2}(1-2p+p^{2})^{n}\right)=o(1).
On the other hand, by the Birthday Paradox, whenever
|\mathcal{G}|=o(n^{2}),
with high probability there exist two integers
u,v\in\{1,2,\ldots,n\}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{u}=\mathbf{x}_{v}.
Clearly, whenever
\mathbf{x}_{u}=\mathbf{x}_{v},
the vertices
u
and
v
are neighbourhood-identical.

Finally, we need to translate the above argument to degree 2 polynomials. One simple way to do this is to analyze the supremum over all pairs
(i,j)\in[n]\otimes[n]
of

P_{i,j}(G):=\sum_{k\not\in\{i,j\}}(1-G_{i,k}-G_{j,k})^{2}.

Note that
P_{i,j}
counts the number of vertices
u
such that
(i,u)
is an edge if and only if
(j,u)
is an edge.
∎

For more direct converses of the low-degree framework one can, for example, count signed four-cycles. We have the following proposition, the proof of which we deffer to
Appendix

F
due to its relative simplicity.

Theorem 7.4
.

Suppose that
\Omega
is an arbitrary finite latent space of size
o(n^{1/8}).
Let
\sigma
be any indicator connection with
{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\Omega)}[\sigma(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})]=p>0.

One can distinguish the graphs
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)
with high probability in polynomial time using the signed 4-cycle statistic.

The constant
1/8
can easily be improved to
1/3
(and, perhaps, beyond) if we consider random algebraic graphs. We do not pursue this.

7.3
Statistical Detection

So far, in this section we have provided strong evidence that
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(\log^{O(1)}n)
is the computational limit for detecting
\mathsf{RRAG}(h,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
for a typical
A\sim\mathsf{AnteU}(\mathcal{G},p).
This leaves a large gap from the
|\mathcal{G}|=\Omega(n\log\frac{1}{p})
in
Theorem

5.1
above which we proved that the two graph models are statistically indistinguishable. So, a question remains - can we prove a better statistical detection rate, ideally on the order of
\exp(\tilde{\Omega}(n))
? We obtain the following lower-bound, which matches our indistinguishability rate when
p=\Omega(1).
It holds in the more general setup of probabilistic latent space graphs.

Theorem 7.5
.

There exists an absolute constant
C
with the following property.
Suppose that
\min(p,1-p)=\omega(\frac{1}{n^{2}})
and
\Omega
is a latent space of size at most
\exp(CnH(p)),
where
H(p)=-p\log_{2}p-(1-p)\log_{2}(1-p)
is the binary entropy. Let
{\mathcal{D}}
be an arbitrary distribution over
\Omega
and let
\sigma
be an arbitrary
\{0,1\}
-valued connection. Then,

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{G}(n,p),\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma,p)\Big)=1-o(1).

Proof.

We analyze the case of
p\leq\frac{1}{2},
in which we simply have
|\Omega|\leq\exp(C^{\prime}p\log\frac{1}{p}n).

We apply a simple entropy argument. Consider the
n
latent vectors,
\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}.
The
n
-tuple
(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})
can take at most
|\mathcal{G}|^{n}\leq\exp(C^{\prime}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p})
values. Since edges are deterministic functions of latent vector (because
\sigma
is
\{0,1\}
-valued),

\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathcal{D},\sigma,p)
is a distribution with support of size at most
\exp(C^{\prime}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p}).
Denote this support by
S.

Now, consider
H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p).
First, by standard Chernoff bounds,

{\mathbf{P}}\bigg[|E(H)|\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}[|E(H)|]\bigg]\leq\exp(-\Omega({\mathbf{E}}[|E(H)|]))=\exp(-\Omega(n^{2}p))=o(1).

On the other hand, for any fixed graph
K
on at least
\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}|E(H)|=\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}p
vertices, we have

{\mathbf{P}}[H=K]=p^{|E(K)|}(1-p)^{\binom{n}{2}-|E(K)|}\leq p^{|E(K)|}\leq\exp\bigg(\log p\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}p\bigg)\leq\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{5}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p}\bigg).

Combining these two inequalities, we conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[H\in S^{c}]

\displaystyle=1-{\mathbf{P}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[H\in S]

\displaystyle\geq 1-{\mathbf{P}}\bigg[|E(H)|\leq\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}p\bigg]-{\mathbf{P}}\bigg[|E(H)|>\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}p,H\in S\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq 1-o(1)-|S|\times\sup_{K\;:\;|E(K)|>\frac{1}{2}\binom{n}{2}p}{\mathbf{P}}[H=K]

\displaystyle\geq 1-o(1)-\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{5}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p}\bigg)\times|S|

\displaystyle=1-o(1)-\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{5}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p}\bigg)\times\exp\bigg(C^{\prime}n^{2}p\log\frac{1}{p}\bigg)=1-o(1)

for small enough
C^{\prime}.

The definition of total variation immediately implies the desired conclusion.
∎

8
Discussion and Future Directions

We introduced random algebraic graphs, which turn out to be a very expressive family of random graph models. In particular, it captures the well-studied random geometric graphs on the unit sphere with connections depending on inner products of latent vectors, the stochastic block model, and blow-ups and random subgraphs of Cayley graphs. We studied in depth the model when the latent space is given by the group
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.
The hypercube is of primary interest as it simultaneously captures a geometry of “uniformly arranged” directions like the sphere, but also has an abelian-group structure. Using this duality of the hypercube, we exploited tools from Boolean Fourier analysis to study a wide range of non-monotone and/or non-symmetric connections, many of which seem unapproachable with previous techniques for studying random geometric graphs. Some of these connections revealed new phenomena. Perhaps most interesting among them is a suggested nearly-exponential statistical-computational gap in detecting geometry over the hypercube for almost all indicator connections with a prescribed density. We extended this to arbitrary groups of appropriate size and interpreted the respective result in the language of random Cayley graphs.

As promised in the beginning, we briefly discuss which of our results on the hypercube can be attributed to the algebraic structure of
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
and which to the geometric.

1.

Algebraic.
Two of our results stand out as algebraic. First, our results on indicators of
s
intervals show that for
s
sufficiently large, even though
\sigma
only depends on the inner product of latent vectors, the dimension might need to be an arbitrary large function of
n
for the phase transition to Erdős-Rényi to occur,
Corollaries

4.14
and
6.8
. This, as discussed in
Remark

6.5
, is a phenomenon specific to the hypercube as it captures the arithmetic property of parity. As follows
Theorem

1.2
, random geometric graphs with Gaussian latent vectors become indistinguishable from Erdős-Rényi when
\sigma
only depends on the inner product for
d=\omega(n^{3}),
regardless of the specific form of
\sigma.
Second, our result on typical indicator connections
Theorem

4.19
not only relies on the fact that the hypercube is a group, but it also utilizes the fact that it is a
finite
latent space. It is far from obvious how to define “typical dense indicator connections” when the latent space is, say, the sphere. In
Theorem

5.1
, we extend
Theorem

4.19
to more general groups of
appropriate size, which is further evidence for the dependence of this result on the underlying group structure.

2.

Geometric.
We believe that most of the other results are a feature of the geometry of
(\{\pm 1\}^{d},\mathsf{Unif},\sigma)
and, thus, likely extend to other random geometric graphs such as ones defined over the sphere. First, we extended the result of Liu and Racz on Lipschitz connections
Corollary

4.4
to non-monotone connections, which we believe can be also done over the sphere or Gaussian space (potentially, via arguments close to the ones of
[
LR21a
]
). Next, we showed that if
\sigma
is even — that is, depends on
|\langle\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}\rangle|
— phase transitions occur at
d=\tilde{\Theta}(n^{3/2})
instead of
d=\tilde{\Theta}(n^{3})
(
Corollaries

4.10
,
4.5
and
4.14
). Again, this behaviour seems to be purely geometric and we expect it to extend to Gauss space and the unit sphere.

Since this work is the first to explicitly study random algebraic graphs and to apply tools from Boolean Fourier analysis to random geometric graphs in high dimension, it opens the door to many new exciting lines of research.

8.1
On The Indistinguishability Argument of Liu and Racz

Our indistinguishability results are based on
1.5
due to Liu and Racz. Unfortunately, as the authors themselves note in
[
LR21a
]
, the statement of this claim is suboptimal. We join Liu and Racz in posing the task of improving
1.5
.

The current paper demonstrates that such an improvement can have further interesting consequences beyond closing the gap in
Theorem

1.4
. Of special interest is its relevance to the Boolean analogue of
1.1
demonstrated in
Corollary

4.8
. In light of this connection, one could hope to close
1.1
with an improved
1.5
. Further applications related to the current paper are
4.16
and the gaps in the
setting of union of intervals (
Corollaries

6.8
and
4.14
), low degree polynomials (
Sections

4.1.5
and
6.1.4
) coefficient contractions and repulsion-attraction twists (
Sections

4.2
and
6.2
), and the statistical phase transition for typical random algebraic graphs
Theorems

5.1
and
7.5
.

Here, we formally prove two limitations of
1.5
.

1. Sublinear dimensions for indicator connections.

First, we show that if
\sigma
is an indicator with expectation
p<1/2,
one cannot hope to prove indistinguishability for
d=o(n)
over the hypercube using
1.5
. Suppose that
n>8d
and
\sigma
is
\{0,1\}
-valued, in which case

\sup_{\mathbf{g}}\gamma(\mathbf{g})=\sup_{\mathbf{g}}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{2}\omega_{S}(\mathbf{g})=\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{2}={\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]=p(1-p).

Consider the term
t=2d
for
k=4d
in
Eq.

4
. Using
\|\gamma\|_{t}^{t}\geq\frac{1}{2^{d}}\left\lVert\gamma\right\rVert^{t}_{\infty}=\frac{1}{2^{d}}{\mathbf{Var}}[\sigma]^{t}=\frac{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}{2^{d}},
together with the positivity in
Proposition

3.2
, we conclude

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{4d}\binom{4d}{t}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{t}]}{p^{t}(1-p)^{t}}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{4d}{2d}\frac{{\mathbf{E}}[\gamma^{2d}]}{p^{2d}(1-p)^{2d}}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{4d}{2d}\times\frac{p^{2d}(1-p)^{2d}}{2^{d}}\frac{1}{p^{2d}(1-p)^{2d}}

\displaystyle\geq\binom{4d}{2d}\frac{1}{2^{d}}\geq 2^{d}.

This shows that the bound used in
1.5
gives a KL-divergence at least
d
and, thus, no non-trivial bound on total variation. Note that there is a significant gap to the entropy bound of
d=\tilde{\Omega}(np)
when
p=o(1)

Theorem

7.5
.

2. Inefficiency in KL-Convexity.

Using our observation on differences of Wishart matrices in
Section

4.2
, we give a formal proof that the KL-convexity used by Racz and Liu in deriving
1.5
is indeed at least sometimes suboptimal. Consider
(\Omega,{\mathcal{D}})=(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})),
where without loss of generality
d
is even,
d=2d_{1}.
Consider the following two indicator connections:

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathds{1}[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}y_{i}\geq 0],

\displaystyle\sigma_{+,-}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=\mathds{1}[\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}x_{i}y_{i}-\sum_{i=d_{1}+1}^{2d_{1}}x_{i}y_{i}\geq 0].

Clearly, both have expectation
1/2.
From
[
BDE+14
]
, we know that
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\sigma_{+},1/2)
converges to
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
in
{\mathsf{TV}}
if and only if
d=\omega(n^{3}).
However,
Theorem

6.17
shows that
\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\sigma_{+,-},1/2)
already converges to
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)
in
{\mathsf{TV}}
when
d=\omega(n^{2}).

Despite this difference, once KL-convexity is applied as in
[
LR21a
]
, the bounds on the two models become identical. Namely, the proof in
[
LR21a
]

goes as follows. Let
\mathbf{A}
be the adjacency matrix of

\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\sigma,1/2)
and let
\mathbf{B}
be the adjacency matrix of
\mathsf{G}(n,1/2).
Let
\mathbf{A}_{k},\mathbf{B}_{k}
be the principle minors of order
k,
and let
\mathbf{a}_{k},\mathbf{b}_{k}
be the respective last rows. Finally, let
\mathbf{X}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n})^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
be the matrix of latent vectors and
\mathbf{X}_{k}
its
k\times d
submatrix on the first
k
rows. Clearly,
\mathbf{X}_{k}
determines
\mathbf{A}_{k}.

Then, Racz and Liu write:

\displaystyle{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RGG}(n,\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d}),\sigma,1/2)\|\mathsf{G}(n,1/2)\Big)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{KL}}(\mathbf{A}\|\mathbf{B})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{A}_{k}}{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathbf{a}_{k+1}|\mathbf{A}_{k}\|\mathbf{b}_{k+1}|\mathbf{B}_{k}=\mathbf{A}_{k}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{A}_{k}}{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathbf{a}_{k+1}|\mathbf{A}_{k}\|\mathbf{b}_{k+1}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{X}_{k},\mathbf{A}_{k}}{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathbf{a}_{k+1}|\mathbf{A}_{k},\mathbf{X}_{k}\|\mathbf{b}_{k+1}\Big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{X}_{k}}{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathbf{a}_{k+1}|\mathbf{X}_{k}\|\mathbf{b}_{k+1}\Big).

We claim that at this point, there is noting distinguishing
\sigma_{+}
and
\sigma_{+,-}.
Namely, we claim that
\mathbf{a}^{(+)}_{k+1}|\mathbf{X}_{k}
and
\mathbf{a}^{(+,-)}_{k+1}|\mathbf{X}_{k}
have identical distributions for any fixed
k
(where the subscripts show the dependence on
\sigma
). This captured by the following simple fact. Take any
\mathbf{v}\in\{0,1\}^{k}.
Then,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{a}^{(+)}_{k+1}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{X}_{k}]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}\bigg[\mathds{1}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{d}(\mathbf{x}_{k+1})_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{j})_{i}\bigg]=\mathbf{v}_{j}\;\forall j\in[k]\bigg]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{P}}\bigg[\mathds{1}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{d_{1}}(\mathbf{x}_{k+1})_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{j})_{i}-\sum_{i=d_{1}}^{2d}(-(\mathbf{x}_{k+1})_{i})(\mathbf{x}_{j})_{i}\bigg]=\mathbf{v}_{j}\;\forall j\in[k]\bigg]

\displaystyle{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{a}^{(+,-)}_{k+1}=\mathbf{v}|\mathbf{X}_{k}],

which follows from the fact that

(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}},\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}+1},\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}+2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d})\longrightarrow(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}},-\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}+1},-\mathbf{x}_{d_{1}+2},\ldots,-\mathbf{x}_{d})

is a measure preserving map on
\bigg(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})\bigg).

The fact that the
1.5
cannot distinguish between
\sigma_{+}
and
\sigma_{+,-}
also has a natural interpretation in the hypercube setting. Note that
\sigma_{+}
is just the
{\mathbf{Maj}}
connection and
\sigma_{+,-}
is the
{\mathbf{HNMaj}}
connection (see
Section

6.2
). However, those two functions only differ in the signs of their Fourier coefficients. However, any information about signs is lost when one analyses the respective autocorrelation
\gamma
as Fourier coefficients are squared (see
Section

2.2
).

8.2
Groups Beyond the Hypercube

As already discussed, the random algebraic model is very expressive and, for that reason, it would be interesting to consider further instances of it, especially over groups other than
\{\pm 1\}^{d}.
Nevertheless, these other groups remain challenging and interesting, especially in the case of concrete rather than typical connections.

1. Abelian Groups.

We believe that products of small (of, say, constant size) cyclic groups can be handled in nearly the same way in which we handled the hypercube. In particular, for such groups a very strong hypercontratcivity result analogous to
Theorem

2.7
holds (see
[
ODo14
, Ch. 10]
). Nevertheless, analysing more general groups - even in the finite case - seems challenging. If one chooses to study those via Fourier analysis, this could lead to a fruitful investigation of complex Wishart matrices since functions of finite groups are spanned by their representations over the complex plane. Similarly, one would encounter complex representations when studying connections over finite-dimensional tori. High-dimensional random geometric graphs over tori are studied, for example, in
[
FGK+23
]
.

2. Non-Abelian Groups.

The situation with non-abelian groups seems more challenging even in the finite case due to the fact that 1-dimensional characters are insufficient to span all functions. Several alternative directions seem viable. As a first step, one could study connections depending only on conjugacy classes, which are spanned by 1-dimensional characters. Alternatively, one could try to develop hyperconcentration tools in the non-abelian case as in
[
FKL+20
]
. This seems like a difficult but extremely worthwhile pursuit even independently of random geometric graphs. Finally, in the finite case, one could undertake a purely combinatorial approach based on the relationship between random algebraic graphs and Cayley graphs outlined in
Section

1.3
.

In the infinite case, it could be especially fruitful to study the orthogonal group, which is relevant to spherical random geometric graphs (see
Section

1.3
).

8.3
The Statistical-Computational Gap

In
Section

7
, we gave evidence for a new statistical-computational gap. We propose several further directions to studying it.

Problem 8.1
.

Find explicit group
\mathcal{G}
and set
A\subseteq\mathcal{G}
for which one cannot distinguish
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
using low-degree polynomials when
|\mathcal{G}|=\exp(\log^{O(1)}n).
Does there exist such a pair
\mathcal{G},A
with a short (polynomial in
n
) description?

Note that our arguments only show that typical sets
A
satisfy the desired property, but provide no means of constructing such sets
A.
Similarly, when
\mathcal{G}
is not a hypercube, our proofs for statistical indistinguishability are not constructive, which leads to the following question.

Problem 8.2
.

Given is a number
n
and a group
\mathcal{G}
of size
\exp(\Omega(n)).
Find an explicit set
A\subseteq\mathcal{G}
for which

{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A}),\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1).

Does there exist such a set
A
with a short (polynomial in
n
) description?

Based on our construction over the hypercube in
Remark

4.21
,
we believe that the answer to the existence question should be affirmative for general groups.

Another natural direction is proving hardness results against other computational models than low-degree polynomials. We note that our current result leaves many possible poly-time algorithms unaddressed such as ones based on arithmetic. Low-degree polynomials over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
are not necessarily low-degree polynomials over
\mathbb{R}
(see
[
ODo14
]
). Refuting the statistical-computational gap we proposed for general groups via arithmetic-based polynomial-time algorithms does not seem completely unfeasible due to the underlying arithmetic in random
algebraic
graphs.

8.4
Boolean Fourier Analysis and Probability

We end with a few directions in Boolean Fourier analysis and probability related to the current paper that might be of independent interest.

1. Wishart Matrices.

Theorem

6.17
suggests that differences of Wishart matrices might behave very differently from Wishart matrices. It could be interesting to study more general linear combinations of Wishart matrices. Specifically, it could be interesting to study the following problem asking for the linear combination closest to GOE.

Problem 8.3
.

Given are
n,d_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{k}\in\mathbb{N}.
Let
X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{k}
be
k
independent Gaussian matrices where,
X_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d_{i}})^{\otimes n}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d_{i}}.
Find

\mathbf{a}\in\arg\min_{\mathbf{a}\;:\;\left\lVert\mathbf{a}\right\rVert_{2}=1}{\mathsf{TV}}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{k}a_{i}X_{i}X_{i}^{T},\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i}}M(n)+\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}d_{i}\alpha_{i}\right)I_{n}\Big).

Tools related to the eigenvalues of Wishart matrices similar to the ones developed in
[
KC20
]
for the complex case may be useful.

2. Hypercontractivity.

In
Theorem

3.5
, we developed novel, to the best of our knowledge, bounds on the moments of elementary symmetric polynomials of Rademacher variables in certain regimes. As discussed, these also hold with standard Gaussian and uniform spherical arguments. We noted that these bounds improve the classical hypercontractivity result of
Theorem

2.7
because of the small spectral
\infty
-norm of the respective polynomials. We wonder whether one can generalize this phenomenon beyond (signed) elementary symmetric polynomials.

3. Bohnenblust-Hille and Level-
k
Inequalities.

In
Section

4.1.5
, we used a combination of level-
k
inequalities
Theorem

2.8
and the corollary of the Bohnenblust-Hille inequality given in
Corollary

2.10
. We wonder if there is a stronger result that interpolates between the two. Specifically, in the case of symmetric polynomials, we pose the following question.

Problem 8.4
.

Does there exist a universal constant
c>0
such that for any symmetric polynomial
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
of constant degree with
{\mathbf{E}}[f]=p,
the inequality

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Var}}[f]=O\left(\frac{p^{c}}{d}\right)
holds?

Such an inequality would, in particular, improve the bounds in our
Corollary

4.17
.

4. Interpretable Boolean Functions Without Low-Degree and High-Degree Terms.

In
Proposition

6.13
we constructed a function
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
with weight 0 on levels
1,2,\ldots,m-1,d-m+1,d-m+2,\ldots,d
and variance of order
\tilde{\Omega}(1)
when
m
is a constant. We wonder whether there are interpretable functions satisfying these properties when
m>3.

Appendix A
Variance of the Signed Triangle Statistic

Proof of
Proposition

2.13
.

Denote
\tau_{(i,j,k)}(G)=(G_{i,j}-p)(G_{j,k}-p)(G_{k,i}-p).

For
G\sim\mathsf{RAG}(n,\{\pm 1\}^{d},p,\sigma),
as in
[
LR21a
]
, we have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Var}}[\tau_{3}(G)]

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{3}{\mathbf{Var}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)]^{2}+O\left(\binom{n}{4}\right){\mathbf{Cov}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G),\tau_{(1,2,4)}(G)]+

\displaystyle O\left(\binom{n}{5}\right){\mathbf{Cov}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G),\tau_{(1,4,5)}(G)]+O\left(\binom{n}{6}\right){\mathbf{Cov}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G),\tau_{(4,5,6)}(G)].

We bound each of the four terms separately.

Case 1)

Covariance when the two triangles have overlap of size 3. Using that each edge is an indicator,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)^{2}]={\mathbf{E}}[(G_{1,2}-p)^{2}(G_{2,3}-p)^{2}(G_{3,1}-p)^{2}]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[(1-2p)(G_{1,2}-p)+(p-p^{2})][(1-2p)(G_{2,3}-p)+(p-p^{2})][(1-2p)(G_{3,1}-p)+(p-p^{2})]

\displaystyle=(1-2p)^{3}\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)+(p-p^{2})^{3}.

We used the simple observations that
{\mathbf{E}}[G_{i,j}-p]=0
and

{\mathbf{E}}[(G_{i,j}-p)(G_{j,k}-p)]={\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\bf\mathbf{x}_{i}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{j})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{k})-p)]={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g},\mathbf{h}\sim_{iid}\mathsf{Unif}(\{\pm 1\}^{d})}[(\sigma(\mathbf{g})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{h})-p)]=0.

It follows that

\displaystyle\binom{n}{3}{\mathbf{Var}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)^{2}]=\binom{n}{3}\left((p-p^{2})^{3}+(1-2p)^{3}{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)]-{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)^{2}]\right)

\displaystyle=\binom{n}{3}\left((p-p^{2})^{3}+(1-2p)^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}-\left(\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\right)^{2}\right)=O\left(n^{3}p^{3}+n^{3}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{3}\right).

Case 2)

When the two triangles have an overlap of size
2.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)\tau_{(1,2,4)}(G)]=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}[(G_{1,2}-p)^{2}(G_{1,3}-p)(G_{2,3}-p)(G_{1,4}-p)(G_{2,4}-p)]=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[{\mathbf{E}}\left[(G_{1,2}(1-2p)+p^{2})(G_{1,3}-p)(G_{2,3}-p)(G_{1,4}-p)(G_{2,4}-p)|\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}_{4},\mathbf{x}_{5}\right]\right]=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{2})(1-2p)+p^{2})(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)\right]=

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{2})(1-2p)+p^{2}){\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{3}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)\right]{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{4}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)\right]\right].

Now, observe that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{3}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)\right]{\mathbf{E}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x})-p)\right]^{2}\geq 0.

Therefore, as
(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{2})(1-2p)+p^{2})\in[0,1]
(as
\sigma
takes values in
0,1
), the above expression is bounded by

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}\left[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{3}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)\right]{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{4}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)\right]\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\left[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4},

where the last equality follows from
2.12
. Thus, the total contribution to the variance from this case is
\displaystyle O\left(n^{4}\sum_{S\neq\emptyset}\widehat{\sigma}(S)^{4}\right).

Case 3)

When the two triangles have an overlap of size
1.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)\tau_{(1,4,5)}(G)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{3}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{1})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{4}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{5})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{5}^{-1}\mathbf{x}_{1})-p))]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{g})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{h})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{g^{-1}h^{-1}})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{k})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{\ell})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{k^{-1}\ell^{-1}})-p))]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{g})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{h})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{g^{-1}h^{-1}})-p)]{\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{k})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{\ell})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{k^{-1}\ell^{-1}})-p)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)]{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,4,5)}(G)],

so the covariance equals 0 in that case.

Case 4)

When the two triangles have an overlap of size
0.

{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)\tau_{(4,5,6)}(G)]={\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(1,2,3)}(G)]{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{(4,5,6)}(G)],

so the covariance is also zero in this case. The conclusion follows by adding the four covariances.
∎

Appendix B
Fourier Weights on Level
d

Proof of
Lemma

4.2
.

Suppose that
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
is a symmetric function. Define by
f_{i}
the value of
f
when exactly
i
of its coordinates are equal to
1.
Let
a_{i}=f_{i+1}-f_{i}
and
a_{0}=f_{0},
so

f_{i}=\sum_{j=0}^{i}a_{j}.

Consider
\widehat{f}([d]),
which is the correlation of
f
with the parity function
\omega_{[d]}(\mathbf{x}).
By definition,

\begin{split}&\widehat{f}([d])={\mathbf{E}}[f(\mathbf{x})\omega_{[d]}(\mathbf{x})]\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{i=0}^{d}\binom{d}{i}(-1)^{d-i}f(\underbrace{1,1,\ldots,1}_{i}\underbrace{-1,-1,\ldots,-1}_{d-i})\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{i=0}^{d}\binom{d}{i}(-1)^{d-i}\sum_{j=0}^{i}a_{j}\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{d}a_{j}\sum_{i=j}^{d}\binom{d}{i}(-1)^{d-i}\\
&=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{d}a_{j}(-1)^{d-j}\binom{d-1}{j-1},\end{split}

(20)

where in the last line we used the simple combinatorial fact that

\displaystyle\sum_{i=j}^{d}\binom{d}{i}(-1)^{d-i}=(-1)^{d-j}\binom{d-1}{j-1},
which can be easily proved by induction and Paskal’s identity
\binom{d-1}{j-1}-\binom{d}{j}=-\binom{d-1}{j}.
We modify this expression even further, showing that

\begin{split}|\widehat{f}([d])|&=\left|\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{d}a_{j}(-1)^{d-j}\binom{d-1}{j-1}\right|=\frac{1}{2}\left|{\mathbf{E}}_{j\sim Bin(d-1,\frac{1}{2})}(-1)^{d-j}(f_{j+1}-f_{j})\right|\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{j\sim Bin(d-1,\frac{1}{2})}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|=O\left(\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{d}|f_{j+1}-f_{j}|}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=O\left(\frac{{\mathsf{Fl}}(f)}{\sqrt{d}}\right).\qed\end{split}

(21)

We now continue to bounding
\widehat{{\mathbf{T}}_{p}}([d])
and
\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([d]).

In what follows, we omit all floor and ceiling signs for simplicity of exposition. Clearly, they don’t affect the asymptotics.

Proof of
Proposition

4.7
.

Note that for
f={\mathbf{T}}_{p},
we only have one non-negative value
a,
which corresponds to the case when
\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}=\tau_{p}-1
or, equivalently,
there are exactly
t=\frac{\tau_{p}-1+d}{2}
ones in the vector
x.
Thus,

a_{t}=1
and
a_{s}=0
when
s\neq t.
In other words,

|\widehat{{\mathbf{T}}_{p}}([d])|=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t-1}.

Thus, we simply need to bound the binomial coefficient
\binom{d}{t}.
To do so, we first bound
\tau_{p}.
As each
x_{i}
is supported on
[-1,1],
it is
1
-subgaussian. Therefore,
\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}
is
d
-subgaussian
6
6

6

For these simple applications of subgaussian concentration, see
[
Han
, Ch.3]
.
and, thus,

p={\mathbf{P}}\left[\sum x_{i}\geq\tau_{p}\right]\leq\exp\left(-\frac{\tau_{p}^{2}}{2d}\right),

so
\tau_{p}\leq\sqrt{2d\log\frac{1}{p}}.
By taking a large enough constant in
O\left(\frac{p\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\right),
we can also assume that
\tau_{p}\geq\sqrt{d}/10.
Indeed, this is true because

{\mathbf{P}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\geq\sqrt{d}/10\right]=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{s=d/2}^{d/2+\sqrt{d}/10}\binom{d}{s}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{d}}\times\frac{\sqrt{d}}{10}\binom{d}{d/2}\geq\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2^{d}}\times\frac{2^{d}}{\sqrt{d}}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{10}=\Omega(1).

Now, let
\psi=\frac{d}{\tau_{p}}<10\sqrt{d}.

We claim that

\binom{d}{t+\psi}\geq C\binom{d}{t}

for some absolute constant
C,
independent of
d
and
p.
Indeed, note that

\displaystyle\frac{\binom{d}{t+\psi}}{\binom{d}{t}}=\prod_{i=0}^{\psi-1}\frac{d-t-i}{t+i+1}\geq\left(\frac{d-t-\psi}{t+\psi}\right)^{\psi}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{\frac{d}{2}-\frac{\tau_{p}-1}{2}-\psi}{\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\tau_{p}-1}{2}+\psi}\right)^{\psi}=\left(1-\frac{\tau_{p}-1+2\psi}{\frac{d}{2}+\frac{\tau_{p}-1}{2}+\psi}\right)^{\psi}\geq\left(1-\frac{2\tau_{p}+4\psi}{d}\right)^{\psi}

\displaystyle\geq\exp\left(-\frac{2\tau_{p}\psi+4\psi^{2}}{d}\right)\geq\exp\left(-\frac{2d+100d}{d}\right)=\exp(-102),

as desired.

Now, by definition of
t,
the function
{\mathbf{T}}_{p}
is equal to one if and only if there are at least
\frac{\tau_{p}+d}{2}
ones among
x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d}.
It follows that

p=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=t}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\geq\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{i=0}^{\psi-1}\binom{d}{t+i}\geq C\psi\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}.

Thus,

\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t-1}\leq\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}=O\left(\frac{p}{\psi}\right)=O\left(\frac{p\tau_{p}}{d}\right)=O\left(\frac{p\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\right).\qed

The proof of
Proposition

4.9
is a nearly trivial consequence.

Proof of
Proposition

4.9
.

Note that

\delta_{p}=\tau_{\frac{p}{2}}.
For the function
f=\mathbf{D}_{p},
we easily see that if

t=\frac{\tau_{p/2}-1+d}{2},

we have
a_{0}=1,a_{d-t}=-1,a_{t}=1,
and all the other differences are equal to
0.
Thus, we simply need to bound

\frac{1}{2^{d}}\left(\binom{d}{t}+\binom{d}{d-t}\right)

as above. Using the result of the previous proof, this expression is just

O\left(\frac{\frac{p}{2}\sqrt{\log\frac{2}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=O\left(\frac{p\sqrt{\log\frac{1}{p}}}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

∎

We we end with a remark which will be useful later on and shows that our estimate for
\binom{d}{t}
is optimal up to the small logarithmic factor.

Corollary B.1
.

When
p=d^{O(1)},

\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{\frac{d+\tau_{p}-1}{2}}=\Omega\left(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}}\right).

Proof.

Again, let
t=\frac{d+\tau_{p}-1}{2}.
Note that
t\in[d/2,2d/3]
by the assumption on
p.
We will first show that whenever
z\geq\frac{d}{2},
it is the case that

\binom{d}{z+2\sqrt{d}}\leq C\binom{d}{z}

for
C=e^{-1/2}<1.

Indeed, this is nearly trivial as

\frac{\binom{d}{z+2\sqrt{d}}}{\binom{d}{z}}=\prod_{i=0}^{2\sqrt{d}-1}\frac{d-z-i}{z+i+1}\leq\prod_{i=\sqrt{d}}^{2\sqrt{d}-1}\frac{d-z-i}{d/2}\leq\left(\frac{d-\sqrt{d}}{d/2}\right)^{\sqrt{d}}=\left(1-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{d}}\right)^{\sqrt{d}}\leq e^{-1/2}.

This, however, is sufficient as

p=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=t}^{d}\binom{d}{j}\leq\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}2\sqrt{d}\binom{d}{t+s2\sqrt{d}}\leq\frac{1}{2^{d}}2\sqrt{d}\binom{d}{t}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}C^{s}=O\left(\sqrt{d}\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}\right),

from which the conclusion follows.
∎

Appendix C
Boolean Functions Without Low-Degree and High-Degree Terms

Proposition C.1
.

For every integer
d
and integer
m<d/2,
there exists a symmetric function
f:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[-1,1]
such that:

1.

\mathcal{W}^{1}({f})=\mathcal{W}^{2}({f})=\cdots=\mathcal{W}^{m-1}({f})=0,

2.

\mathcal{W}^{d-m}({f})=\mathcal{W}^{d-m+1}({f})=\cdots=\mathcal{W}^{d}({f})=0,

3.

\mathcal{W}^{m}({f})=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{m^{13/2}\log^{m}d}\right).

We only prove the statement when
d
and
m
are both odd, but explain how to remove these parity assumptions.
Our construction is based on modifying the majority function
{\mathbf{Maj}}={\mathbf{T}}_{1/2},
about which we first recall the following fact.

Lemma C.2

(
[
ODo14
]
)
.

Suppose that
d
is odd and
k<d.

If
k
is even,
\mathcal{W}^{k}({{\mathbf{Maj}}})=0.
If
k
is odd and

k<d/2,

\mathcal{W}^{k}({{\mathbf{Maj}}})=\Theta(\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}).

Now, define the threshold operator
T(\cdot)
over real functions on the hypercube as follows.
T(f)[\mathbf{x}]=\min(f(\mathbf{x}),1)
when
f(\mathbf{x})\geq 0
and

T(f)[x]=\max(f(\mathbf{x}),-1)
when
f(\mathbf{x})<0.
Define also the operator
R_{m}
on real functions over
\{\pm 1\}^{d}
removing layers
\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,d-m+1,\ldots,d\}.
Formally, for any
h,

R_{m}(h)=h-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}h^{=i}-\sum_{j=d-m}^{d}h^{=j}=\sum_{S\;:\;m\leq|S|\leq d-m}\widehat{h}(S)\omega_{S}.

With this definition in mind, we are ready to prove the statement of
Proposition

C.1
in the case when
d
and
m
are both odd. Our goal will be to roughly construct a function satisfying the desired properties by combining the operators
T
and
R_{m}
over
{\mathbf{Maj}}.

Proof of
Proposition

C.1
when
m
and
d
are both odd.

Let
\displaystyle A=\left(e(4m+1)(2m+2)\log^{m/2}d\right)^{-1}
(we will demistify this number in the proof).
We will show that the function
\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ(A\times R_{m}\circ{\mathbf{Maj}})
satisfies the desired properties. We do this in steps and begin with analyzing the mapping

\displaystyle g(\mathbf{x}):

\displaystyle=A\times R_{m}({\mathbf{Maj}})[x]

\displaystyle=A\left({\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{0<i<m}{\mathbf{Maj}}^{=i}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}{\mathbf{Maj}}^{=j}(\mathbf{x})\right)

\displaystyle=A\left({\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{0<i<m}\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([i])p_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([j])p_{j}(\mathbf{x})\right).

Note that
\mathcal{W}^{m}({g})=A^{2}\mathcal{W}^{m}({{\mathbf{Maj}}})=\Omega(\frac{1}{m^{11/2}\log^{m}d})
and

\widehat{g}([m])=\binom{d}{m}^{-1/2}\mathcal{W}^{m}({g})^{1/2}=\Omega\left(\binom{d}{m}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{m^{11/4}\log^{m/2}d}\right).

We will show that with probability at least
\frac{1}{d^{3m}},
it is the case that

g(\mathbf{x})\in[-1,1].
Indeed, note that

|g(\mathbf{x})|\leq A|{\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{x})|+A\sum_{1\leq i<m}|\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([i])|\times|e_{i}(\mathbf{x})|+A\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}|\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([j])|\times|e_{j}(\mathbf{x})|.

The first term is clearly bounded by
A.

Note also that for each
i,

\binom{d}{i}|\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([i])|^{2}=\mathcal{W}^{i}({{\mathbf{Maj}}})\leq 1,
so
|\widehat{{\mathbf{Maj}}}([{i}])|\leq\binom{d}{i}^{-1/2}.
Finally, we bound each
|e_{i}|
in high probability using the moment method and
Theorem

2.7
.
Suppose first that
i<m.

For
t=(4m+1)\log d
and
a=\frac{1}{A(2m+2)}=e(4m+1)\log^{m/2}d,

{\mathbf{P}}\left[|e_{i}(\mathbf{x})|>a\sqrt{\binom{d}{i}}\right]\leq\frac{\left\lVert e_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{t}^{t}}{a^{t}\sqrt{\binom{d}{i}}^{t}}\leq\frac{\sqrt{t-1}^{it}\left\lVert e_{i}(\mathbf{x})\right\rVert_{2}^{t}}{a^{t}\sqrt{\binom{d}{i}}^{t}}.

Now, observe that
\left\lVert e_{i}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}={\mathbf{E}}[\sum_{S,T\;|S|=|T|=i}\omega_{S}\omega_{T}]=\binom{d}{i}.
Thus, the above expression becomes

\left(\frac{(t-1)^{i/2}}{a}\right)^{t}\leq\left(\frac{(t-1)^{m/2}}{a}\right)^{t}\leq\left(\frac{(4m+1)^{m/2}\log^{m/2}d}{a}\right)^{(4m+1)\log d}\leq e^{-(4m+1)\log d}\leq\frac{1}{d^{4m+1}}.

The same conclusion follows for
i\geq d-m
by observing that
|e_{i}(\mathbf{x})|=|\omega_{[d]}e_{i}(\mathbf{x})|=|e_{d-i}(\mathbf{x})|
holds for all
\mathbf{x}.

By union bound, with probability at least
1-\frac{2m}{d^{4m+1}}\geq 1-\frac{1}{d^{4m}},
the event

K=\left\{\mathbf{x}\;:\;|e_{i}(\mathbf{x})|\leq a\sqrt{\binom{d}{i}}\;\forall i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,d-m+1,\ldots,d\}\right\}

occurs. Note that on
K,
however,

\displaystyle|g(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\leq A\left(1+\sum_{1\leq i<m}\binom{d}{i}^{-1/2}a\binom{d}{i}^{1/2}+\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}\binom{d}{j}^{-1/2}a\binom{d}{j}^{1/2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq A(1+(2m+1)a)\leq Aa(2m+2)=1.

Thus, on
K,
the function
g
is bounded by
1.
Outside of
K,
we can just bound
g
by its
L_{\infty}
norm

\displaystyle|g(\mathbf{x})|

\displaystyle\leq A\left(\left\lVert{\mathbf{Maj}}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\sum_{1\leq i<m}\binom{d}{i}^{-1/2}\left\lVert e_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}\binom{d}{j}^{-1/2}\left\lVert e_{j}\right\rVert_{\infty}\right)

\displaystyle\leq A\left(1+\sum_{1\leq i<m}\binom{d}{j}^{-1/2}\binom{d}{i}+\sum_{d-m\leq j\leq d}\binom{d}{j}^{-1/2}\binom{d}{j}\right)\leq d^{m}.

Now, define
h(\mathbf{x}):=g(\mathbf{x})-T\circ g(\mathbf{x}).
Note that

h\equiv 0
on
K.
Therefore, any Fourier coefficient of
h
is bounded as follows.

\displaystyle|\widehat{h}(S)|

\displaystyle=|{\mathbf{E}}[h(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})]|=|{\mathbf{E}}[h(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})1_{K^{c}}(\mathbf{x})]|

\displaystyle\leq{\mathbf{E}}[|h(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})|1_{K^{c}}(\mathbf{x})]\leq\left\lVert h\right\rVert_{\infty}{\mathbf{E}}[1_{K^{c}}]

\displaystyle\leq(\left\lVert g\right\rVert_{\infty}+\left\lVert T\circ g\right\rVert_{\infty}){\mathbf{P}}[K^{c}]

\displaystyle\leq(d^{m}+1)\frac{1}{d^{4m}}<\frac{1}{d^{2m}}.

Therefore, for any set
S,
we have that

|\widehat{[T\circ g-g]}{(S)}|\leq\frac{1}{d^{2m}}.
In particular, this means that

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{m}({T\circ g})\geq\binom{d}{m}\left(\widehat{g}([i])-\frac{1}{d^{2m}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\binom{d}{m}\left(\binom{d}{m}^{-1/2}\frac{1}{m^{11/4}\log^{m/2}d}-\frac{1}{d^{2m}}\right)^{2}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{m^{11/2}\log^{m/2}}d\right).

Furthermore,
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,d-m+1,\ldots,d\},

|\widehat{T\circ g}([i])|=\|\widehat{[T\circ g-g]}([i])\|\leq\frac{1}{d^{2m}}.

Finally, we will show that the symmetric function
\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ g
satisfies the desired properties. First, note that
\mathcal{W}^{m}({\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ g})=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{W}^{m}({T\circ g})=\Omega(\frac{1}{m^{11/2}\log^{m/2}d}).

Since
R_{m}
is the last operator applied, also

\mathcal{W}^{i}({\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ g})=0
for
i\in\{1,2,\ldots,m-1\}\cup\{d-m,d-m+1,\ldots,d\}.
Finally, we need to show that
\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ g
is bounded in
[-1,1].
This, however, is simple.

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{2}R_{m}\circ T\circ g\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert T\circ g-\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}(T\circ g)^{=i}-\sum_{j=d-m}^{d}(T\circ g)^{=j}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert T\circ g\right\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\left\lVert(T\circ g)^{=i}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=d-m}^{d}\left\lVert(T\circ g)^{=j}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}|\widehat{T\circ g}([i])|\times\left\lVert e_{i}\right\rVert_{\infty}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=d-m}^{d}|\widehat{T\circ g}([j])|\times\left\lVert e_{j}\right\rVert_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{d^{2m}}\binom{d}{i}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=d-m}^{d}\frac{1}{d^{2m}}\binom{d}{j}\leq 1,

with which the proof is complete.
∎

Remark C.3

(Removing parity assumptions)
.

Removing the assumption on
d
being odd is easy as we can just consider the majority function over an even number of elements, introduced in

Section

6.2
. Removing the parity assumption on
m
is also not hard. We need to repeat the above argument with some function instead of
{\mathbf{Maj}},
which also has
\Omega(\frac{1}{m^{3/2}\log d})
weight on even levels. Using the same reasoning as above, we can prove that such a function is

\mathbf{H}(\mathbf{x}):=T\left({\mathbf{Maj}}(\mathbf{x})\frac{\sum_{i=1}x_{i}}{\log d\sqrt{d}}\right)=T\left(\left|\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}}{\sqrt{d}\log d}\right|\right)

Now, suppose that
f
satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

C.1
and let

\alpha={\mathbf{E}}[f].
Then,
h=\frac{f-\alpha}{2}
also satisfies the conditions in
Proposition

C.1
and has mean zero. Thus, for any
p\leq 1/2,
the mapping
p(1+h)
has mean
p
and takes values in
[0,1].
With this, we are ready to state our main corollary in this appendix.

Corollary C.4
.

For every integer
d,
integer
m<d/2,
and real number
p\in[0,1]
there exists a symmetric connection
\sigma:\{\pm 1\}^{d}\longrightarrow[0,1]
such that:

1.

\mathcal{W}^{1}({\sigma})=\mathcal{W}^{2}({\sigma})=\cdots=\mathcal{W}^{m-1}({\sigma})=0,

2.

\mathcal{W}^{d-m}({\sigma})=\mathcal{W}^{d-m+1}({\sigma})=\cdots=\mathcal{W}^{d}({\sigma})=0,

3.

\mathcal{W}^{m}({\sigma})=\Omega\left(\frac{p^{2}}{m^{13/2}\log^{m}d}\right).

4.

{\mathbf{E}}[\sigma]=p.

Appendix D
Further Computations of Fourier Coefficients

D.1
The Case of Symmetric Thresholds

Proof of
Proposition

6.3
.

Suppose that
S
has size 2 or
d-2,
in particular
|S|
is even. Then,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}(S)={\mathbf{E}}[\mathbf{D}_{p}(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})]={\mathbf{E}}\left[\mathds{1}[\sum x_{i}\geq\tau_{p/2}]\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})\right]+{\mathbf{E}}\left[\mathds{1}[\sum x_{i}\leq-\tau_{p/2}]\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})\right]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}\left[\mathds{1}[\sum x_{i}\geq\tau_{p/2}]\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})\right]+{\mathbf{E}}\left[\mathds{1}[\sum(-x_{i})\geq\tau_{p/2}]\omega_{S}(-\mathbf{x})\right]

\displaystyle=2{\mathbf{E}}\left[\mathds{1}[\sum x_{i}\geq\tau_{p/2}]\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})\right]=2\widehat{{\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}}(S),

where we used the fact
\mathbf{x}
and
-\mathbf{x}
have the same distribution and
|S|
is even. Thus, we simply need to find the Fourier coefficients of
{\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}
for our purposes. We do this in a similar manner to

[
ODo14
, Chapter 5]
. Namely, suppose that

|S|=2k
and, without loss of generality due to symmetry,
S=\{d\}\cup S_{1},
where
S_{1}
has size
2k-1
it follows that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}[{\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}(\mathbf{x})\omega_{S}(\mathbf{x})]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}\left[\left({\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d-1},1)-{\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d-1},-1)\right)\omega_{S_{1}}\right]=\frac{1}{2}{\mathbf{E}}[J(\mathbf{y})\omega_{S_{1}}(\mathbf{y})]=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{J}(S_{1}),

where
\mathbf{y}=(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{d-1})
and

J(\mathbf{y}):=\left({\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{d-1},1)-{\mathbf{T}}_{p/2}(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{d-1},-1)\right)=\mathds{1}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}y_{i}=\tau_{p/2}-1\right].

We now find the coefficients of
J
via the noise-stability operator. Namely, pick the all-ones vector
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{1}
and let
\mathbf{z}
be
\rho-
correlated with
\mathbf{b}.
On the one hand,

{\mathbf{P}}[J(\mathbf{z})=1]=\binom{d-1}{\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\rho}{2}\right)^{\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}\right)^{\frac{d-1-\tau_{p/2}+1}{2}}

as we need exactly
\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}
of the coordinates of
\mathbf{y}
to equal
1
and each
y_{i}
is iid
Bernoulli(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\rho}{2})
as
\mathbf{y}
and
\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{1}
are
\rho-
correlated. On the other hand, by the definition of the noise operator

{\mathbf{P}}[J(\mathbf{z})=1]={\mathbf{E}}[J(\mathbf{z})]=T_{\rho}J(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{S}\rho^{|S|}\widehat{J}(S)\omega_{S}(\mathbf{b})=\sum_{s=0}^{d-1}\rho^{s}\binom{d}{s}\widehat{J}(S_{1}).

Now, we simply compare coefficients of the two polynomial expressions as

\sum_{s=0}^{d-1}\rho^{s}\binom{d-1}{s}\widehat{J}([s])=\binom{d-1}{\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\rho}{2}\right)^{\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\rho}{2}\right)^{\frac{d-1-\tau_{p/2}+1}{2}}

holds for all
\rho\in[-1,1].
For notational purposes, we set
t=\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}.

Case 1)

When
s=1.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\binom{d-1}{1}\widehat{J}([1])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}\left(t-(d-t-1)\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}\left(2t-d+1\right)

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}\right)=\Omega(p),

using the bounds
t=\frac{d}{2}+\Omega(\sqrt{d})
and
\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}=\Omega(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}})
in
Appendix

B
on
t
and
\binom{d}{t}.
Thus,

\widehat{J}([1])=\Omega(\frac{p}{d})
and
so
\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([2])=\Omega(\frac{p}{d}).
Using the bounds
t=\frac{d}{2}+\tilde{O}(\sqrt{d})
and

\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d}{t}=\tilde{O}(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}}),
we similarly conclude that

\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([2])=\tilde{O}(\frac{p}{d}).

Case 2)

When
s=d-3,
we similarly have

\displaystyle\binom{d-1}{d-3}\widehat{J}([d-2])

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}\left(\binom{t}{t-2}(-1)^{d-t}+\binom{t}{t-1}\binom{d-1-t}{d-2-t}(-1)^{d-t-1}+\binom{d-1-t}{d-3-t}(-1)^{d-t-2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}O\left(\binom{t}{t-2}-\binom{t}{t-1}\binom{d-1-t}{d-2-t}+\binom{d-1-t}{d-3-t}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}O\left(\frac{t(t-1)+(d-t-1)(d-t-2)-2t(d-1-t)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}O\left(\frac{t(t-1)+(d-t-1)(d-t-2)-2t(d-1-t)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}O\left(\frac{(\tau_{p/2}-1)^{2}-(d-1)}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\tilde{O}\left(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}}d\right)=\tilde{O}\left(p\sqrt{d}\right),

where we used the fact that
t=\frac{d-1+\tau_{p/2}-1}{2}
to expand the expression and the bounds
\tau_{p/2}=\tilde{O}\left(\sqrt{d}\right)
and
\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{t}=\tilde{O}(\frac{p}{\sqrt{d}}).
Hence, we know that

\widehat{\mathbf{D}_{p}}([d-2])=\frac{1}{2}\widehat{J}([d-2])=\tilde{O}\left(pd^{-3/2}\right),

as desired.
∎

D.2
Interval Unions

Proof of
Proposition

6.9
.

We have to prove three things about
\zeta_{s}.
First, note that

{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta_{s}]\geq{\mathbf{P}}\left[\sum x_{i}\geq d_{1}+\sqrt{d}\right]=\Omega(1).

Second, for each
\ell,
we have that

1\geq{\mathbf{E}}[\zeta_{s}^{2}]\geq\mathcal{W}^{\ell}({\zeta_{s}})=\binom{d}{\ell}\widehat{\zeta_{s}}([\ell])^{2},

from which the bound on coefficients on levels
1,2,d-1,d-2
follow.

Lemma

4.2
implies that

\displaystyle\left|\widehat{\zeta_{s}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{2^{d}}\sum_{j=0}^{s-2}(-1)^{d-d_{1}-2j}\binom{d-1}{d_{1}+2j-1}+(-1)^{d-d_{1}-\sqrt{d}}\binom{d-1}{d_{1}+\sqrt{d}-1}\right|

\displaystyle=\Omega\left(\frac{s-1}{\sqrt{d}}+(-1)^{d_{1}-\sqrt{d}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{s}{\sqrt{d}}\right),

where we used the fact that
\frac{1}{2^{d}}\binom{d-1}{d_{1}+\psi}=\Theta(\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}})
whenever
\psi=O(\sqrt{d}).

∎

Appendix E
Distributions of Cayley Graph Generators

Here, we show that both our statistical and computational indistinguishability results on typical Cayley graphs
Theorems

5.1
and
7.2
are robust with respect to the underlying distribution of generators in the following sense. They still hold (with potentially different constants) as long as the distribution satisfies the following “small-scale conditional independence” property even if it is not
\mathsf{AnteU}.

Definition E.1
.

(Small-scale Conditional Independence)

Given are a group
|\mathcal{G}|,
a probability
p\in(0,1),
and an integer
N=o(|\mathcal{G}|).
We say that a distribution
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)
satisfies the “
(c_{1},c_{2})
-small-scale conditional independence property for
(\mathcal{G},N,p),
” where
c_{1},c_{2}\in(0,1)
are constants if the following holds. There exists a set
\mathcal{L}\subseteq\mathcal{G}
of size at most
|\mathcal{G}|^{c_{1}}
such that all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{G}\backslash\mathcal{L}
satisfy the condition: For any set
M\subseteq\mathcal{G}\backslash\{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{x}^{-1}\}
of cardinality
0\leq|M|\leq N
and any
I\subseteq M,

\bigg|{\mathbf{P}}_{A\sim\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)}[\mathbf{x}\in A|A\cap M=I]-p\bigg|\leq\frac{1}{|\mathcal{G}|^{c_{2}}}.

Importantly, one can easily see that this definition captures
\mathsf{PostU}
if the underlying group has at most
|\mathcal{G}|^{c}
elements of order two for some
c<1
or, respectively, at most most
|\mathcal{G}|^{c}
elements of order at least three for some
c<1.
Our theorems are rephrased as follows.

Claim E.2
.

(See
Theorem

5.1
)

An integer
n
is given and a real number
p\in[0,1].
Let
\mathcal{G}
be any finite group of size at least
\exp(Cn\log\frac{1}{p})
for some universal constant
C.
Let
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)
be a distribution over subsets of
\mathcal{G}
satisfying the “
(c_{1},c_{2})
-small-scale conditional independence property for
(\mathcal{G},n^{3},p),
” where
c_{1},c_{2}
are constant independent of
|\mathcal{G}|,n,p.

Then, with high probability at least
1-|\mathcal{G}|^{-D}
over
A\sim\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p),
we have

{\mathsf{KL}}\Big(\mathsf{RAG}(n,\mathcal{G},\sigma_{A},p_{A})\|\mathsf{G}(n,p)\Big)=o(1),

where
p_{A}={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{g}\sim\mathsf{Unif}(\mathcal{G})}[\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g})]
and
D>0
depends only on
c_{1},c_{2}.

Claim E.3
.

(See
Theorem

7.2
)

An integer
k=o(n)
and real number
p\in[\frac{1}{n^{3}},1-\frac{1}{n^{3}}]
are given.
Suppose that
\mathcal{G}
is an arbitrary group of size at least
\exp(Ck\log n)
for some universal constant
C.

Let
\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)
be a distribution over subsets of
\mathcal{G}
satisfying the ‘
(c_{1},c_{2})
-small-scale conditional independence property for
(\mathcal{G},n^{3},p),
” where
c_{1},c_{2}
are constant independent of
|\mathcal{G}|,n,p.

Let
A\sim\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p).
Consider the connection
\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathds{1}[\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}^{-1}\in A]
with
{\mathbf{E}}[\sigma_{A}]=p_{A}.
Then, one typically cannot distinguish
\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})
using low-degree polynomials in the following sense. With high probability
1-\exp(-Dk\log n)
over
A,

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{E}}_{G\sim\mathsf{RRAG}(n,\mathcal{G},p_{A},\sigma_{A})}P(G)-{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}P(H)|=\exp(-Dk\log n)

holds simultaneously for all polynomials
P
of degree at most
k
in variables the edges of the input graph which satisfy
{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[P(H)]\leq 1.

Here,
D>0
is a constant depending only on
c_{1},c_{2}.

For both statements, the proofs are simple modifications of
Theorem

5.1
, respectively
Theorem

7.2
. We only outline how one needs to change the first proof as the modifications are identical.

Proof sketch of
E.2
.

proceed in the same way as in
Theorem

5.1
, except that we remove from
B
the event that one of
\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{(1)})^{-1},\mathbf{z}_{i}^{(1)},\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}_{i}^{(2)})^{-1},\mathbf{z}_{i}^{(2)}
belongs to the set
\mathcal{L}
from
Definition

E.1
. By union bound, the resulting event
B_{1}
satisfies

{\mathbf{P}}[B_{1}]\geq{\mathbf{P}}[B]-4t\frac{|\mathcal{L}|}{|\mathcal{G}|}=1-O(|\mathcal{G}|^{-D_{1}})

for some fixed constant
D_{1}.
Thus, again, over
B_{1}^{c},
the expectation of
Eq.

15
is at most
O(|\mathcal{G}|^{-D_{1}}).
On the other hand, over
B_{1}^{c},
we have that

\displaystyle\Bigg|{\mathbf{E}}_{A\sim\mathsf{D}(\mathcal{G},p)}\Bigg[\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\Bigg]\Bigg|

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq{|\mathcal{G}|^{-c_{2}}},

where the last inequality follows directly from
Definition

E.1
applied to
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{1})^{-1}
and the fact that

\Bigg\|\prod_{i=2}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(1)}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(1)}_{i})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{g}^{(2)}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})^{-1})-p)\prod_{i=1}^{t}(\sigma_{A}(\mathbf{z}^{(2)}_{i})-p)\Bigg\|<1

holds
with probability
1
over
A.
Using these bounds in
Eq.

15
, we finish the proof analogously.
∎

Appendix F
Signed Four-Cycles on Probabilistic Latent Space Graphs

Proof of
Theorem

7.4
.

We need to bound
{\mathbf{E}}[\tau_{4}(H)],{\mathbf{Var}}[{\tau_{4}(H)}]
for
H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)
and
H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma).
We begin with
{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{4}(H)].
Since
\sigma
is an indicator with positive expectation
p,
there exists some
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\Omega
such that
\sigma(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{2})=1.

In particular, this means that
p\geq{|\Omega|^{-2}}.
Consider the signed cycle
(1,2,3,4).

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[(H_{1,2}-p)(H_{2,3}-p)(H_{3,4}-p)(H_{4,1}-p)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}_{3})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{3},\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{4},\mathbf{x}_{1})-p)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}}\bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{3},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)|\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}]{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{4}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{3},\mathbf{x}_{4})-p)|\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}]\bigg]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}}\bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{3},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)|\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}]^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)(\sigma(\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)]\bigg]^{2}\times{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{x}_{1}=\mathbf{x}_{3}=\mathbf{y}_{1}]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{2}}\bigg[{\mathbf{E}}_{\mathbf{x}_{2}}[(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{2})-p)^{2}]^{2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{2}}[(1-p)^{2}{\mathbf{P}}[\mathbf{x}_{2}=\mathbf{y}_{2}]]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|^{4}}(1-p)^{4}.

In particular, this means that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{4}(H)]=\Omega(n^{4})|\mathcal{G}|^{-3}(1-p)^{4}=\Omega(n^{4}|\Omega|^{-4}).

Now, we will prove that

{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{4}(H)]=O(n^{7}).

For brevity, denote

\tau_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}(H)=(H_{i_{1},i_{2}}-p)(H_{i_{2},i_{3}}-p)(H_{i_{3},i_{4}}-p)(H_{i_{4},i_{1}}-p).

Under thies notation, clearly

\tau_{4}(H)=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}}\tau_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}(H).

Now,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{4}(H)]

\displaystyle={\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}\bigg[\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}\tau_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}(H)\bigg]

\displaystyle=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}(H)]

\displaystyle+\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\neq(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})}{\mathbf{Cov}}_{H\sim\mathsf{PLSG}(n,\Omega,\mathsf{Unif},p,\sigma)}[\tau_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})}(H),\tau_{(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})}(H)].

The key observation is that if
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
and
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},j_{4})
are disjoint, the covariance is zero since the two functions depend on disjoint set of latent vectors. Otherwise, the covariance is at most 1 since
\tau
only takes values in
[0,1].
However, there are
O(n^{7})
ways to take two four-tuples which are not disjoint among the elements of
[n],
so the claim follows.
Finally, note that one similarly has

{\mathbf{E}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[\tau_{4}(H)]=0,{\mathbf{Var}}_{H\sim\mathsf{G}(n,p)}[\tau_{4}(H)]=O(n^{7}).

Thus, a sufficient condition for the signed 4-cycle statistic to differentiate between the two graph distributions with high probability is that

n^{4}|\Omega|^{-4}=\omega(\sqrt{n^{7}}),

which is guaranteed by
|\Omega|=o(n^{1/8}).

∎
